# Artificial Intelligence for (Environmentally-Extended) Input–Output Economics

## An opportunity map for the IO–AI nexus: use cases, evidence, limits and enabling conditions

*Research report. Prepared for an audience of input–output, industrial-ecology and AI researchers.*

*by Maximilian KOSLOWSKI @NTNU/Norway. A result of prompting with multiple LLMs.*

*Version note (second edition). This edition additionally synthesises eleven independently produced reports on the identical task, written by other systems, against the first edition. Claims those reports contained and this one did not were re-verified at source before adoption; §21 records what was adopted, what was rejected and why, and what appears here and nowhere else. The substantive changes are concentrated in §§1, 7, 8, 10 and 12, in the opportunity matrix, and in the two new closing sections §21 and §22. Method note: this report synthesises five independently conducted literature reviews (data pipeline; static/structural analysis; forward-looking analysis; coupling and integration; validation, computation and enabling conditions) together with the principal investigator's own search, reconciled by cross-comparison and majority adjudication of contested claims. Approximately 190 distinct use cases were catalogued and consolidated into the 14 sections below. Evidence status and the feasibility/impact ratings reported here are the reconciled positions; where the reviewers disagreed, the disagreement is stated rather than averaged away.*

---


# 0. Executive synthesis

Input–output analysis is an accounting identity plus a linear operator. It is not a behavioural model, and artificial intelligence does not make it one. Almost everything "forward-looking", "causal" or "predictive" in IO is imported from outside the accounts — a demand projection, a coefficient trend, an exogenous scenario, a characterisation factor. What AI changes is (i) how fast, how cheaply and how completely the *inputs* to the operator can be estimated; (ii) how large the *ensemble* of runs around the operator can be; (iii) how many people can *operate* it correctly; and (iv) how much of the pipeline can be *checked* automatically. Those are four real and large gains. None of them resolves the identification problem, the Lucas critique, or the absence of counterfactual data — and several of the most attractive use cases below are dangerous precisely because they make it look as though they do.

The single deepest technical connection between the two fields has been almost entirely unexploited. The Leontief inverse expands as `L = Σ_k A^k`, which is exactly weighted *k*-hop message passing on the directed, weighted, sparse graph `A`. **The Leontief model is, formally, a linear graph neural network with identity weights and no nonlinearity.** Every serious "AI replaces part of the model" opportunity is a principled relaxation of one of three restrictions — linear → nonlinear, identity weights → learned weights, fixed coefficients → attribute-conditioned coefficients — subject to the accounting identities that give IO its authority. Despite the exactness of that correspondence, we located essentially one direct GNN⊕IO application in the published literature [buildenv26], alongside a large and fast-moving firm-level supply-chain GNN literature [chang25; gnnbench; hypergnn] that does not cite input–output economics at all. The two communities are not talking.

The literature is thin, lopsided, and younger than it looks. Two independent systematic reviews of AI in industrial ecology — one bibliometric over 1,068 publications [gong25], one LLM-assisted over 209 AI-in-LCA papers [mensikova26] — both fail to identify input–output analysis as a significant AI-adopting subfield; the first reports that 69 of 71 trending terms emerged within the last eight years. Meanwhile the AI×LCA literature is booming. The seam between them — hybridisation, concordance, double counting, the monetary–physical boundary — is where the methodological hazards concentrate and where almost nothing has been published. We could not find a dedicated review of AI for input–output economics; on the evidence assembled here, none exists.

The most methodologically advanced "AI in IO" work we found is not machine learning at all. It is **differentiable programming**: re-implementing an ARIO-class production-network model in JAX so that gradients with respect to thousands of firm-level parameters are available, replacing approximate Bayesian computation with variational inference and reporting lower loss after 300 model evaluations than ABC achieved after 30,000 — a speed-up of over three orders of magnitude, with uncertainty quantification retained [hamid25]. Nothing about the economics changes; only the computational representation does. On a benefit-per-unit-of-epistemic-risk basis this is the strongest item in the entire map, and it generalises directly to IO proper, where reverse-mode automatic differentiation would give the exact sensitivity of any footprint to all ~10⁸ coefficients at roughly the cost of one forward solve. We found no published application of automatic differentiation to an MRIO pipeline.

Three quantitative anchors should discipline every claim made in this area.

*First, the noise floor.* Propagating authoritative inventory uncertainties through EXIOBASE with Dirichlet sampling to preserve disaggregation-induced correlation gives median **country-level** coefficients of variation of 4% (CO₂), 12% (CH₄) and 33% (N₂O) — but median **sector-level** CVs of 94%, 100% and 113% [schulte24]. An ML method that improves an imputation by 10% is not visible against a 94% CV. The same study finds, encouragingly and counter-intuitively, that uncertainty *decreases* when propagated from emission accounts to consumption-based footprints, through supply-chain cancelling-out — a result that badly needs replication on other satellite accounts and databases before it is relied upon.

*Second, the deployment gap.* The most widely deployed AI×IO application in the world is the classification of corporate spend ledger lines onto EEIO sectors for Scope 3 accounting. On the only public benchmark, covering 295 classes (294 BEA codes plus a non-emissive class) over 10,000 synthetic line items, the best method — a few-shot LLM with dynamic retrieval — reaches **57.3% top-1 and 72.2% top-3 accuracy**, against 48.5% for logistic regression and 40.6% for text embeddings; the authors report that their private benchmark is *harder* than the public one [atlas; watershed]. Roughly one line item in four has the correct sector nowhere in its top three. On a different task and label space (1,114 NAICS categories, real company descriptions rather than terse ledger lines), a retrieval-plus-hierarchical-LLM design reaches 83.68% top-1 but still carries 45.88% MAPE on company-level emission estimates [green25]. Zero-shot semantic matching of 40,000 household products to NAICS→BEA→USEEIO reaches 48% accuracy and 22% MAPE, comparable to non-expert human annotators [caml23]. Vendor claims of 85–87% F1 [ibm-scope3] and "3× more accurate" mappings [climatiq-guide] are on private data with unspecified label spaces and are not comparable to any of these. This is the number the field should quote when asked whether AI has solved EEIO-based carbon accounting.

*Third, the reproducibility base rate.* In the nearest neighbouring field, a randomised audit of LCA studies finds 60% without a complete transparent inventory account and **only 2% sharing any analysis code**; reproduction error is under 1% with complete documentation but **−20.97% when only the database version differs** [kamali26]. Undocumented version drift alone exceeds most published effect sizes. Any AI programme built on this substrate inherits the substrate.

*Fourth, and counterweight to the first three: the ceiling is higher than the deployment gap suggests.* Two results, both published in peer-reviewed venues and both absent from the first edition of this report, show what a well-posed AI component actually delivers on IO objects. Residual deep learning stacked on RAS raises one-year R² on US tables from 0.6412 to 0.8726 and cuts median absolute percentage error from 37.49% to 11.35%, with the gain carried through to a carbon-footprint application [zhao22-dl]; and a GAN with an embedded IPF constraint layer plus random-forest boosting reaches R² 0.9417 against 0.8524 for Improved-RAS on Italian regional tables, lifting the correlation of diagonal entries from 0.3165 to 0.8228 [depretis26]. **The pattern in both is the same and it is the design rule this report recommends: the classical, accounting-consistent estimator remains the backbone, and the learned component is a bounded, structurally constrained correction to it.** The 57.3% spend-classification figure and these two results are not in tension — they measure different things. Where the task is *estimating a number inside a known accounting structure*, machine learning is already good. Where the task is *deciding what a line of free text means*, it is not.

The opportunity therefore stratifies into three tiers by epistemic risk, and we recommend that the field adopt this stratification explicitly.

**Tier 1 — AI upstream of the algebra.** ML fills, classifies, maps, imputes or nowcasts the *inputs* to an unchanged IO computation: gap-filling satellite accounts, classifying text to sectors, reconstructing trade blocks, estimating regional coefficients, building concordances, nowcasting final demand. Accounting consistency is preserved by construction; validation is often possible; regulatory and practical demand is immediate. This is where nearly all published work sits, and where it should sit for now.

**Tier 2 — AI as computational infrastructure.** The model is unchanged; only its computation changes. Differentiable IO pipelines, hard-constraint projection layers, randomised numerical linear algebra, multilevel Monte Carlo, GPU sparse solvers, learned search heuristics for structural path analysis. High leverage, low epistemic risk, and — on our assessment — the most under-exploited tier by a wide margin.

**Tier 3 — AI replacing part of the model.** GNNs that generalise the Leontief propagation, learned production functions, reinforcement learning over IO-based environments, emulators standing in for IAM or CGE structure. Justified only where the linear model demonstrably fails (nonlinear cascades, within-sector heterogeneity, adaptive rationing), and only with hard accounting constraints attached. The prerequisite technology exists — KKT-style projection layers guarantee `Cŷ = d` exactly at negligible cost [kkthpinn; kkthpinn-arxiv] — and, since the first edition of this report, it has been applied to input–output identities: a generative model with an **iterative-proportional-fitting layer inside the generator** produces regional and world tables that satisfy their margins by construction and beats Improved-RAS on both [depretis26]. That transfer is what converts "ML breaks the accounts" from a fatal objection into a solved problem, and it is now demonstrated rather than proposed. What has *not* been done is the same construction at multi-regional scale, with heterogeneous cell reliabilities, and with the constraint set that a full MRIO actually carries.

The binding constraints on all three tiers are not algorithmic. They are: **data poverty masquerading as big data** (an MRIO is one graph observed 20–30 times); **licensing asymmetry** (the field's only open ML benchmark could be built only on the openly licensed database, and therefore cannot test cross-database generalisation [exioml-arxiv; exioml-sdata]); **confidentiality** (firm-level transaction data would change what MRIO *is*, and is legally locked); **the total absence of ground truth and benchmarks** for the tasks that matter; and **validation circularity** — the hazard that the same model family imputes cells, flags anomalies, writes the checks and drafts the narrative, so that errors become self-confirming. On the reconciled view of all five reviews, validation circularity is the single largest systemic risk in the AI-for-IO programme, and it carries a geographic gradient: it will be worst in the data-poor economies whose footprints matter most for the equity claims consumption-based accounting exists to make.

One institutional fact deserves emphasis because the academic IO community appears largely unaware of it. The official-statistics system is already further along than the research literature. Eurostat's AIML4OS programme (grant 101146355, April 2024 – March 2028, 15–16 participating countries, 13 work packages) has dedicated work packages on AI/ML data editing (WP8), imputation (WP9), **mapping supply-chain networks from company-level data (WP11)** — which is an input–output problem being solved outside the input–output literature — large language models (WP12) and synthetic data (WP13), plus a shared experimentation "sandpit" (WP3) [aiml4os; aiml4os-cros]. UNECE's HLG-MOS Machine Learning Project has run multi-country pilots in classification and coding with published, sobering results, and Destatis has produced a six-dimension quality concept for ML in official statistics that maps almost directly onto MRIO compilation [unece-class; dumpert23]. Whatever the research community decides, ML-derived estimates will arrive in supply-use tables through this channel — and MRIO compilers will not necessarily know which figures are model output.

Finally, a caution the field should pre-empt rather than absorb. The analogy from weather and climate emulation — GraphCast matching or beating operational numerical weather prediction at a fraction of the cost [graphcast], Aurora as an Earth-system foundation model [aurora] — will be made, and it is a category error. Weather ML trains on order 10⁶–10⁹ hourly, globally complete, physically reconciled reanalysis states of a stationary, non-reflexive system. Input–output has roughly 25 annual, partly modelled, revision-prone observations of a non-stationary, reflexive system whose structure changes in response to the policies being modelled. "GraphCast beat the physics model, therefore ML will beat IO models" does not follow, and any output of this research programme should say so explicitly.


## 0.1 Opportunity matrix: AI capability × IO workflow stage

Rows are AI capability families; columns are stages of the IO workflow. Each cell gives **evidence status** and **impact if realised**.

Legend — evidence status: **▲** demonstrated in IO/EEIO (published applications on actual IO objects) · **◆** demonstrated adjacent (proven in a neighbouring field, transferable, not yet applied to IO) · **○** speculative (no known implementation anywhere in this combination) · **–** not a meaningful combination. Impact: **H** high · **M** medium · **L** low.

| AI capability ↓ / Workflow stage → | A. Compilation & estimation | B. Dis/aggregation & imputation | C. Harmonisation & concordance | D. Structure discovery & indicators | E. Footprinting | F. Shocks & forward-looking | G. Coupling & integration | H. Validation, comms & decision support |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Supervised tabular learning** (GBDT, RF, SVM, k-NN, shallow ANN) | ▲ H — regional coefficients, non-survey estimation; ▲ H residual learning on top of RAS | ▲ H — satellite gap-filling; subnational extensions | ▲ H — auto-coding to NACE/ISIC/HS | ▲ M — key-sector features, learned indicators | ▲ H — attribute-based product footprints | ◆ H — nowcasting output, trade, emissions | ▲ H — material stocks → dynamic MFA | ◆ M — quality scoring, reliability priors |
| **2. Graph & representation learning** (GNN, embeddings, network science, matrix/tensor factorisation) | ◆ H — trade-block estimation as edge prediction | ▲ H — firm-network reconstruction; ○ H tensor completion for missing country-years | ○ M — structural (Gromov–Wasserstein) sector alignment | ▲ H — communities, motifs, multilayer MRIO, centrality | ▲ M — GNN footprint propagation (single instance) | ◆ H — learned production functions; cascade models | ◆ H — bill-of-materials recovery from transactions | ○ M — learned aggregation for visualisation |
| **3. Generative & language models** (LLM, RAG, agents, GAN/VAE/diffusion, synthetic data) | ▲ H — constraint-embedded GAN for regional/world tables; ◆ H LCI/EPD extraction from documents | ◆ M — synthetic microdata for disclosure control | ▲ H — spend→sector, product→EEIO classification | ○ M — literature-scale hypothesis mining | ▲ H — spend-based Scope 3 at industrial scale | ◆ L–M — scenario narratives; storyline→parameters | ▲ H — multi-agent inventory construction; ◆ H RAG methodology assistants | ○ H — LLM agents over pymrio/useeior; ◆ H automated reporting audit |
| **4. Probabilistic & Bayesian ML** (Bayesian inference, GPs, conformal, ensembles, UQ) | ▲ H — Bayesian/entropy balancing; posterior over tables | ◆ H — calibrated intervals on imputed cells | ◆ M — conformal sets for auto-coding triage | ◆ M — indicator confidence intervals | ▲ H — Monte Carlo footprint UQ with correlated priors | ◆ M — density nowcasts; probabilistic tech forecasts | ▲ H — conformal hybrid LCA (preprint) | ▲ H — uncertainty layers, posterior predictive checks |
| **5. Optimisation, differentiable programming & RL** | ▲ H — constrained-optimisation compilation; ▲ H balancing operator inside a learned generator (single-region); ○ H the same at MRIO scale | ○ M — constrained disaggregation layers | – | ○ M — symbolic regression for indicator discovery | ○ H — autodiff sensitivity of footprints to all cells | ▲ H — IO as LP with capacity limits; ▲ H differentiable ARIO calibration; ◆ M RL policy search | ◆ H — surrogates closing IAM/ESM↔MRIO loops | ○ H — hard-constraint layers guaranteeing balance |
| **6. Unsupervised discovery** (clustering, anomaly detection, dimension reduction) | ▲ M — geospatial foundation models locating supply-chain assets; ◆ M editing and outlier flagging pre-publication | ◆ M — similarity-based donor selection | ◆ M — embedding-based crosswalk proposals | ▲ H — PCA/clustering of key sectors; ○ H NMF technology archetypes | ▲ H — household consumption archetypes | ▲ M — change-point detection on A_t | ◆ M — divergence diagnostics EEIO vs process LCA | ○ H — anomaly detection on coefficient matrices (verified gap) |
| **7. Causal ML** (DML, causal forests, synthetic control, structure learning) | – | – | – | ◆ M — heterogeneity in emission drivers | ◆ M — attribution vs accounting decomposition | ◆ H — ex-post policy evaluation feeding IO parameters | ◆ H — leakage/CBAM effect estimation | ◆ M — disciplining causal language in IO reporting |

**How to read the matrix.** The mass of ▲ (demonstrated in IO) sits in the upper-left quadrant — supervised and probabilistic methods applied to compilation, imputation and harmonisation. That is Tier 1 and it is working. The mass of ◆ (proven next door, unapplied here) sits in rows 2, 5 and 7 — graph learning, differentiable optimisation and causal ML — which is where the transferable value is concentrated and where a well-designed research programme would concentrate effort. The ○ cells in row 5, column H (hard accounting constraints inside learned components) and row 3, column H (agents that *call* an IO model rather than recall its numbers) are the two highest-leverage empty cells in the table: the first makes Tier 3 respectable, the second makes Tier 1 safe at scale. **One change since the first edition should be read as a proof of concept for the whole upper-left strategy:** column A of rows 1, 3 and 5 has turned ▲, because two 2022/2026 papers put the balancing operator and the classical estimator *inside* the learned model rather than beside it [zhao22-dl; depretis26]. That construction is not database-specific and not table-specific; it is the template, and nothing prevents its application to every other column.


---


# Part A — Data (pre-)processing


## 1. Data estimation, generation and compilation

Estimation and compilation is where AI has actually landed in input–output economics, and where the accounting identities protect the analyst: a learned prior followed by a balancing step cannot break the accounts. The demonstrated cases cluster in regional coefficient estimation, Bayesian and entropy-based balancing, satellite-account construction, and near-real-time compilation; the open frontier is end-to-end differentiable balancing, generative models of tables themselves, and calibrated uncertainty on every produced cell. The recurring hazard is validation circularity — models are validated against tables produced under the same conventions they are learning.

- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO]** **Supervised learning of regional input coefficients, replacing location-quotient non-survey methods.** Classical non-survey estimators (SLQ, CILQ, FLQ, RAS) are hand-specified parametric functions `a_ij^r = f(a_ij, LQ_i^r)` with one or two tuned parameters; ML simply replaces the assumed functional form with a learned one over far richer regional covariates. A feed-forward network with *mixup*-style augmentation — synthesising training regions by composition and rescaling of observed ones — reports higher and more stable accuracy than conventional non-survey methods and matches officially published municipal tables for three Japanese cities [mixup24; mixup-arxiv]; a 2026 successor adds a matrix-balancing refinement and beats balancing alone against Japan's 2015 benchmark [dl-balance26]; tree ensembles and ANNs have separately been reported to substantially outperform location quotients for regional coefficients and multipliers [pakizeh22]. Mixup is unusually well motivated here because IO tables are approximately closed under non-negative linear combination and rescaling, so augmented samples are economically valid rather than statistical noise. **Caveat the reviewers converged on:** the entire demonstrated evidence base is Japanese, in a country with an unusually rich regional statistical system, and the models learn from officially published regional tables many of which were themselves produced by non-survey methods.
- **[F: medium-high · I: high · Proven in IO — the strongest constraint-embedded architecture found anywhere]** **Generative estimation of regional tables with the balancing operator *inside* the network.** A hybrid "GAN+Boost" framework generates regional input–output tables with **hard marginal constraints imposed by an iterative-proportional-fitting layer embedded inside the GAN generator**, followed by random-forest residual boosting on the generator's errors [depretis26]. On 43 Italian national tables (35 train / 8 test) it reports R² = 0.9417 against 0.8524 for Improved-RAS, and — the diagnostically important number — raises the correlation of the *diagonal* (own-sector) entries, the cells biproportional methods systematically distort, from 0.3165 to 0.8228; on 15 world tables (12/3) it reports R² = 0.9972 and 56% lower RMSE. **This single paper resolves what we had listed as an open speculation.** It demonstrates in the IO setting exactly the design principle this report argues for throughout — a learned prior composed with a differentiable projection that makes the accounting identities structurally unbreakable rather than penalised — and it does so with the balancing step *differentiated through*, not applied afterwards. Three cautions the paper does not fully address: the test sets are eight and three tables respectively, so the reported R² carries split variance that is not quantified; a GAN trained on published tables inherits whatever RAS-derived structure those tables already contain, so the comparison against Improved-RAS is not fully independent; and R² on log or level entries is dominated by the large cells, while the policy-relevant errors usually sit in small ones.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO — the strongest demonstrated gap-filling result in the field]** **Residual deep learning on top of RAS: let the classical method carry the structure and learn only its error.** Rather than replacing biproportional updating, a deep neural network is trained to predict the *residual* between the RAS estimate and the observed table, and the correction is added back. On US summary input–output tables this raises one-year-ahead R² from 0.6412 to 0.8726 and cuts median absolute percentage error from 37.49% to 11.35%; over a five-year horizon, from R² 0.5271 to 0.7893 and median APE 51.12% to 18.26% — and the improvement is carried end-to-end through a US carbon-footprint case study rather than being reported only in cell-error space [zhao22-dl]. **This is, on our reading of everything assembled here and in the eleven peer reports, the single most convincing published demonstration that machine learning materially improves an actual EEIO product.** Its architecture is also the safest available template: the accounting-consistent classical estimator remains the backbone, the learned component is a bounded correction, and the residual formulation means a failure of the network degrades gracefully towards RAS rather than catastrophically. The limits are the field's usual ones — a single country, summary-level sectors, tables that are themselves partly modelled — plus one specific to residual designs: re-balancing after the correction is required, and the paper's treatment of what balancing does to the learned residual deserves replication.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Proven in IO]** **Bayesian balancing of supply-use tables: RAS and GRAS as special cases of posterior inference.** Matrix balancing *is* posterior inference — the unbalanced table is a prior mean, source reliability is a prior variance, and the accounting identities are a likelihood or a hard constraint manifold. It has been shown formally that GLS, WLS and biproportional methods are particular cases of relative-entropy minimisation under a Bayesian model, with RAS corresponding to the implicit assumption of uniform relative uncertainty on every cell [rodrigues14]. Once that is seen, the upgrade is obvious: replace the flat prior with a learned, heterogeneous, data-quality-aware prior and sample rather than optimise, yielding a *posterior over tables* with the covariances the constraints induce. A working large-scale implementation (Hamiltonian Monte Carlo via Stan, >1.8 million flows at BONSAI scale, monetary and physical layers) has been presented, at a cost of 19–44 hours and 24 GB RAM for four chains [schulte26-bayes]. This is much further along than we expected and is the only credible route to honest covariance-aware footprint uncertainty.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Proven in IO]** **Generalised cross-entropy balancing for tables with negative entries.** Each cell is treated as a categorical variable over a support vector with prior probabilities; the estimator minimises KL divergence subject to margin constraints — the same maximum-entropy machinery that underlies softmax classifiers. Crucially the discretisation lets estimates cross zero, which biproportional updating mathematically cannot, and it yields cell-level variances as a by-product [fvazquez-gce; temurshoev18]. The known weakness is that results depend on the arbitrary choice of support bounds, a hidden researcher degree of freedom.
- **[F: medium · I: medium-high · SPECULATION]** **End-to-end differentiable balancing (RAS-as-Sinkhorn inside a learned pipeline).** RAS *is* Sinkhorn–Knopp, the workhorse of entropic optimal transport, and is fully differentiable and GPU-parallelisable. A network can therefore predict a prior matrix and be trained through the balancing operator, with the loss defined on the *balanced* output — so it learns priors that are good *after* balancing, which is not the same objective as current two-stage practice (predict, then RAS). **This is no longer wholly speculative: the IPF layer inside the GAN generator of [depretis26] is precisely this construction, and it works.** What remains unbuilt is the general case — a learned prior for a full multi-regional table trained through GRAS or KRAS with heterogeneous, quality-weighted cell reliabilities, rather than a single-region generator trained through IPF. *Speculation, with a warning that the demonstrated case does not remove:* a prior optimised to survive balancing may be systematically biased in exactly the directions balancing cannot see, producing tables that are internally consistent and externally wrong — a failure mode that passes every routine check.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Adjacent]** **Nowcasting IO structure from high-frequency inter-industry payment data.** An inter-industry payments matrix *is* an observed, high-frequency monetary transaction matrix. A generalised network autoregressive model with time-varying edge weights on UK payment-system data has been used for GDP nowcasting, validated against nine quarterly ONS releases for revision robustness [gnar-payments]. The binding constraint is access, not method: the data sit with central banks and payment operators. Bias risk is specific and severe — payment coverage skews to large formal firms, so a payments-derived structure would systematically understate small-firm and informal input patterns.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO]** **Near-real-time MRIO compilation as the baseline any AI nowcast must beat.** A full-scale near-real-time MRIO for global emerging economies has been built with a pipeline of extrapolation, proxy-driven estimation and optimisation [emerging22]. Its AI content is limited, which is precisely why it matters: it defines the incumbent that learned methods must outperform, and it is the natural host for ML components (learned proxies, learned extrapolators). Its structural-change blindness — extrapolated structures assume slow change, which is wrong exactly for rapidly industrialising economies — is the gap an ML approach should target.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO]** **Monte Carlo uncertainty propagation with correlated (Dirichlet) sampling.** Emission allocation across sectors is a *composition*; independent lognormal sampling of shares is simply wrong, and Dirichlet sampling is the correct object — and is exactly what a Bayesian treatment would use. Propagating UNFCCC National Inventory Report and EDGAR uncertainties through EXIOBASE at 2015 gives the country/sector CV asymmetry quoted in the synthesis, with the full correlated accounts deposited openly [schulte24; schulte-zenodo]. This is the strongest quantitative anchor in the field and its sector-level numbers are a standing rebuke to the routine practice of reporting sectoral footprints to three significant figures.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent]** **Conformal prediction and calibrated uncertainty for ML-produced statistical cells.** Split conformal prediction gives finite-sample, distribution-free marginal coverage from a held-out calibration set with no assumption about the model — which matches the accountability requirements of official statistics far better than a Bayesian posterior with an invented prior, and maps naturally onto the existing NSI workflow of auto-coding the confident cases and routing the rest to human coders. Statistics Canada has discussed conformal prediction, Bayesian dropout, ensembles and calibration inside a quality framework for statistical algorithms [molladavoudi23; puts-daas23; tmle-manifesto]. Quantile-regression prediction intervals with 94.5% empirical coverage have been demonstrated for ML-estimated subnational GHG inventories [yu26]. **No published application to IO table cells exists** — one of the cleanest low-cost gaps in the map. The catch is that conformal guarantees are *marginal*, not conditional: a procedure can be 90% correct overall and 40% correct for African agriculture unless it is explicitly conditioned (Mondrian/class-conditional variants).
- **[F: high · I: medium · Adjacent]** **Gaussian-process surrogates for inventory and uncertainty analysis.** GPs give closed-form predictive distributions and interpolate exactly through observations — the right choice when observations are few, expensive and high-quality, and when a smoothness prior over technology space is more plausible than "pick the nearest proxy dataset". Demonstrated for LCA inventory and uncertainty analysis [gp-lca]; scaling is O(n³), so the role is targeted rather than database-wide.
- **[F: medium · I: medium · Mixed]** **Generative models for synthetic IO tables and synthetic microdata.** Synthetic microdata for disclosure control is established practice in official statistics, including GAN-based approaches [unece-gan; gan-compare], and synthetic *text* has already been used to build an EEIO benchmark because real corporate ledgers are confidential [atlas]. Synthetic *IO tables* are no longer a blank: a GAN with an embedded IPF constraint layer plus random-forest residual boosting generates regional and world tables and outperforms Improved-RAS [depretis26]. We found no VAE or diffusion model for IOTs, and no generative model of a *multi-regional* table with its trade blocks. Copulas are the better-motivated tool than vanilla GANs here, because they separate known heavy-tailed marginals from the dependence structure, and a projection layer can impose the hard linear constraints. **The risk is the sharpest instance of validation circularity in the whole map:** if models are benchmarked on LLM-generated data, they are being scored on their ability to reproduce a language model's priors about the economy rather than the economy.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO]** **ML/AutoML construction of environmental extensions where no inventory exists.** Gradient-boosted and stacked ensembles over gridded emissions products, climate, air-quality, population, GDP and electricity covariates produce Scope 1 and 2 estimates for 5,972 cities and 116 regions across the G20 for 2000–2020, with R² = 0.77, MAPE 38.6%, 95% prediction intervals achieving 94.5% empirical coverage, and — importantly — honestly reported per-country epistemic uncertainty of 11% (Europe), 34% (South Africa), 55% (Saudi Arabia) [yu26]. The authors explicitly decline to present it as a replacement for official inventories. This entry is also the clearest quantified example of bias entrenchment in the map: the model is trained mostly on European self-reporters and is least certain exactly where policy stakes are highest.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Proven in IO]** **"Choose the simplest gap-filler": empirical method selection for emissions-database completion.** A systematic benchmark of 18 gap-filling methods across three GHG datasets finds interpolation reaching ~97% for missing *time steps*, random forests and decision trees 60–70% for missing *emitters*, and deep and graph models winning only for complex gaps *and only when many features are available*; the operational conclusion is that feature count, not model class, is the limiting factor [cullen24]. This is an unusually honest negative result and it should temper enthusiasm throughout this report. Graph-based methods are separately flagged as the scalable choice for live pipelines because predictions update cheaply as new nodes and edges arrive.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent]** **LLM extraction of inventory and impact data from unstructured documents.** The bottleneck in extension construction is *reading*: the numbers exist, in tables and prose, across tens of thousands of PDFs, in inconsistent units and system boundaries. Schema-constrained retrieval-augmented extraction with citation back to the source span preserves auditability, and peer-reviewed frameworks now exist in *Environmental Science & Technology* [est-llm-lci; est-llm-grand] *(both citation-unverified: DOIs confirmed in indexes, full text 403)*, with reviews of ML and LLMs for life-cycle inventory compilation [jcp-llm-lci]. The failure mode is not a hallucinated number but a hallucinated *system boundary* or unit, which no balance check will catch; mandatory human verification sampling with reported extraction error rates is the minimum discipline.


## 2. Data handling, aggregation/disaggregation and imputation

This is the stage where AI most directly changes what an IO table *is*: from a sector-level aggregate to something that can be resolved to firms, sub-national regions, products and grid cells. The strongest demonstrated results are in firm-level network reconstruction — including one validated against a real national transaction registry — and in ML-driven satellite-account completion. The strongest untried opportunity is low-rank matrix and tensor completion, which fits the MRIO panel exceptionally well and appears nowhere in the literature.

- **[F: high · I: high · Proven / adjacent]** **Firm-to-firm production-network reconstruction as supervised link prediction.** For each candidate ordered firm pair, predict `P(i supplies j)` from industry, location and size — a learned generalisation of the gravity model, and a ranking rather than classification problem given the extreme N² class imbalance. Gradient boosting on three real firm-level datasets substantially outperforms three baselines [mungo23]. The key predictive signals are exactly the variables available in almost every business register, so the method is deployable without new data collection. **The single most important negative finding in this literature is that cross-dataset transfer degrades performance significantly** — a model fitted in Ecuador does not transfer to Hungary — which is a direct warning against any "one global firm-network model".
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO]** **Maximum-entropy reconstruction of firm networks *from sector-level IO tables*, validated against ground truth.** Four generative models (stripe-corrected gravity, IO-gravity, density-corrected gravity, stripe-corrected maximum entropy) are validated against Ecuador's 2008 VAT data — 60,488 firms, 387 ISIC sectors, 2.189 million transactions — with the best reaching Pearson correlation ≈0.8 against empirical Economic Systemic Risk Index values and correctly identifying 7 of the top 10 riskiest firms [fessina24; fessina-sr]. This is one of very few rigorously ground-truthed disaggregation methods in the field, and it means public IO tables carry more firm-level information than has been assumed. It also demonstrates why the exercise matters: on Hungarian VAT data, 32 firms out of 91,595 (0.035%) carry systemic risk equivalent to ~23% of national output, firm *size* does not predict systemic risk (network position does), and sector-level analysis obscures the pattern entirely [diem21]. **The risk is proportionate:** naming specific firms as systemically important on the basis of a *reconstructed* network is a serious misuse hazard with market and legal consequences.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent]** **Graph neural networks as a gravity-model generalisation for bilateral trade estimation.** The gravity model is a specific parameterisation of edge weight as a function of node masses and a distance kernel; a gravity-informed graph auto-encoder learns the node embeddings and the interaction function instead, and message passing lets a country's trade with A inform its predicted trade with B — the correct inductive bias for a trade matrix, which is manifestly not a set of independent dyads [ggae24; nber-gravity-ml]. MRIO trade blocks, especially services and rest-of-world, are among the weakest parts of every global table. The entrenchment risk is sharp: a model trained on 2000–2019 trade will smooth over exactly the discontinuities (COVID, sanctions, export controls) users most want to see.
- **[F: high · I: high · SPECULATION — a verified gap]** **Matrix completion and low-rank tensor factorisation for missing IO cells and missing country-years.** IO tables are empirically very low rank — a handful of latent technology factors explain most variation across countries and years, which is the same premise that makes RAS work at all. Nuclear-norm matrix completion and CP/Tucker factorisation over a (country × sector × sector × year) tensor would predict a missing country-year from the low-rank structure of the rest of the panel, needing neither margins (unlike RAS) nor covariates (unlike regression), and combining naturally with margin constraints to stay accounting-consistent. The machinery is mature in econometrics and recommender systems [athey-mc; sengupta23; coupled-tensor]; we found **no** application to IO/MRIO panels. Two caveats: MNAR missingness (data absent *because* a country is poor or opaque) violates standard completion theory; and low-rank completion will make every imputed country look like a weighted average of observed countries, erasing structural distinctiveness exactly where it matters most. A prior question is directly testable on open EXIOBASE data and appears never to have been published: **does a global MRIO actually exhibit low-rank spectral decay?**
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO]** **Sectoral disaggregation using a donor database plus GRAS — and the ML upgrade.** FIGARO-E3 disaggregates FIGARO's 64 industries to 175 industries × 213 products across 46 countries plus rest-of-world, using EXIOBASE monetary structures as the donor prior and GRAS to restore margins, with labour, energy and GHG extensions [figaro-e3]. Its headline number is the strongest available argument that consistency with official statistics is a first-order property: **weighted absolute percentage error between FIGARO and FIGARO-E3 is 1%, against 34–83% when other MRIOs are compared to FIGARO.** The ML opportunity is precisely the donor-structure step — a learned prior conditioned on country, year and observable auxiliaries could beat a fixed donor — and the risk is spurious resolution: 213 products where the information supports 64 is interpolation dressed as data, and users cannot tell which cells are informative.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Proven in IO]** **Enterprise-level MRIO: disaggregating sectors into named firms.** A published enterprise-level global supply-chain database covers 121 countries, 9,466 companies and 86,305 subsegments — roughly 5.7× the resolution of conventional MRIO — by fusing financial segment data, B2B transaction records, bills of lading and regulatory filings [emrio25]. Notably it does *not* rely on ML prediction; but its three hardest processing steps are natural AI targets: segment→sector mapping (text classification), trade-value imputation from incomplete shipping records (regression/matrix completion), and consolidated→unconsolidated conversion (entity resolution). Coverage bias is structural — listed and large firms only — and proprietary inputs make it unauditable.
- **[F: low-medium · I: high · Adjacent]** **Product-level input structures from cross-border firm transaction records.** An excess-co-occurrence measure (how much more often firms producing Y buy X than average) plus community detection, applied to roughly 20 million firms and 1 billion cross-border transactions, recovers interpretable product communities and identifies chokepoint products by betweenness [oclery25]. This is the first large-scale *empirical* check on the sparsity structure IO compilers assume. **A methodological red flag with direct bearing on circularity:** the resulting network was validated in part against LLM-generated product networks — using a language model's latent priors about production as ground truth, when those priors derive from the same public descriptions (and possibly the same IO tables) the exercise is meant to test independently.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Adjacent]** **Graph-based / relational imputation across processes and flows — and the field's best cautionary tale.** Similarity-based k-NN link prediction on ecoinvent 3.1 (8,154 processes × 5,242 flows), validated on the US LCI database, achieves 34.68% MAPE on existing flows and 0.15% misclassification for correctly identifying non-existent flows [zhao25]. The same paper documents why every accuracy claim in this literature should be discounted: the top 20% of flows account for 72% of non-zero entries; magnitudes span twelve orders of magnitude; **random train/test splits produce 25.82% performance variation**; transferring to the smaller US LCI raises median error by 46% (50.6% vs 34.68%), so database *size* rather than method drives performance; and only 5% missingness was tested, far below real gap rates. Any claim of the form "our model achieves X% error on gap-filling" should be treated as an upper bound unless split variance is reported. Popularity bias is the substantive danger: recommender-style methods over-predict common flows and under-represent rare-but-toxic ones.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Proven in IO, citation unverified]** **Bayesian spatial models for high-resolution interregional tables.** Interregional trade coefficients vary smoothly in space, so CAR/SAR or Gaussian-process distance priors are the right regularisation for a problem with far more parameters than observations, and they give posterior intervals the location-quotient tradition cannot [bayes-spatial-irio *(citation unverified)*; streco24]. Smoothing erases genuine regional specialisation, which is exactly what regional policy cares about.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Adjacent, increasingly in IO]** **Spatial downscaling of IO-derived footprints with remote sensing.** Emissions allocation is a disaggregation with a known total — a compositional problem where a learned model predicts shares that are then normalised to the national or sectoral control total, and that normalisation is what keeps the result IO-consistent. Night-lights, population, land cover, traffic and atmospheric composition products are mature [odiac-vs-hestia; china-downscale; nightlights-elec]. Two structural limits: night lights saturate in dense cities and are near-blind to non-electric industrial heat; and proxies correlate with *activity*, not *emission intensity*, so technology heterogeneity is invisible. Validation against other downscaled products that share covariates is circular, and bottom-up comparisons have repeatedly shown large divergence.
- **[F: medium · I: medium-high · Adjacent]** **Imputation of item non-response in the surveys that feed national accounts.** A survey record with missing items is literally a masked sequence, so masked-token and denoising-autoencoder imputers fit naturally, and LLM prompting for item non-response has been demonstrated [remasker; fi-prompt-imputation; gles-llm]. The blocker is not accuracy but variance estimation: proper multiple-imputation variance under a black-box model is unsolved in the design-based framework NSIs use, and MNAR non-response (large firms declining to report) is not fixable by any imputer. Imputing non-responding large firms from responding ones systematically compresses the upper tail — which matters enormously for concentrated, high-emitting sectors.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent]** **Entity resolution and record linkage across firm, trade and environmental databases.** Blocking plus learned pairwise classification plus transitive clustering, with sentence-embedding similarity over company name, address and activity text handling transliteration and legal-form suffixes far better than edit distance; embedding-based blocking makes the O(N²) problem tractable [census-rl; entity-res-sciadv; ibm-rl]. Every micro-to-macro and firm-level EEIO project spends most of its effort here and linkage quality caps everything downstream. The asymmetry is the risk: matching is easier for large, Latin-script, well-documented firms, so linkage rates are systematically higher in rich countries — a coverage bias that looks like a real economic difference in the final table.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent, in production]** **Web-scraped and administrative data to improve the business register — the sector-allocation spine of the IOT.** A firm's actual activity is described in natural language on its website and in its job advertisements, while its register code may be decades stale; a text classifier maps description → NACE with a confidence score and the register is updated only above threshold. Eurostat's Web Intelligence Network and Hub run online job advertisements and online-based enterprise characteristics as flagship use cases, with published experimental statistics [cros-win; cros-oja; eurostat-exp-ict; cros-sbr-scraping]. A mis-coded firm is a systematic mis-allocation of value added and emissions between IO sectors. **The under-appreciated risk is time-series damage:** improving the register with ML creates spurious structural change in the IO series that will be read as real economic change and will propagate into structural decomposition analyses.


## 3. Data harmonisation and concordance

Harmonisation is the highest-volume, lowest-status and most immediately automatable part of the IO workflow, and it is where language models are already deployed commercially at scale. It is also where the field's most sobering measured accuracy figure sits. The strategic point is that concordance is not a nuisance to be automated away but the layer at which comparability across databases, classifications and vintages is either preserved or silently destroyed.

- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent, in production]** **Automated coding of text to statistical classifications (NACE / ISIC / NAICS / COICOP / occupation).** A hierarchical, extremely imbalanced text-classification problem where the label tree can be exploited to make graceful errors rather than arbitrary ones. The UNECE HLG-MOS Machine Learning Project's classification-and-coding theme is the best evidence base available, spanning eight statistical organisations: reported accuracies of 87.7% (economic activity) and 83.1% (occupation) in Mexico, >95% in Canada combined with clerical review, 69.8–91.9% at the US Bureau of Labor Statistics **outperforming human coders**, 90–92% in Poland, ~22% auto-coded but in production in Norway — and only 55–75% in Serbia, judged insufficient for production [unece-class; unece-ml-pub]. **Only three of eight solutions reached operational status**, the report warns that neural networks are hard to use effectively and advises starting with simpler methods, and it identifies establishing a validated ground-truth dataset as the make-or-break prerequisite. The reported primary benefit shifted from cost saving to *consistency* — ML removes subjective coder variation. The workable production pattern is bounded automation: auto-accept above a confidence threshold, route the rest to human coders, publish the threshold and the error rates.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO]** **LLM and retrieval classification of spend, product and company descriptions into EEIO sectors.** Two published designs define the state of the art. ATLAS is a public benchmark over 295 classes with the accuracy figures given in the synthesis [atlas; watershed]. GREEN reframes the task as *information retrieval* rather than flat classification, fine-tunes Sentence-BERT with contrastive learning and ensembles LLM classifiers hierarchically over the NAICS ontology, arguing — correctly, in our view — that with 1,114 leaf classes a flat softmax is statistically and computationally wasteful whereas retrieval over class *descriptions* generalises to unseen classes; it reports 83.68% top-1 and 91.47% top-10 over 20,850 companies and releases the open ExioNAICS dataset [green25; exionaics]. CaML establishes the zero-shot baseline at 48% accuracy / 22% MAPE, matching non-expert annotators [caml23]. **These numbers are not comparable to each other** — different label spaces, different input quality (a company description is far richer than a terse ledger line), different metrics — and the field should stop quoting them as if they were. The error propagates multiplicatively: sector emission intensities vary by two to three orders of magnitude across BEA codes, so a 43% top-1 error rate does not translate into a 43% footprint error; nobody reports the distribution.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Adjacent]** **ML and LLM classification of goods to HS/HTS codes.** Hierarchical multi-label text classification with an explainability requirement, because a customs classification is a legally consequential decision that must be justifiable; published work emphasises explainable and generalisable frameworks for exactly that reason [customs-xai]. HS coding quality drives the HS→CPA/CPC→IO-sector chain and is a documented source of bilateral asymmetry. **A risk class largely absent from the IO literature's vocabulary appears here:** once a classifier is known, it can be probed and evaded, and traders have financial incentives to mis-declare.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent]** **Data-driven construction of concordance and bridging matrices between classification vintages.** The clearest published example exploits the empirical persistence of product-level trade flows and solves a constrained least-squares problem to allocate trade values across reclassifications, deriving conversion weights from data rather than assuming a hand-made concordance, and — critically — preserving many-to-many relationships instead of forcing 1:1 mappings. It recovers roughly three million additional trade observations annually versus raw Comtrade, a coverage increase of about 50% [growthlab-sdata]. This is a learning problem in the strict sense: infer an allocation matrix between two label spaces from observed joint behaviour across the vintage boundary. *Speculation:* a learned concordance smooths genuine discontinuities, so structural decomposition analyses spanning a classification break may attribute to technology change what is actually concordance smoothing.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent, ML upgrades speculative]** **ML-assisted reconciliation of bilateral trade asymmetries.** The same pipeline decomposes mirror-statistics discrepancies through a regression model of CIF-to-FOB margins from distance and contiguity, a network-based reliability decomposition exploiting the fact that each country reports trade with many partners, and reliability-weighted averaging [growthlab-sdata; comtrade-asym; unescap-asym]. Each component generalises naturally to ML — a learned margin model with commodity effects, a hierarchical Bayesian or graph model with per-commodity reliability, a learned weighting. Trade asymmetries determine which country's account carries an embodied emission, so mirror reconciliation *is* footprint allocation. **Entrenchment risk:** a country scored unreliable is down-weighted permanently, so its reported structure never gets to correct the record — with real distributional consequences.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO-adjacent]** **Semantic-similarity matching of products and processes to proxy datasets and emission factors.** Zero-shot or fine-tuned sentence embeddings with nearest-neighbour retrieval, and generative-AI recommenders over factor databases [est-ef-recommender; sself25]. Proxy selection is currently manual, slow, undocumented and highly analyst-dependent; making it reproducible and score-bearing is a genuine methodological gain. Two limits deserve emphasis: textual similarity is not *environmental* similarity (two chemically similar products can differ tenfold in impact depending on energy source), and embedding retrieval will converge many distinct items onto a few well-described central datasets, artificially reducing cross-product variance — which defeats the purpose of product-level differentiation.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO, no published evaluation]** **Embedding-based automation of the LCA↔IO concordance specifically.** A research module explicitly automating the mapping of two classifications by sentence-transformer word similarity, targeting NAICS/NACE/ISIC, product classifications and LCIA frameworks, exists as working software in the same toolchain as the leading hybridisation package — **with no associated peer-reviewed publication and no published accuracy evaluation** [ciraig-mapping]. That combination (deployed, unevaluated) is itself a finding.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Proven in IO]** **Ontologies and knowledge graphs as the harmonisation substrate.** Explicit ontologies make concordances *derivable* rather than hand-maintained: if HS, CPC and CPA codes are all linked to shared product concepts, the bridging matrix falls out of the graph; and once the data is a graph, missing edges become a link-prediction problem, unifying harmonisation and imputation in one representation. The BONSAI core ontology for life-cycle sustainability assessment on the Semantic Web is the reference effort [ghose22; bonsai-eswc; bonsai-wiki]. **A concrete interoperability gap surfaced by two reviewers independently:** olca-schema, the dominant LCA exchange format, has no native IO/EEIO representation [olca-schema], so after decades of hybridisation research the two data worlds still lack a shared schema. The deeper risk is false interoperability — two databases linked to the same concept URI may still be measuring different things (system boundary, valuation basis); the graph makes the join *possible*, not *correct*.
- **[F: medium · I: high · SPECULATION]** **Structural alignment and ensembling across GMRIO databases.** Aligning EXIOBASE, WIOD, Eora, GTAP, ICIO, FIGARO and GLORIA sector spaces is a schema-matching problem: embeddings handle the semantics, and Gromov–Wasserstein optimal transport is the mathematically natural tool for the cases where labels are uninformative, because it aligns two *relational structures* without needing a shared feature space. The need is thoroughly demonstrated by the divergence literature [moran-wood14; owen16; giljum19; figaro-e3]; the ML method is not. *Speculation with a trap attached:* an ensembled footprint across GMRIOs would have a spread that looks like convergence but reflects shared upstream sources (all use Comtrade, IEA, EDGAR). The spread between GMRIOs is a **lower bound** on uncertainty, not an estimate of it.
- **[F: medium · I: medium-high · Mixed]** **Unit, currency, deflator and price-basis harmonisation.** Mostly a rules-and-metadata problem, with two genuine AI roles: LLM extraction of unit and valuation metadata from source documentation and table headers (today read by humans and lost), and learned deflators from web-scraped and scanner prices, where product matching across time is a classic entity-resolution task already in production in official statistics [cros-tss; istat-bigdata; cros-suiot]. **We rate this one of the largest *unquantified* systematic errors in commercial spend-based carbon accounting:** spend data are in purchasers' prices while EEIO intensities are per unit of basic-price output, and applying one to the other overstates footprints by the trade-and-transport margin plus taxes — potentially 20–60% for retail-heavy categories. It is invisible to every accuracy metric reported in the classification literature, which scores labels, not valuation. At least one commercial API implements the deterministic corrections (inflation, currency, margins, basic↔purchaser conversion) explicitly [climatiq-guide] — vendor evidence, unverified — which is a case where the right recommendation is "automate deterministically; use AI only to detect which adjustment is needed."


---


# Part B — Analysis


## 4. Data mining and structure discovery: input–output data as a network

Representing an MRIO as a weighted, directed, multilayer network is the most natural and best-developed bridge between IO and modern machine learning, and it is where the field's own methods and network science demonstrably converge. The mature results are in community detection, centrality-based key-sector analysis and motif mining against maximum-entropy nulls; the untried frontier is graph neural networks on the A-matrix, non-negative matrix factorisation for technology archetypes, and graph-level representation of whole economies. The recurring hazard is that discovered "structure" is a construction artefact of one database.

- **[F: high · I: medium · Proven in IO]** **Centrality-based key-sector identification.** PageRank is algebraically a damped Leontief inverse — `p = (1−d)e + dA'p ⟹ p ∝ (I − dA')⁻¹e` — so classical Rasmussen backward-linkage multipliers are a *special case* of a centrality family, and HITS hub/authority scores map onto the Leontief/Ghosh duality. A systematic mapping between IO concepts and network metrics establishes which measures are redundant with existing IO theory and which acquire genuinely new interpretations, while emphasising that IO networks have *weighted nodes* as well as weighted directed links — a property most off-the-shelf network tools ignore [an24; appnetsci22; mcnerney13; ionet]. Value lies in the non-redundant measures (betweenness, k-core, motif position), because a low-multiplier sector can be a high-betweenness bottleneck. Threshold artefacts are the standing risk: topology conclusions flip with the cut-off [threshold23].
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Proven in IO]** **Community detection on the world input–output network.** Louvain modularity maximisation on WIOD (56 sectors × 43 countries, 2000–2014) with 90 restarts per year and three initial-partition schemes yields 26 largely country-anchored communities, documents the decline of US- and Japan-centred blocs and Germany's absorption of neighbours, and introduces an *assignment variability index* that anticipates community reorganisation — instability as an early-warning signal [kireyev22; cerina15]. This answers a question no multiplier answers. Two structural caveats: modularity has a resolution limit and severe degeneracy (hence the restarts), and MRIO communities are dominated by the country dimension because domestic flows dwarf trade flows, masking sectoral structure unless the domestic block is normalised out.
- **[F: medium-high · I: medium · Proven in IO]** **Multilayer / multiplex representation.** `Z` is natively a four-index tensor `Z[r,i,s,j]`, so multilayer community detection and multilayer centrality ("versatility") are the correct generalisations rather than the aggregated projections normally used; published multilayer analysis of the global IO network reveals trade relations invisible in the aggregate projection and shows both community membership and internal centrality rankings reordering over time [multilayer-gion]. The honest limitation is that inter-layer coupling weights are not given by the data and must be assumed. **Layer-specific results are the most sensitive of all to product-classification concordances, among the least documented parts of any MRIO pipeline.**
- **[F: medium · I: medium-high · Proven in IO]** **Motif mining against maximum-entropy null models — the statistical hygiene the rest of this section lacks.** Output heterogeneity in IO spans six orders of magnitude and manufactures apparent structure; comparing a triad census against maximum-entropy random-graph ensembles is the statistically correct way to ask whether a pattern is real or a consequence of node heterogeneity. Applied to the Dutch inter-industry production network reconstructed from ~900,000 firms (862 industries × 187 commodity groups), the *aggregate* network shows many triadic motifs but **~85% of single-commodity layers show two or fewer significant motifs** — a "triadic fingerprint" per product, implying most of the network is reconstructible from pairwise information once reciprocity is controlled [divece24]. Maximum-entropy ensembles are also generative, so they double as synthetic-MRIO generators for stress-testing downstream ML.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Thin in IO, thick adjacent]** **Graph neural networks on the A-matrix.** A *k*-layer GNN computes `H^(k) = σ(Â H^(k−1) W^(k))`; drop the nonlinearity, set `Â = A` and `W = I`, and you recover the truncated Neumann expansion of `L`. A GNN over the IO graph is therefore the minimal generalisation that makes propagation nonlinear (capacity limits, rationing, substitution), makes edge weights learned functions of node and edge attributes rather than fixed coefficients, and attaches heterogeneous features. **Graph attention is the natural home for input substitutability:** attention weights over incoming edges are a learned, state-dependent generalisation of fixed technical coefficients, interpolating between Leontief (hard complements) and CES/linear. We found one direct GNN⊕IO application, for construction supply-chain carbon footprints [buildenv26] *(Crossref-verified DOI; full text not retrieved)*, against a thick adjacent literature on firm-level supply-chain GNNs [chang25; gnnbench; hypergnn; poisson-gnn; trade-gnn]. **The binding constraint is data poverty:** a global MRIO is roughly 10⁴ nodes and *one graph observed 20–30 times*. That is not a training set. The credible routes are transfer from firm-level transaction networks, pre-training on synthetic tables, graph-level datasets (below), or deliberately low-capacity constrained models — and a GNN that merely reproduces `Lf` is not a contribution.
- **[F: high · I: medium · Adjacent]** **Node and edge embeddings of sectors and country-sectors.** Column-normalised `A` is a Markov kernel over input requirements, so random-walk embeddings are the Neumann series read probabilistically. Knowledge-graph embeddings have been applied to international trade flow analysis, multi-scale embeddings have been developed with explicit *consistency under node aggregation* — exactly the right property for IO, where sector aggregation is arbitrary — and GNN-based industry classification from supply-chain structure exists [kge-trade; kge-trade-springer; multiscale-embed; gnn-industry-class]. We found no node2vec/GraphSAGE embedding of an MRIO table. **An honest critique the reviewers converged on:** for a graph with a known linear generative model, the column of `L` for sector *i* is already a principled, exactly interpretable embedding; a learned embedding must earn its place against that baseline.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Partial: PCA proven, NMF speculative]** **Dimensionality reduction on A and L.** `A` and `L` are non-negative, so **non-negative matrix factorisation is the mathematically correct factorisation**: `A ≈ WH` yields parts-based, additive technology archetypes where columns of `W` read as basis input recipes and `H` as sector loadings — an interpretation PCA's signed loadings cannot support. Stacking `A^(country, year)` gives a four-way tensor whose CP/Tucker decomposition separates country, sector, technology and time modes, which is precisely what structural decomposition analysis attempts by hand with an arbitrary factor ordering. PCA plus hierarchical clustering on OECD tables for Australia is demonstrated and validated against classical linkages at Spearman > 0.80, with clustering adding interconnection information a ranking loses [faridzad25]; unsupervised structural-change typologies exist on composite indices rather than tables [salinas25]. **We found no published NMF, tensor or autoencoder decomposition of `A` or `L`, and rate NMF on stacked A-matrices the highest-value untried idea in this section.** Two warnings: the leading factors of `A` across EXIOBASE years may encode EXIOBASE's own estimation procedures rather than technology; and a low-rank approximation of `A` breaks the accounting identities, so footprints from `Â` will not reconcile to national totals.
- **[F: medium · I: medium-high · Partial]** **Unsupervised discovery of structural change.** Treat `{A_t}` as a temporal graph and apply change-point detection to graph-level summaries, or dynamic community detection with a temporal coupling term — detecting when coefficient structure breaks instead of assuming SDA factors. The assignment-variability index of [kireyev22] is a working instance; formal change-point machinery on `A_t` is not established. **A fundamental, under-appreciated obstacle:** annual resolution and MRIO smoothing (many MRIOs interpolate or hold coefficients fixed between benchmark years) destroy exactly the signal being sought. And the probability of detecting *methodological* change-points — new benchmark tables, classification revisions, source-data changes — and mis-reporting them as economic structural change is high. Any such study must run on ≥2 databases and be cross-checked against revision logs.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · SPECULATION — a verified gap]** **Anomaly and outlier detection on coefficient matrices.** `A` is highly structured (columns are cost shares summing to <1; rows are sales shares), so model-based residuals — fit a maximum-entropy or gravity-style baseline for `Z[i,j]` from marginals and flag cells extreme under that model — are well defined, with cross-year and cross-database consistency as further axes (a coefficient that jumps tenfold in one release and reverts in the next is almost certainly an error). Ensemble unsupervised detection with consensus voting is demonstrated on European regional statistics [oancea26]; the ExioML dataset paper explicitly nominates anomaly detection as an intended use [exioml-sdata]. **Targeted searches for ML anomaly detection on technical coefficients returned nothing.** A published "top-500 suspicious cells in EXIOBASE", risk-weighted by footprint sensitivity, would be immediately useful. **But the anomaly-detection literature's own decisive finding must be carried over:** flagged anomalies "do not necessarily signal data quality issues; rather, they reflect meaningful structural divergence" — anomaly ≠ error [oancea26]. Deploying such a detector as an automatic editing gate would contradict its own evidence base and would systematically erase the structural heterogeneity of small and unusual economies.
- **[F: high · I: high · SPECULATION on method; problem well demonstrated]** **Learning what distinguishes EXIOBASE, WIOD, Eora, GTAP and ICIO.** Two framings. Discriminative: train a classifier to predict which database a country-sector row came from, then use SHAP attribution to localise systematic disagreement — an automatic generalisation of the manual structural-path-decomposition comparisons already published [owen16]. Generative: learn an alignment between databases to build principled concordances and a consensus ensemble with uncertainty. The comparison literature is mature but artisanal [moran-wood14; giljum19; tukker18-owen]. **"Which MRIO did you use?" is the single largest unquantified source of variation in published footprints,** and turning this into a repeatable pipeline is high-value. The trap: a consensus MRIO produced by averaging is *not balanced* and has no accounting interpretation.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Adjacent, contested]** **Economic complexity and relatedness as AI-adjacent inference machinery.** ECI/PCI is a spectral method — an eigenvector of a bipartite country–product matrix, mathematically a cousin of eigenvector centrality on `A` — and the modern literature has replaced hand-designed relatedness density with supervised ML, showing tree-based and network-based models forecast industrial upgrading better than classical density [albora23; hidalgo21; sciadv-complexity; hausmann-hidalgo11; capability-arxiv]. The transfer to IO is direct: replace country–product revealed comparative advantage with country-sector × input requirement, and relatedness becomes "which sectors could this economy plausibly build given its current `A` column structure?" **Contested, and we do not resolve it:** a live critical literature argues ECI/PCI add little beyond diversity measures or GDP-per-capita residuals [rethinking-eci]. ML strengthens the *predictive* case without settling the *interpretive* one, and complexity rankings already justify industrial policy — so the misuse risk is real.
- **[F: high · I: medium · SPECULATION]** **Graph-level representation of whole national tables ("economy fingerprints").** A GNN with global pooling, a Weisfeiler–Lehman graph kernel, or a spectral descriptor (sorted eigenvalues of `A`, the Perron root, `tr L`) gives one vector per country-year; the Perron root has direct economic meaning because it governs total multiplier magnitude. **This framing solves the small-n problem:** OECD ICIO offers roughly 70 countries × 25 years ≈ 1,500 graphs under a common classification, turning "one graph seen 25 times" into 1,500 samples. No published graph-level ML on IO tables was found. The risk is that any convergence discovered may be *classification* convergence — a harmonisation artefact — rather than economic convergence, which must be tested against national tables in native classifications.


## 5. Economic and environmental indicator development

Indicator development is where IO has proliferated fastest and disciplined itself least: the standard indicator families are heavily collinear, so published "key sector" lists are often four names for the same eigenvector. AI offers three things here — rationalisation of the existing indicator zoo, calibration of composite indicators against observed outcomes, and discovery of new interpretable formulas. It also offers the field's most widespread current misuse pattern, the causal over-reading of feature attributions.

- **[F: high · I: medium · Proven in IO]** **ML-assisted rationalisation of the indicator zoo.** Treat backward and forward linkages, multipliers, hypothetical extraction, centralities and emission intensities as a *feature set*, then use dimension reduction or regularised supervised learning to find the minimal non-redundant subset. This is exactly what PCA-plus-clustering with explicit Spearman validation against classical linkages does in embryonic form [faridzad25]. The finding that PCA reproduces classical linkages at ρ > 0.80 is simultaneously a validation of both methods and an argument that ML adds relatively little beyond clustering structure — an honest result worth quoting. It gives a defensible answer to a real question statistical offices ask: which indicators should we publish? The limitation is that collinearity makes LASSO-style selection unstable, arbitrary among correlated features, and it would go unnoticed without bootstrapping.
- **[F: medium · I: medium-high · Adjacent]** **Supervised learning of composite indicators: learned weights instead of expert weights.** Criticality, resilience and sustainability composites normally combine normalised sub-indicators with expert or equal weights; a supervised alternative fits weights against an *observed outcome* — realised disruptions, price spikes, export-restriction events — turning an axiomatic index into a calibrated predictor, with monotonicity-constrained gradient boosting preserving the "more of a bad thing ⟹ higher risk" property policy audiences require. The criticality literature explicitly treats indicator choice and normalisation as unresolved [criticality-review; criticality-norm; criticality-focus], and at least one study replaces an assumed sub-index with a fitted probability of export restrictions [export-restrictions25]. We found no supervised composite indicator built on MRIO outputs. Two hard limits: labelled disruption events are rare and inconsistently recorded, and outcomes are endogenous — an indicator that triggers stockpiling changes the outcome it predicts, so a widely adopted learned indicator will degrade its own predictive validity.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Adjacent in volume, speculative on L]** **Explainable-AI attributions as an indicator and communication device — and the field's most widespread misuse pattern.** Shapley-value attribution and structural decomposition analysis are, at root, the same object: SDA distributes a change in a footprint over multiplicative factors, and Shapley values distribute a prediction over features with the symmetry and efficiency axioms that classical SDA lacks — indeed the Shapley/Sun decomposition is the accepted fix for SDA's factor-ordering ambiguity. So SHAP is not an alien import; it is the same axiomatic machinery, now available for nonlinear models. There is an abundant literature applying XGBoost+SHAP to emissions drivers [shap-china-county; shap-road; shap-agri; shap-construction; shap-building]. **But almost all of it uses socio-economic covariates rather than IO structure, and reports feature attributions as "drivers" implying policy levers, with minimal identification discussion.** Two disciplined positions follow, and both reviewers who examined this converged on them: (i) SHAP is appropriate for explaining an *ML component inside* an IO pipeline (why did the gap-filler impute this value?), not for explaining the IO model itself; (ii) **using SHAP where structural path analysis is available is a methodological regression dressed as an AI advance** — SPA is exact, additive and structural, while SHAP is a post-hoc approximation to a black box and is unreliable under the strong feature correlation that is normal in IO data.
- **[F: medium · I: medium-high · SPECULATION]** **Symbolic regression to discover indicator formulas.** IO is unusually well suited to symbolic regression because its primitives (`A`, `L`, `x`, `f`, `s`, row and column sums) are few, dimensioned and physically meaningful, so a dimension-aware search has a small, well-typed space. Two uses: *validation* — check whether symbolic regression rediscovers known indicators (multipliers, hypothetical extraction) from data, as a sanity test of the method; and *discovery* — search for a short formula in IO primitives that predicts realised disruption impact better than existing indices. The methods literature is mature [symreg-compare; symreg-gp; symreg-telo]; we found **no** application to input–output indicators. The output is an *equation* — inspectable, dimensionally checkable, and publishable in a statistical manual, which a GNN is not. *Speculation, with the obvious warning:* a short formula looks like a law, and publishing a symbolic-regression indicator without out-of-sample and cross-database validation would be an unusually persuasive form of spurious discovery.
- **[F: medium-high · I: high · Proven]** **Simulation-defined systemic-risk indicators and their learned surrogates.** The Economic Systemic Risk Index is computed by simulating a node's failure and propagating it with an assumed production function — an expensive, simulation-defined indicator that a supervised surrogate can approximate from structural features, turning an O(N × simulation) index into an O(N) predictor and permitting sensitivity analysis over the production-function assumption, which is currently its largest free parameter [diem21; fessina24]. Central banks and resilience agencies want exactly this number. The dependence on the production function is not incidental: results differ materially across the four specifications tested.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Adjacent, with the sharpest equity risk in the report]** **Social and decent-work footprint indicators with ML-filled satellite accounts.** Social satellite accounts (forced labour, occupational injury, informal employment, gender) are sparse and unevenly reported — a matrix-completion problem with strong side information, and one where the AI contribution sits entirely upstream of the IO algebra so accounting consistency is preserved.
