The IO side is well developed without ML, including structural path analysis of forced labour in cotton supply chains [garcia-alaminos24]; the ML side is demonstrated for environmental satellites [cullen24]; the equity critique is published [bozeman24]. **The missingness is emphatically not at random — data are absent precisely where governance is weak, i.e. exactly where true values are extreme — so standard imputation will regress high-risk cases toward the mean and *understate* risk.** This is the gravest misuse risk we identified: an imputed forced-labour intensity attached to a named country-sector can trigger import bans, contract termination and reputational harm on the basis of a model prediction. Any such indicator must carry prediction intervals and an explicit "modelled, not observed" label, and arguably should never be reported below a coarse aggregation.
- **[F: medium · I: medium-high · Adjacent, gap in IO]** **Learned circularity and material-efficiency indicators.** Circularity indicators require flows that IO tables record only partially, so ML enters as imputation of missing waste and secondary-material flows and as prediction of material-flow network change. Mass balance provides a rare hard validation constraint that a constrained model can be forced to obey. Efficient computation methods for EEIO scenario and circular-economy modelling supply the substrate [cetinay20], and ML for material-flow network change prediction exists [procedia-mfn]; we found **no** published ML-learned circularity indicator built on an IO table. Risk: imputed secondary flows can create the appearance of circularity progress that is entirely model-generated — a direct policy hazard given legally binding EU circularity targets.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent]** **Indicator uncertainty: surrogates and conformal intervals on published indicators.** Full Monte Carlo over an MRIO is expensive; a surrogate mapping perturbation parameters to indicator values, trained on a few hundred MC runs, makes uncertainty quantification cheap enough to publish routinely, and quantile regression or conformal prediction gives distribution-free intervals — attractive because MRIO error distributions are unknown and heavy-tailed [schulte24; hb-uncertainty; esr-uncertainty-review]. **The honest caveat is that the interval will be too narrow:** it propagates only the uncertainty that is modelled, and the dominant term — structural and model-choice uncertainty across databases — is not captured by perturbing one database's cells. A tight but wrong interval is worse than no interval.


## 6. Footprinting analysis

Footprinting is where AI is already deployed at industrial scale and where the gap between deployment and demonstrated accuracy is widest. The defensible uses put ML upstream of the algebra (classify, impute, allocate) and leave `s L f` untouched; the questionable ones replace the linear operator with a learned approximation and thereby forfeit the accounting consistency that gives consumption-based accounting its authority. A contrarian position that survived cross-examination: for the *linear* problem, exact fast computation beats machine learning, and unmotivated surrogate use should be read as a methodological red flag.

- **[F: high · I: medium · Emerging in IO; contested]** **ML surrogates and emulators for consumption-based accounting.** One footprint is a cheap linear solve; ten million footprints — Monte Carlo, scenario ensembles, per-household or per-product queries, interactive dashboards — is not. But because the map is *linear in `f`*, an exact "surrogate" already exists: the multiplier vector `sL`. The interesting surrogates are therefore for the *nonlinear* parts — scenarios that change `A`, price and substitution responses, or footprints conditioned on product attributes. ExioML restructures EXIOBASE 3.8.2 into ML-ready tables with a GPU toolkit and publishes a regression baseline in which **gradient-boosted trees outperform neural networks** on sectoral GHG-intensity prediction [exioml-arxiv; exioml-sdata]; exact efficient-computation methods provide the algebraic counterpart [cetinay20]; emulator practice is established in adjacent climate economics [gmd-emulator; ej-climate-emulator]. **Reconciled position: a surrogate for a linear model is usually the wrong tool, and surrogate footprints do not satisfy the accounting identities — the sum over consumers no longer equals the production-based total, which is the property that makes consumption-based accounting meaningful.**
- **[F: medium · I: low-medium for current MRIOs, high for firm-scale · Mixed]** **Fast approximation of the Leontief inverse.** Three routes: Neumann truncation `L ≈ Σ_{k≤K} A^k` (classical, with error bounded by the Perron root, and literally a *K*-layer GNN forward pass, inheriting GPU sparse kernels for free); low-rank factorisation enabling Woodbury-style inverses; and learned iterative solvers — neural preconditioners or learned Krylov acceleration, where the network only accelerates convergence of an exact iteration and the final answer stays exact to tolerance. Route three is the safest because it changes runtime, not the answer. **For a 10⁴-node MRIO a direct sparse solve is already fast and `(I − A)` is well conditioned, so the headroom for ML is genuinely small;** the case becomes strong only at firm scale (millions of nodes) or inside a loop with millions of solves [cetinay20; leontief-existence]. Truncation error is one-signed — always downward — which is a much worse property than unbiased noise and biases exactly the long, diffuse supply chains consumption-based accounting exists to capture.
- **[F: medium-high · I: medium-high · SPECULATION — a verified empty intersection]** **Learned path ranking to accelerate structural path analysis.** SPA enumerates supply-chain paths in descending contribution order; path count grows as `n^k`, so practical SPA relies on breadth-limited search with a cut-off. This is exactly where learned heuristics excel: a value network predicting the maximum achievable contribution of any extension of a partial path turns brute-force enumeration into best-first or beam search — the same idea as learned branching in mixed-integer programming. **Self-supervised training data are free:** run exhaustive SPA on small subsystems to generate labels, and validate the heuristic against exact enumeration so correctness is checkable. A targeted Crossref query for works combining structural path analysis with machine learning returned none [spa-review; spd-energyecon]. SPA is the main tool that makes footprints *actionable* — it names the specific upstream link to intervene on — and its cost is what limits its use. Risk: a learned cut-off makes the "top paths" list a function of the model, so two analysts could report different dominant supply chains for the same footprint; in due-diligence contexts a missed path is a missed liability.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent, mature]** **Hybrid LCA–IO with ML-filled process data.** Similarity-based estimation of missing unit-process data [est-similarity], decision-tree extrapolation of manufacturing-process inventories [dt-lci], and knowledge-graph embeddings for LCI extrapolation [kge-lci] all sit upstream of the hybrid algebra, so the accounting is untouched. The data-centric critique is explicitly a caution: performance is limited by data quality, representativeness and leakage, not by model class [zhao25]. Circularity risk is direct — models trained on ecoinvent and then used to extend ecoinvent entrench its biases.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent, large and growing]** **Attribute-based prediction of product footprints.** Supervised regression from product attributes (material composition, mass, dimensions, category, route, origin) to LCA/EPD impact scores, complementing IO's economy-consistent but coarse `$ → kgCO₂e` factor. The two are reconcilable in a way IO practitioners already know how to do: IO as the aggregate constraint, ML for within-sector heterogeneity, with raking to restore consistency. There is a substantial literature, including work that treats prediction uncertainty explicitly and draws out reporting implications [spc-uncertainty24], and a large-scale study of how EPD data quality bounds ML prediction [ijlca-dataquality26; sustainability-epd; springer-epd]. **A selection-bias finding that matters for regulation:** EPD datasets are non-random samples — products with EPDs are made by firms that can afford them, typically better performers — so models trained on them are *biased optimistic*, and a predicted EPD used for green-claims substantiation transfers that bias into legal exposure.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO]** **Spend-based Scope 3: NLP mapping of transactions to IO sectors.** Covered in §3 on the classification side; the footprinting point is that once the mapping is made the EEIO factor is applied unchanged, so accounting stays consistent by construction, and the method scales EEIO from research to millions of firms while providing a screening layer that tells firms where supplier-specific data collection is worth the cost [caml23; atlas; green25; nguyen-scope3]. **Two critiques must travel with it.** First, spend-based accounting is contested as an accounting basis because it rewards cheap sourcing rather than clean sourcing; automating it at scale entrenches that incentive without addressing the critique. Second, misclassification is *correlated*, not random — classifiers trained on English, US-centric descriptions systematically mis-map non-US suppliers — so sector totals are biased, not merely noisy, and errors do not cancel.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO]** **Household footprints: supervised prediction and consumption-archetype clustering.** Two distinct roles, both downstream of `s L f` applied to expenditure vectors so the accounting is untouched: learn `footprint ← socio-demographics` to extend estimates to surveys lacking expenditure detail or to small areas; and cluster households in footprint-*composition* space to find lifestyle types, which is far more policy-useful than deciles because it separates *how* people emit from how much [erl-2stage-clustering; energypolicy-china-hcf; esr-microdata]. Distributional carbon policy depends on exactly these estimates. Two error sources are routinely unreported: COICOP→IO-sector concordance, and price heterogeneity — rich households' higher spend partly reflects higher prices rather than more physical consumption, systematically overstating their footprints, while top-coding cuts the other way. The framing risk is real: archetype narratives readily become "high-emitting lifestyles" language that shifts attention from structural drivers, and socio-demographic prediction risks proxy discrimination if used for targeting.
- **[F: medium · I: medium-high · Single instance in IO]** **GNN-based footprint propagation.** Rather than surrogating the *output* of `sLf`, embed the propagation itself in a GNN whose message function generalises the technical coefficient, with emission intensity as a node feature propagating upstream and attention weights representing an attribute-conditioned input mix. This is the right generalisation for sectors with high internal heterogeneity — construction is the canonical case, where two buildings in the same IO sector can differ by an order of magnitude — and is where the one direct GNN⊕IO application sits [buildenv26; sftr26]. Requires paired attribute-and-footprint data that exist for very few sectors, and is hard to validate against anything except the IO model it generalises.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent, with a circularity warning]** **Corporate footprint prediction benchmarked against EEIO.** Predict a firm's Scope 1/2/3 from financial and structural attributes, using EEIO-derived sector intensities as feature, prior or benchmark, with IO supplying the *aggregate consistency constraint* — a rare case where IO disciplines an ML model rather than the reverse [nguyen-scope3; hbs-scope3; exioml-sdata; applied-intel-survey]. **The circularity here is severe and is already forming in the ESG data industry:** many "ML-predicted" Scope 3 figures are trained on targets themselves generated from EEIO factors, so agreement with EEIO is not validation. Predicted firm emissions feed investment screening and index construction, and error is systematic by sector and geography, so capital allocation is systematically distorted by an opaque commercial model with essentially no external validation.
- **[F: medium-high · I: medium-high · Adjacent / partially in IO]** **Sub-national and urban footprint downscaling.** ML learns the allocation function instead of assuming proportionality, with a constrained final step (raking/IPF) restoring consistency with the national total — IO's own balancing logic used to keep the ML honest [zheng19; energy-buildings-retrofit; dc-emissions-ml]. Almost never validated, because sub-national ground truth is what is missing in the first place. City rankings derived from downscaled model output are politically consequential and essentially unfalsifiable; the choice of proxy can determine the ranking.


## 7. Shock analysis, and input–output as an optimisation problem

Shock analysis is where the linear model most obviously fails — a fixed-coefficient Leontief system has no mechanism to represent scarcity — and therefore where reformulating IO as constrained optimisation, and then bringing modern optimisation and learning machinery to bear, has the clearest payoff. This section contains the single strongest demonstrated AI contribution in the whole report (differentiable calibration) and the single most important untried transfer (hard accounting constraints inside learned components).

- **[F: high (optimisation) / medium (ML integration) · I: high · Proven in IO]** **Input–output as constrained optimisation.** `x = Ax + f` becomes a linear or convex program once capacity constraints `x ≤ x̄`, non-negativity and an objective (minimise unmet final demand, maximise value added) are added; Ghosh and mixed endogenous/exogenous models are the dual and partial-closure variants. This is the *right* mathematical home for shock analysis. Simultaneous supply and demand constraints in IO networks were formalised for COVID-19 in Germany, Italy and Spain, and a dynamic IO model with rationing was developed and then used to produce genuine out-of-sample forecasts [pichler-esr22; pichler-jedc22; pichler-arxiv]; constrained-optimisation MRIO construction under scenarios is also being done [eartharxiv-prospective]. **The dual variables are shadow prices — bottleneck indicators obtained for free — and constraints guarantee accounting consistency by construction, which no pure ML approach does.** The reading discipline: rationing rules are assumptions with large effects, and a planner-optimal allocation is not what a decentralised economy does, so LP results are bounds, not forecasts. Assuming perfect reallocation systematically *understates* shock impact, the opposite bias to fixed-coefficient Leontief.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO — the field's best out-of-sample shock validation]** **Survey-elicited *input criticality* as the learnable object that fixes the Leontief rigidity, and the out-of-sample test that proves it matters.** An independent replication of the Pichler-type dynamic input–output model on Belgian data for 2020–2021, validated against four independent economic time series, reports a value-weighted mean absolute deviation of **4.69%** and reproduces GDP, revenue and employment contractions both by activity and in aggregate [alleman23; pichler-jedc22; pichler-arxiv]. Three findings from it should discipline this whole section. **First**, relaxing strict Leontief helps, but the *degree* of relaxation barely does: the "half-critical" variant is best, yet strongly-critical, weakly-critical and linear variants differ by only 0.04–0.11% — so the enormous methodological literature on production-function form is, at this resolution, arguing about the fourth decimal place. **Second**, the useful information is not in the functional form but in the **criticality labels** — an analyst survey classifying each input to each sector as critical, important or non-critical, which is what stops the model concluding that restaurant closures halt construction. That label matrix is a small, expensive, expert-elicited object of exactly the kind an LLM over technical documentation, procurement records and industry standards could plausibly extend to sectors and countries where no survey exists, and it is a far better AI target than the production function itself. **Third**, the model systematically **overestimates B2B demand reduction**, by up to 13% on average during the first lockdown, because it assumes instantaneous delivery while real firms complete standing orders — an inventory-and-contract effect that is a natural target for a learned delay kernel. *Our assessment:* extending criticality matrices by machine learning, and validating the extension against this Belgian benchmark, is one of the highest-value and most immediately executable items in this report.
- **[F: medium-high · I: high · Proven in IO/adjacent — the benchmark item]** **Differentiable calibration of ARIO-type disaster IO models.** ARIO-class models are built directly on Leontief technology with capacity constraints, inventories and rationing rules; implementing the whole simulator in an autodiff framework makes gradients with respect to thousands of firm-level parameters available, replacing rejection-sampling calibration with gradient-based variational inference. The published result — lower loss after 300 model evaluations than approximate Bayesian computation achieved after 30,000, speed-ups over three orders of magnitude, uncertainty quantification retained — is on 1,000-firm synthetic networks [hamid25]. **This is the cleanest example in the report of modern AI *infrastructure* (not a model) transforming IO practice, because the IO model is unchanged; only its computational representation changes.** Gradients also give exact sensitivities for free, which classical IO obtains only by finite differences. Missing step: validation against real disaster outcomes. Watch item: discrete rules (a firm halts) are non-differentiable and need smoothing, which may remove exactly the cascade nonlinearity of interest.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent method, SPECULATIVE in IO — the highest-value transfer]** **Hard accounting constraints embedded in neural components.** The defining property of IO data is a set of hard linear equalities: row sums = column sums = total output; intermediate + value added = intermediate + final demand; satellite accounts reconcile to national totals. **A network that merely penalises violations in the loss will violate them at inference.** The correct construction projects the output onto the affine feasible set with a fixed linear projection layer that guarantees `Cŷ = d` *exactly*, at negligible cost and with no loss of expressiveness inside the constraint set [kkthpinn; kkthpinn-arxiv; pinn-hard-nonlinear; siam-hard-pinn]. In IO terms this is a learned analogue of RAS/GRAS embedded as a differentiable layer, and it drops unchanged into GNN footprint models, imputation models, surrogate models and generative table models. We found **no** application to input–output accounting identities. **Two further points from the physics-informed literature transfer directly and are load-bearing:** soft penalties leave *local* balance violations even when global balance looks satisfied [pinn-mass-balance] — which maps exactly onto a table that balances in aggregate while individual cells are nonsense; and a perfectly balanced but wrong table is *more* dangerous than an obviously unbalanced one, because balance is the community's main sniff test and enforcing it removes a diagnostic.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Adjacent, direct and strong]** **Learning production functions and substitution elasticities from network data.** The Leontief-vs-CES-vs-linear choice dominates shock results yet is almost always assumed rather than estimated. Temporal GNNs with hyperedge message passing and an explicit inventory module learn hidden production functions — which inputs make which outputs, i.e. a bill of materials — from transaction-level supply-chain data, reporting 6–50% improvement over baselines on production-function inference and 11–62% on future transaction prediction, with an open simulator released alongside [chang25]. **This replaces the field's largest free assumption with an estimate.** Both the systemic-risk and the dynamic-IO literatures show results move materially with the production-function choice [diem21; pichler-jedc22]. Identification is the hard part: observed input mixes reflect both technology and price/availability, so a learned aggregator may confound substitution with rationing, and a function estimated in normal times may not hold in the crisis it will be used for.
- **[F: medium-high · I: medium-high · Adjacent]** **ML surrogates for expensive firm-level cascade simulations.** GNN surrogates are the natural architecture because the input is a graph, the output is a node- or graph-level quantity, and the simulator's own dynamics *are* message passing, so the inductive bias matches exactly. Training data are generated by the simulator itself, so — unusually in this report — genuine validation is available, since surrogate error can be measured against the simulator [lamperti18; sim-opt-scm; sim-ml-scm; gmd-emulator]. Note the alternative: differentiable simulation often removes the need for a surrogate entirely. Tail behaviour, the regime policymakers care about, is exactly where surrogate error is largest.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Proven in a directly comparable setting]** **Random-forest emulation of a spatial agent-based economy to make policy comparison exhaustive rather than illustrative.** 11,076 real runs of a regional agent-based model across 46 Brazilian metropolitan regions are used to train a random-forest emulator, which then produces **one million** emulated policy runs, allowing competing policies to be ranked region by region instead of tested on a handful of hand-picked configurations [furtado22]. The transferable lesson for IO is not the ABM but the *ratio*: the value of emulation lies less in speed than in changing what question can be asked — from "does this policy work?" to "for which regions, under which parameter combinations, does it work?", which is the scenario-discovery framing recommended below. **Two limits carry directly across:** an emulator can only interpolate within the design that generated it, so the million runs contain no more information than the 11,076, only more resolution; and random forests extrapolate poorly and flatten at the design boundary, which is precisely where extreme scenarios live.
- **[F: medium · I: medium-high · Adjacent; speculative economy-wide]** **Reinforcement learning for adaptive rationing and rerouting.** Post-shock allocation is a sequential decision problem under uncertainty — the textbook RL setting — and RL for supply-chain recovery is established in operations research [ijpr-rl-recovery; tre-adaptive-resilience; ssrn-marl-scm]. Applied to an IO-calibrated economy-wide network: none found. **The most defensible use is diagnostic rather than prescriptive:** comparing a learned adaptive policy against the fixed heuristics that ARIO-class models hard-code (proportional rationing, priority rules) quantifies how much of a modelled shock impact is an artefact of the rationing assumption, bracketing impacts between rigid-Leontief (pessimistic) and RL-optimal (optimistic). The distributional consequences of learned rationing — which regions and sectors get cut — are embedded in the reward function and are easily hidden.
- **[F: medium-high · I: high · Adjacent, with an elegant special case]** **Identification of critical links and fragile edges.** Exhaustive edge-removal on an MRIO is O(n²) shock simulations and infeasible on firm networks; a learned edge-scoring function ranks edges in one pass. **The elegant special case: in a differentiable shock model, `∂(output loss)/∂A_ij` is a first-order criticality score computed exactly at the cost of one backward pass — no learning required** [hamid25]. Hypergraph and explainable systemic-risk GNNs are appearing [hypergnn; attention-risk-gnn]. First-order gradients miss threshold and cascade nonlinearities, which are the essence of criticality. **Publishing named critical links is strategically sensitive — it is a target list as much as a resilience list** — and at MRIO aggregation a "critical link" may be an artefact of the sector classification.
- **[F: medium · I: medium · Proven model, speculative ML]** **Inoperability input–output models with ML-estimated parameters.** IIM recasts the Leontief system in terms of inoperability, `q = A*q + c*`, and DIIM adds recovery dynamics; both need recovery-rate parameters that are essentially unknown and set by expert judgement [haimes-jiang05; ress-diim25; springer-diim; plos-outage23]. ML's role is parameter estimation from observed outage and recovery data, and surrogate modelling of the combinatorially growing multi-state dynamic version. The inoperability interpretation of technical coefficients is itself contested — a share of monetary flows is not a share of functional dependency — and fitted parameters would carry the biases of the recorded event sample (large, well-documented, rich-country events).
- **[F: medium · I: high · Proven in IO, strong and mature]** **Agent-based and network-cascade models calibrated on IO data.** Firm-level ABMs taking technology from IO tables (or from reconstructed networks) simulate cascades that sector-level IO cannot represent — inventories, capacity, bankruptcy, rewiring — while remaining nationally consistent by construction, which pure micro-founded ABMs are not [inoue-todo19; inoue-todo-plos; plos-japan-trade; arxiv-cross-country; arxiv-decarb-firm]. AI contributes at three points: network reconstruction, calibration, and surrogate-based exploration. Model-structure uncertainty dominates and is rarely quantified, and ABM outputs are often reported without uncertainty while feeding compensation and insurance decisions.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO — one of the few falsifiable cases]** **Data-driven shock vectors feeding IO propagation.** The propagation machinery is well established; the hard part is estimating the *initial* shock — which occupations can work remotely, which plants closed, which routes stopped. This is where ML and NLP genuinely add value: classifying occupations by remote-workability from task descriptions, nowcasting sectoral activity from alternative data, extracting disruption events from news. The COVID programme built occupation- and industry-level supply and demand shocks from detailed task and consumption data and propagated them through a dynamic IO model, first for scenarios and then as genuine published *forecasts* that could be scored against realised outcomes [pichler-arxiv; pichler-jedc22; pichler-esr22]. **This is one of very few cases in which IO-based models produced falsifiable, scoreable predictions, and the field should treat it as a template rather than an episode.** The caveat is that calibration rested on a single, extreme, unusually observable shock.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Adjacent at firm level]** **Transition-scenario optimisation on production networks.** Choosing which nodes to close or transform, subject to constraints on unemployment and output loss, is a combinatorial optimisation problem on the production network, demonstrated at national firm level [arxiv-decarb-firm] and approached from the table-construction side at MRIO scale [eartharxiv-prospective]. It makes the emissions/employment trade-off explicit and quantitative. **The central unsolved tension:** a fixed `A` is a poor basis for scenarios that by definition change technology, and employment estimates from fixed labour coefficients are known to be unreliable over the horizons involved — while the outputs name firms and sectors to close, which is maximally politically salient.
- **[F: medium · I: medium · SPECULATION]** **Learning the propagation direction: Ghosh, Leontief and mixed models in one architecture.** A directed GNN with two message directions represents upstream (Leontief) and downstream (Ghosh) propagation simultaneously and could *learn* which dominates for a given shock — a data-driven engagement with a debate that has run in IO for forty years — with mixed endogenous/exogenous models corresponding to masking messages on a subset of nodes [miller-supply-side; an24]. **The risk is a category error:** the Ghosh model's economic interpretation is contested on theoretical grounds (implausible price behaviour), and learning direction weights measures predictive value, it does not vindicate the theory.


## 8. Forward-looking analysis: nowcasting, coefficient projection, and scenario modelling

The IO field's chronic weakness is timeliness — global tables lag by three to five years — and its chronic ambition is to say something about the future. AI attacks the first directly and the second only indirectly, by making inputs cheaper and ensembles larger. The highest expected-value single item we identified anywhere in this report sits here and is unbuilt: a machine-learning nowcaster for consumption-based footprints. The most dangerous single item also sits here: opaque emulators of integrated assessment models.

- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent]** **Nowcasting the final-demand vector and sectoral output that drive the model.** Dynamic factor models augmented with ML on ragged-edge, mixed-frequency indicator panels is the classical nowcasting problem, and the tooling is commoditised [imf-nowcast22; ijf-nowcast; arxiv-monthly-gdp; comp-econ-gdp]. Every "current-year" IO application silently assumes a stale `y`. **Two IO-specific complications that the macro literature does not face:** nowcasting at IO product detail multiplies targets by 100–200 and there is no reason to think indicator panels are informative for most narrow products, so a hierarchical reconciliation layer is mandatory; and a nowcast `y` that is wrong in *composition* rather than level produces footprint errors that look precise and are invisible because they are buried before the inversion.
- **[F: high · I: medium · Adjacent / speculative]** **Time-series foundation models applied zero-shot to the thousands of series inside an MRIO.** Pretrained probabilistic sequence models tokenise series by scaling and quantisation and achieve competitive zero-shot accuracy on unseen datasets [chronos; tsfm-benchmark]. The structural fit is real — MRIO updating requires forecasting a very large number of short, heterogeneous series where fitting a bespoke model per series is infeasible — and the outputs come with calibrated intervals usable as priors for a Bayesian balancing step. **Impact is capped because zero-shot output violates every accounting identity in the system, and the reconciliation step is where the real difficulty sits.** Pretraining corpora are dominated by retail, energy-demand, web-traffic and finance data whose inductive bias may be badly matched to slow-moving structural coefficients, and annual series of length ~25 sit at the bottom of the useful range.
- **[F: medium-high · I: high · Adjacent]** **AIS and satellite nowcasting of the trade block.** Vessel-tracking data give near-census coverage of seaborne trade at daily frequency — a genuinely new information set rather than a better estimator on old data. Monthly country-level trade estimates releasable within seven working days have been produced and validated across a deliberately diverse country set [imf-portwatch; imf-ais-poc; imf-space]. Trade lag is a principal driver of MRIO lag, and trade is the channel through which consumption-based accounting differs from territorial accounting at all. **The limitation is precisely where footprints are most sensitive:** seaborne only (missing pipelines, air freight, land trade and all services), with cargo composition *inferred* rather than observed, so product detail is weakest exactly where emission intensity varies most. Commercial AIS licensing also creates reproducibility and access asymmetries.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Partially proven in IO]** **Learned A-matrix updating versus RAS.** Instead of imposing biproportional structure, train a model to map (previous table, current row/column totals, auxiliary indicators) → current table, supervised by the historical sequence of benchmark tables. RAS is a constraint-satisfaction method with no learning: it reproduces whatever biproportional drift the totals imply and cannot use covariates such as energy prices, technology deployment or firm surveys. The demonstrated instances are the Japanese regional coefficient papers plus their balancing hybrid [mixup24; dl-balance26], with Bayesian shrinkage updating under a benchmark LASSO prior as the nearest classical relative — a multiplier matrix updated subject to final-demand and total-output constraints, with a sparsity-inducing prior handling the fact that unknowns vastly outnumber observations, demonstrated on a 196×196 US table for 2012 [bayes-lasso; esr-updating15]. The peer reports were unanimous in treating this as the classical benchmark that any learned updater must beat, and we agree: it already delivers regularised, uncertainty-bearing updates without a neural network. **The binding constraint is supervision, not compute:** there are only a handful of true benchmark tables per country. And the circularity is acute — benchmark tables are themselves partly RAS-derived, so a learned updater may be learning to imitate RAS rather than reality.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Embryonic in IO, otherwise speculative]** **Bayesian dynamic and state-space models of coefficient evolution.** Treat the coefficient matrix as a high-dimensional latent state with a learned transition: shrinkage priors toward the last benchmark, hierarchical pooling across countries so data-rich economies inform data-poor ones, variational or particle filtering for the non-Gaussian, non-negativity-constrained case. Coefficients are persistent, sparse in their changes (most cells barely move; a few move a lot) and observed with error — the canonical setting for shrinkage plus state space. It would deliver *distributions* over future coefficients, which is what scenario work needs and never has. **This is where the Lucas critique bites hardest:** coefficients projected from a historical regime are invalid under a policy that changes relative prices, and a carbon price is precisely such a policy. A well-calibrated-looking predictive distribution over `A` conveys an authority the identification does not support, and users will read the intervals as policy uncertainty when they are historical-drift uncertainty.
- **[F: medium-high · I: medium-high · SPECULATION — a clean, tractable gap]** **Tensor factorisation of the MRIO time series.** CP/Tucker or tensor-train decomposition of the (sector × sector × region × year) object, forecasting ~50 latent factors instead of 10⁸ cells, with latent-ODE variants handling irregular benchmark spacing [temporal-tensor-ode; tensor-review]. No application to input–output tables was found by any reviewer. Compression, denoising, nowcasting and detection of database-revision artefacts all fall out of the same construction. **A prior that must be tested rather than assumed:** low-rank structure may be an artefact of the compilation methods (RAS, proportionality assumptions) rather than of the economy, and testing it against benchmark-year-only data is a well-posed and publishable question in itself.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO — the single most actionable item]** **ML gap-filling of the environmental extension.** Covered in §1; restated here because timeliness and coverage are the same problem viewed from two ends. The operational finding — that missingness has *structure* (missing time step, missing emitter, missing emitter with novel properties are three different statistical problems) and that method choice should follow the gap type — is what makes this immediately deployable [cullen24].
- **[F: high · I: high · SPECULATION — the highest expected-value single item]** **Nowcasting consumption-based footprints to cut the three-to-five-year MRIO lag.** Two designs: *structural*, nowcasting `F`, `A`, `y` and the trade block separately and then running the standard algebra; or *reduced-form*, learning the map from fast current-year indicators (territorial emissions, trade volumes, energy prices, GDP composition, near-real-time activity data) directly onto the footprint that is later published, trained on 20+ years of EXIOBASE/GLORIA/ICIO footprint series. The reduced-form variant is exactly where gradient boosting and shrinkage estimators excel and admits a proper backtest (train on t ≤ T, predict T+1…T+4) yielding calibrated prediction intervals — the honest way to state footprint timeliness uncertainty. **We could find no published ML footprint nowcaster, and the incumbent is weak and known to be weak:** the standard practice is to extrapolate the last available footprint by territorial growth [gcb2025; owid-cba]. Policy demand is acute, since national consumption-based targets and border-adjustment-adjacent reporting are being designed against data three to five years stale. **The risk is proportionate to the value:** a nowcast footprint carries the authority of the underlying MRIO without its already-contested empirical basis, and if used for target compliance or border pricing it becomes a legally consequential number produced by an opaque model. Provenance and revision policy must be fixed *before* deployment, not after. *One reviewer's caution we endorse: the claim that no such nowcaster exists rests on a bounded search and should be verified against Scopus/Web of Science before it is asserted in print.*
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Adjacent]** **High-frequency density nowcasts of sub-national emissions.** Panel mixed-data sampling linking monthly indicators to lower-frequency targets, combined with panel quantile regression to deliver a *distribution* rather than a point — directly usable for probabilistic budget-compliance statements [arxiv-density-co2; umidas-lstm; midas-china-power]. **That the published work chose MIDAS over deep learning is itself informative**, and MIDAS is the fair, transparent baseline any neural approach must beat. Backtest windows that exclude COVID and the 2022 energy shock, and an electricity→emissions mapping destabilised by grid decarbonisation — precisely the trend of interest — are the standing limits.
- **[F: medium-high · I: high · Adjacent]** **Near-real-time activity data as the driver of a "fast MRIO".** Daily global CO₂ from fossil fuel and cement, with regional and city versions, provides the fastest-moving component of the footprint at near-observational quality and a natural hard constraint for any nowcast MRIO — territorial totals must reconcile [carbon-monitor; carbon-monitor-covid; carbon-monitor-europe; carbon-monitor-cities]. Sectoral resolution is coarse (six sectors versus 160–200 IO sectors) and the concordance to IO sectors is itself a consequential modelling choice. Daily global CO₂ is precise in *timing*, not in *level*; propagating it into footprints implies an accuracy the underlying activity proxies do not have.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Proven as an official product]** **Nowcasting GVC and trade-in-value-added indicators.** The OECD publishes TiVA nowcast estimates and documents the methodology [oecd-tiva-nowcast; oecd-tiva-data; oecd-icio-2025]; whether the production method currently uses ML we could not establish. **Two points follow.** First, institutional appetite for nowcast IO-derived indicators demonstrably exists, and the published revision history is a public benchmark any ML method must beat — which is a rare gift in this field. Second, nowcasting an *indicator* is much easier than nowcasting the *table*, and indicator-level nowcasts cannot be reused for a different question such as footprints.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Adjacent / speculative]** **Placing genuinely new technologies into IO classifications.** Two routes: relatedness and economic-complexity ML over the country–product network to forecast which activities a location will plausibly add [albora23; mc-complexity; firm-relatedness; ecomplexity-data], and text/patent mining plus LLM-assisted concordance to classify technology descriptions into IO product codes and generate candidate cost breakdowns [patent-ml-ev]. **The inability to represent genuinely new sectors is arguably the deepest structural weakness of forward-looking IO** — an IO table cannot represent a technology that has no historical cost structure, and every net-zero scenario is dominated by such technologies. Relatedness models are trained on historical diversification and are therefore structurally conservative about discontinuities: they will systematically under-predict the technologies that matter most. **This is the entry where LLM misuse would be least visible and most consequential:** a hallucinated cost column for "green hydrogen" inserted into an MRIO produces multipliers that will be quoted as evidence.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent]** **Probabilistic experience-curve forecasts to endogenise technological change.** Not deep learning but statistical learning with methodological discipline: fit probabilistic Wright's-law curves to long technology cost series, validate by genuine out-of-sample hindcasting, and propagate the resulting distributions. The published result — that empirically validated probabilistic forecasts imply far faster and cheaper transitions than the deterministic cost assumptions embedded in most integrated assessment models — has direct implications for any IO-based scenario borrowing IAM cost trajectories [way22]. **The hindcasting protocol is the transferable asset: the IO field should copy it wholesale for coefficient projection.** Experience curves are reduced-form correlations with known endogeneity and apply to modular, mass-manufactured technologies, failing for lumpy infrastructure — which is exactly where IO representation is hardest.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent — and the most dangerous item in this section]** **ML emulators of integrated assessment models.** Trained on the published AR6 scenario ensemble across 95 model families, XGBoost and LSTM emulators both reach R² = 0.97 and generate 2,000 scenarios in 50 seconds against hours for the underlying models; a temporal fusion transformer *underperformed* both [ml-iam; ar6-zenodo; ar6-explorer]. A GCAM-specific deep-learning emulator for the energy sector also exists [gcam-emulator]. This is the enabling technology for large IO scenario ensembles: it turns scenario analysis from a handful of runs into a sampling problem, which unlocks uncertainty propagation, sensitivity analysis, scenario discovery and optimisation on top of an IO layer. **And it carries the most dangerous limitation in the report.** An emulator trained on a scenario database learns the *manifold of scenarios modellers chose to run*, not the physics or economics of the system. AR6 is a convenience sample shaped by project calls, model availability and publication incentives — small-N in the sense that matters (few genuinely independent structural assumptions) however many thousands of rows it contains — and extrapolation beyond the convex hull of submitted scenarios is unjustified and undetectable from R². **This is assumption-laundering in its purest form:** an IAM scenario carries a documented model, provenance and caveats; an emulator output carries none of that while looking like the same object. Mandatory mitigations: publish the emulator's training-domain boundary, refuse to extrapolate outside it, and pair every emulator with an inter-model attribution analysis.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Adjacent]** **Reduced-form structural emulators as the transparent baseline.** Fitting parametric marginal-abatement-cost curves to IAM output by gas, region and technology portfolio, then coupling to a simple climate model, reproduces a majority of original IAM CO₂ outcomes across ten models [emiam]. We include it deliberately: **it is the interpretable baseline that neural emulators must beat**, every coefficient has meaning, failure modes are visible, and for IO coupling MAC curves are directly usable to endogenise abatement in a price-side model. Its weaknesses — static reduced forms discarding path dependence and capital vintages, poor fit in deep-decarbonisation regions — are well understood, which is exactly the property a reference point needs.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent]** **ML surrogates of CGE and energy-system models for systematic sensitivity analysis.** Design of experiments over parameter space, then a supervised surrogate mapping parameters to outcomes, with global sensitivity indices computed on the surrogate — converting "we ran three scenarios" into "we characterised the response surface" [gtap-ml-sensitivity; energyplan-surrogate; capex-uq; deep-surrogate-energy]. CGE elasticity assumptions are both consequential and poorly constrained, and systematic sensitivity analysis is almost never done because runs are expensive. **Two structural cautions.** Sensitivity over *parameters* says nothing about sensitivity to *structure*, which is where the real uncertainty lives; and energy-system optima are discontinuous in inputs because technologies switch in and out at threshold prices, so smooth surrogates smear exactly the transitions that matter, biasing IO-derived impact estimates toward gradualism.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Adjacent, gap in IO]** **Active learning and Bayesian optimisation for scenario-space design.** Fit a probabilistic surrogate, then choose the next expensive run to maximise information gain or locate a decision-relevant boundary [entropy-bal; deepgp-al; self-learning-surrogate]. **This addresses the small-N scenario-ensemble problem the right way — not by running more scenarios but by running better-chosen ones** — and makes the design of a scenario set an explicit, auditable optimisation rather than a negotiation. Feasibility is limited less by algorithms than by institutions: scenario ensembles are produced by distributed, independently funded modelling teams on multi-year cycles, which is nearly the opposite of a tight active-learning loop. And algorithmically chosen scenarios lack the stakeholder legitimacy that makes SSPs usable in assessment processes.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent, gap in IO — the most concrete near-term programme]** **Scenario discovery on IO-based ensembles.** Rule-extraction and box-finding (PRIM, CART, bagged random boxes) produce human-readable conditions — "targets are missed whenever import intensity of X exceeds Y *and* electrification is slower than Z" — which survive translation to policy far better than a coefficient table, and which are explicitly robustness-oriented rather than prediction-oriented, suiting IO's weak predictive claims [kwakkel16; scenario-discovery-rules *(citation unverified)*]. **We found no scenario-discovery study on an MRIO ensemble; combining emulation with scenario discovery is the most concrete near-term research programme identified in this report.** Validity is entirely contingent on the ensemble spanning the relevant uncertainty — the small-N problem inherited from the emulator — and PRIM boxes are sensitive to peel/paste parameters and to the outcome threshold, both analyst choices that materially change the story.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent]** **ML attribution of inter-model differences — the antidote to emulator laundering.** Applying diagnostics, ML and econometrics to a scenario ensemble's inputs and outputs decomposes where models disagree and why: energy-demand variables account for the largest input discrepancies across mainstream IAMs, and decarbonisation-pathway differences stem primarily from technological inertia, investment-cost and maturity assumptions [duan26; ar6-emulator-change]. **This tells an IO practitioner which borrowed assumption is driving their result before they publish it, and provides a defensible basis for choosing or weighting scenarios rather than averaging them.** Models are not independent (shared data, teams, calibration targets), so variance decomposition overstates effective sample size.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven adjacent, directly transferable]** **Prospective databases generated from IAM scenarios.** Rule-based automated transformation aligning process-LCA inventories to IAM outputs for 2005–2100 across six IAMs, updating electricity, heat, steel, cement and transport markets and efficiencies, is mature open-source infrastructure [premise; premise-tiam]; the MRIO analogues exist but are hand-built [jes-exogenous-mrio; est-ce-scenario]. It makes forward technology matrices reproducible and version-controlled, with assumptions traceable to a named model and scenario. **A critical and under-discussed flaw: only the mapped sectors change.** Everything else is frozen at present-day technology, which for a 2050 database systematically biases prospective footprints toward "today's economy with clean electricity", and there is no uncertainty propagation. The gap to a prospective-EXIOBASE pipeline is engineering and concordance work, not research risk.
- **[F: high (concordance) · I: high · Proven coupling, speculative ML layer]** **Energy-system and macro-econometric coupling with ML-assisted concordance.** Make-and-use-based coupling of an energy-system model with an MRIO is published and is methodologically the correct way to introduce new technologies — as new *activities* producing existing *products* — which sidesteps part of the new-sector problem [esm-mrio-coupling *(citation unverified)*; e3me-manual; e3me-jrc]. **The ML opportunity is unglamorous and, we judge, the single largest labour bottleneck in practice: the concordance and reconciliation layer.** Automating it would cut months of work per coupling and make couplings reproducible and updatable. The risk is LLM-generated concordances that look plausible and are wrong in a handful of high-multiplier cells, and couplings presented as integrated models when they are one-directional.
- **[F: medium · I: medium · Adjacent, IO-untried]** **Reinforcement learning for policy-pathway search.** An IO model is a fast, deterministic, differentiable environment — ideal for RL in a technical sense — and there are three established adjacent strands: two-level multi-agent RL for tax-policy design [ai-economist], multi-agent RL for climate-negotiation protocols [rice-n], and RL over socio-environmental simulations and climate surrogates [eds-rl-climate; cicero-surrogate], with central-bank-adjacent reviews of deep RL in macroeconomics [imf-drl]. No RL over an IO-based model was found. **We deliberately rate impact only medium, and the reason is the Lucas critique in its sharpest form:** an RL agent optimising against a fixed-coefficient IO model will discover that it can drive impacts to zero by reallocating final demand toward low-intensity sectors, because nothing in the model prevents it. Reward specification determines the answer and is where all the normative content hides. RL-derived "optimal" carbon-price paths carry an aura of optimisation rigour that is inherited entirely from the model, not the algorithm.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent, at the highest level of the literature]** **Causal machine learning for ex-post policy evaluation feeding IO parameters.** Double/debiased ML uses flexible learners for high-dimensional nuisance functions with cross-fitting and Neyman-orthogonal scores so that regularisation bias does not contaminate the causal parameter. Screening ~1,500 climate policies by combining ML with break detection finds that only a small number produced large emission reductions and that policy *mixes* matter more than single instruments [stechemesser24]; combining economic theory with ML-estimated counterfactuals finds the UK Carbon Price Support lowered emissions by 6.2% at an average cost of €18/tonne, **with effectiveness depending on relative fuel prices** — exactly the state-dependence IO assumes away [abrell22; natcomms-carbon-pricing-meta; nature-cc-policy-interactions]. This supplies externally estimated effect sizes that could discipline IO scenario assumptions instead of assumed elasticities. **The essential caveat, which the IO literature is at risk of eliding:** DML solves a bias-from-regularisation problem; it does *not* solve identification. And the distinction between evaluating a policy that happened and validating a counterfactual that never could is the single most important boundary in this section — an IO comparative-static exercise ("remove sector X's demand") has no observational counterpart at all. Importing causal-ML vocabulary without importing a causal design would lend false credibility to what remains a scenario comparison.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Adjacent, unresolved]** **Causal ML for carbon leakage and border adjustment.** This is the headline forward-looking question EE-MRIO is most used for, and **the most important unresolved tension between IO practice and causal inference: MRIO answers a causal question (does unilateral policy relocate emissions?) with accounting decompositions, which cannot identify causation** [jeem-ets-leakage; edas-cbam; climate-policy-leakage]. A design-based estimate would replace the standard narrative (embodied imports rose, therefore leakage) and would give IO models an estimated leakage rate rather than an elasticity assumption. Two limits are severe: leakage is a general-equilibrium phenomenon and partial-equilibrium designs miss the international price channel that theory says dominates; and ex-post evidence covers a low-carbon-price, free-allocation era that may not extrapolate to the regime border adjustment anticipates — a textbook external-validity failure.
- **[F: low-medium · I: medium · SPECULATION, and we advise against it]** **Causal discovery / DAG structure learning on IO panels.** We list this for completeness and rate it lowest-feasibility deliberately. The motivation is real — an IO coefficient records that sector *j* bought from sector *i*, which is a flow, not a demonstration that *i*'s output responds causally to *j*. But causal discovery needs many observations relative to variables and IO panels have ~25 annual observations against thousands of variables; faithfulness and causal sufficiency are violated by construction (prices, technology and policy are unobserved common causes of nearly every pair of sectors); annual simultaneity defeats lag-based identification; and contemporaneous accounting identities create deterministic relationships that break conditional-independence testing outright [causal-climate; causal-survey; timegraph]. **Any published "causal graph over sectors" should be treated with strong scepticism as a likely artefact of the accounting identities used to construct the data.**


---


# Part C — Coupling and integration


## 9. Coupling IO data and models with other modelling approaches

Coupling IO to anything else — a process-LCA database, an energy-system model, a physical stock model, an agent-based model — reduces to three chores: concordance construction, unit and boundary reconciliation with double counting at the seam, and gap filling. All three are pattern-matching and imputation problems with weak supervision, which is why ML and now LLMs land here first, and why the risk profile is dominated by silent, plausible-looking errors rather than loud failures. The most consequential empty space in the whole report is here: **no ML has been applied anywhere to the double-counting correction, which is the methodological heart of hybridisation.**

- **[F: medium · I: high · Proven hybridisation, speculative ML layer]** **Full-database process↔MRIO hybridisation with a learned correspondence and correction layer.** Automated hybridisation of a complete process-LCA database with a complete MRIO exists as open software, adding inputs deemed missing on the basis of the average usage of such services by the corresponding MRIO sector, and citing four peer-reviewed publications on streamlined hybridisation, double-counting correction, coverage and truncation [pylcaio; agez20; jakobs24 *(citation unverified)*]. **Its documentation contains no mention of automation or ML for the mapping step, which is a precise statement of the gap.** Two distinct AI opportunities: embedding-based generation of the ecoinvent↔EXIOBASE correspondence (mature, §3), and learned estimation of the **double-counting correction factors** — how much of an IO sector's input bundle is already represented in the process inventory — which is currently handled by crude heuristics across thousands of process–sector pairs. The second is the harder and more valuable: an ML-estimated correction biased low inflates footprints, biased high deflates them, and neither is visible in the output. Note honestly that double counting is a *definitional* problem as much as a statistical one — an estimator can only learn a convention, not the truth.
- **[F: medium · I: medium-high · Adjacent deterministic, speculative AI]** **Automating path-exchange hybridisation and system-boundary completion.** Deciding which upstream IO paths a process inventory already covers is a matching problem between path descriptors and inventory exchanges, and both halves are natural-language and require domain reasoning; classical ML fits the ranking and pruning of which paths matter enough to exchange. Deterministic automation exists [ijlca-path-exchange *(citation unverified)*; procedia-auto-hybrid *(citation unverified)*; jes-hybrid-vlab *(citation unverified)*]. **Two warnings.** The path-exchange method is itself contested [path-exchange-critique *(citation unverified)*], so automating it may automate a disputed method; and introducing a non-deterministic component into the exchange step makes double counting — the defining failure mode of path exchange — irreproducible.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Proven in IO, preprint only]** **Neuro-symbolic completion of hybrid inventories from electronic tax records, with conformal calibration.** A dual-pathway architecture — deterministic activity-based calculation where physical units are observed, graph-attention estimation where only spend is observed — with split-conformal calibration producing coverage-guaranteed intervals, applied to 132 third-party-verified cement and concrete footprints from a national registry; reported prediction error falls from 0.185 to 0.042 kg CO₂e per unit of spend, with 95.2% empirical coverage at a 90% nominal target [neuro-symbolic-preprint]. **This is the only calibrated-uncertainty result we found anywhere in the coupling domain, and it arrives from an unexpected direction.** It explicitly treats the missingness as Missing Not At Random — data absent precisely where suppliers have weak reporting infrastructure — which is the correct framing and is almost universally ignored elsewhere. It is also a preprint, n = 132, one country, one material family, unreplicated, and dependent on privileged access to tax microdata.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Proven deterministic, speculative ML]** **ML-assisted regionalisation of process inventories using MRIO trade structure.** Replacing global-average process datasets with region-specific supply mixes derived from MRIO trade data is demonstrated [peng24 *(citation unverified)*], and database and region choice demonstrably matter [est-machinery-comparison *(citation unverified)*]. The speculative layer is learned *sourcing inference*: predicting the likely sourcing region of an input from product characteristics, firm attributes and trade data where the MRIO's national-average import share is too coarse. Regionalisation materially changes results for electricity-, metals- and agriculture-intensive products and is a precondition for the geographic disaggregation that due-diligence regulation demands. Risk: two studies of the same product with different regionalisation choices are no longer comparable, yet both will be labelled "regionalised".
- **[F: high · I: medium · Adjacent paired data, speculative diagnostic]** **ML diagnostics for reconciling divergent EEIO and process-LCA results.** Train on paired process-vs-IO footprints for the same products with predictors describing sector aggregation level, price heterogeneity, import share, allocation choices and impact category; the output is not a better footprint but an *explanation of the divergence*, which is what practitioners actually need when two numbers differ by an order of magnitude [steubing22 *(citation unverified)*]. A substantial paired dataset already exists, making this genuinely supervised — a rarity here. **Risk: using a learned divergence model to "correct" one database toward the other manufactures false precision and institutionalises one database's biases; used to select the method giving the lower number, it becomes a greenwashing instrument.**
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent, peer-reviewed and high-profile]** **Multi-agent multimodal systems that build product inventories with IO as the background.** Agents emulating collaboration between LCA practitioners and product stakeholders mine the open web, repair communities, regulatory databases and LCA databases and reason to a complete inventory, reporting carbon footprints **within 19% of expert LCAs with zero proprietary data, in under one minute**, with accuracy improving with additional agent reasoning steps [zhang26]. This is the strongest single demonstration that agentic AI can do end-to-end assessment work at near-expert accuracy, and it targets exactly the long tail of products that will never receive a manual LCA — precisely where EEIO is currently used as a fallback. **The 19% figure is defended as "typical of the variation between human LCAs", which reframes it: it is evidence about LCA's imprecision as much as about the agent's accuracy.** Electronics are unusually well documented; generalisation to food, chemicals or services is unproven. And automation at this cost makes it trivial to generate thousands of footprints that *look* like LCAs and enter procurement and marketing without assurance.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Proven in industrial ecology]** **ML estimation of material stocks feeding dynamic MFA and stock-driven IO.** Supervised regression on geospatial and infrastructure covariates predicts material intensity and stock where direct inventory is absent, giving national-scale stock accounts without national-scale surveys [ebrahimi22 *(citation unverified)*; road-stock-ml *(citation unverified)*; dmfa-review *(citation unverified)*]. Infrastructure stocks dominate national material cycles and drive future demand for cement, steel and aggregates — exactly the input stock-driven IO scenarios need. Material-intensity coefficients transfer poorly across countries and construction eras, and roads are the easy case; buildings and networks are harder.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO, published in *Nature*]** **Coupling ML-derived remote-sensing layers with MRIO attribution.** The upstream data product is itself a machine-learning classification of satellite imagery; the MRIO then attributes the physical change to distant final consumers. Combining a global forest-change product with an MRIO tracing consumption-driven deforestation for 24 developed countries and species range maps shows that developed countries cause much greater cumulative range loss to species outside their borders than within them [wiebe-wilcove25]. **The pattern — ML-derived physical satellite layer × MRIO attribution — is proven, generalises to water, air quality, land degradation, methane plumes and heat, and publishes in the highest-profile venues.** Its limits are structural: MRIO sector resolution is far coarser than the satellite data so commodity attribution rests on strong assumptions; "forest loss" from a classifier is not "deforestation"; and classifier errors are spatially correlated, so they do not average out. Attributing a specific physical impact to a specific country or company invites liability on evidence whose classifier uncertainty is rarely propagated.
- **[F: medium · I: high · SPECULATION]** **ML with hard constraints for physical input–output tables and monetary–physical reconciliation.** Neural or gradient-boosted models supply prior estimates for unobserved physical flows; a differentiable constrained layer then enforces mass balance, energy balance and consistency with the monetary table. **Physics-informed learning is the textbook-correct paradigm here because the balance constraints are hard and exactly known while the cell values are unknown** — and yet the PIOT literature has attracted, so far as all five reviewers could determine, *zero* ML attention [jes-piot-modular *(citation unverified)*; ecolecon-piot *(citation unverified)*; singh-n-piot *(citation unverified)*; streeck23 *(citation unverified)*]. The monetary–physical gap is the deepest structural limitation of EEIO, underlying price-heterogeneity bias, the mishandling of recycling and low- or negative-value flows, and the difficulty of circular-economy modelling. Ground truth is extremely scarce (few countries publish PIOTs), and mass balance across heterogeneous aggregates such as "chemicals" is conceptually ill-posed. **Enforcing constraints can *hide* error by redistributing it: a balanced table is not a correct table.**
- **[F: medium · I: medium-high · Adjacent, coupling speculative]** **Waste input–output coupling with ML-predicted composition and treatment allocation.** WIO requires an allocation matrix mapping waste types to treatment methods and a composition matrix mapping products to waste types, both data-poor and both learnable; computer vision systems already deployed commercially in sorting facilities generate exactly the labelled composition data WIO needs [nakamura-kondo07 *(citation unverified)*]. Circular-economy policy creates hard demand for coefficients that currently rest on decade-old surveys. Sorting-facility data are proprietary and unrepresentative (they see post-collection, post-loss streams), and overstated recycling rates flowing into circularity claims is a direct greenwashing route.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Adjacent, direct and strong]** **GNNs learning bills of materials from transaction data — physical technical coefficients without surveys.** Temporal graph networks with an inventory module using attention-based weights mapping external supply to internal consumption learn "how much of each product is necessary to make a given quantity of output" from real transaction data, with 6–50% improvement on production-function inference [chang25]. **This is the most direct route we found to *physical* supply-chain representations and firm-level A-matrices — the long-standing wish of both IO economics and product-level footprinting, and the natural empirical foundation for the PIOT problem above.** Transaction data with product-level detail are rare and commercially restricted; learned "production functions" conflate genuine recipes with procurement patterns and inventory policy; and inferred commercially sensitive recipes raise confidentiality and competition-law issues.
- **[F: medium-high · I: high · Proven coupling, adjacent emulation]** **IAM↔IO coupling with ML emulation.** Coupling an integrated assessment model with an IO database is published [esr-iam-io *(citation unverified)*], and IAM emulation is an active, well-funded field [ml-iam; duan26; gmd-emulator; natcomms-impact-ml *(citation unverified)*; ceearth-emulators *(citation unverified)*]. Together they would let consumption-based accounting — which currently lives almost entirely in retrospective analysis — speak to forward-looking mitigation policy, addressing the carbon-leakage and burden-sharing questions IAMs handle crudely. **The fundamental problem the emulator does not touch, and may obscure: IO coefficients are fixed and historical while IAM pathways involve structural change over decades.**
- **[F: medium · I: high · Proven coupling, speculative ML]** **IO↔energy-system soft-linking with surrogates to close the loop.** Soft-linking is currently a slow manual iteration; surrogates of one or both models make the loop fast enough to iterate to convergence and to run large ensembles [est-io-times *(citation unverified)*; esm-mrio-coupling *(citation unverified)*; message-macro *(citation unverified)*]. Emulating an MRIO is trivial because it is linear algebra; emulating a full capacity-expansion optimisation is genuinely hard because the solution is a constrained optimum with discontinuities — and surrogate error concentrates at the technology-switching thresholds where policy conclusions are drawn.
- **[F: medium · I: medium · SPECULATION]** **Agent-based models seeded with IO structure and calibrated by simulation-based inference.** ABM calibration is a likelihood-free inference problem — what neural simulation-based inference was built for — with IO tables providing the aggregate moments to calibrate against and firm-network reconstruction providing the topology to initialise [fessina24; cicero-surrogate]. It offers endogenous technical change, IO's deepest limitation. But highly flexible models can be tuned to any desired conclusion, and calibration to IO aggregates leaves micro-behaviour badly under-determined.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Adjacent]** **AI for critical-raw-material criticality coupled to IO material footprints.** Supervised learning on price, trade and geology features for supply-risk prediction; NLP over news and policy text for disruption signals; network analysis over trade graphs [natcomms-crm-ai *(citation unverified)*; mineral-econ-cobalt *(citation unverified)*; criticality-focus]. IO supplies the demand-side exposure that criticality frameworks handle poorly, and NLP on news gives leading indicators that trade statistics lag by a year or more. **Two structural limits: criticality indices are normative composites and ML can predict components but cannot adjudicate weights; and IO sector resolution cannot represent individual minerals, which hide inside "basic metals".**


## 10. Integration of IO into other scientific branches and into industry and policy workflows

This is where EEIO meets money, law and liability — corporate carbon accounting, mandated sustainability reporting, border carbon adjustment, due diligence — and where AI is deployed hardest and validated least. The section's organising fact is the gap between the 57.3% top-1 accuracy measured on the only public benchmark and the confidence with which the resulting numbers enter regulated disclosures. The corresponding opportunity is that regulated workflows create, for the first time, an institutional demand for exactly the uncertainty quantification, provenance and method-sensitivity testing that the research literature has been unable to make routine.

- **[F: high · I: high · Demonstrated in practice; the flagship use case]** **LLM classification of corporate spend ledgers into EEIO sectors.** Ledger account names are terse, idiosyncratic, multilingual and company-specific ("AWS reserved instances", "Prof svcs — legal"), which defeats rule-based mapping; the label space is large; and the task requires carbon-accounting domain knowledge, not string matching. The public benchmark and its numbers are given in the synthesis and §3 [atlas; watershed]. Corroborating industry evidence reports a four-stage pipeline mapping to EEIO commodity classes and computing emissions with USEEIO or Eora factors, with F1 rising from 69–72% for classical methods to 85.20–87.19% for fine-tuned LLMs — **private data, unspecified label space, no public benchmark: unverified marketing evidence, and not comparable to the public numbers** [ibm-scope3]. **The risk register for this single use case is the densest in the report.** Greenwashing: misclassification systematically shifts spend toward lower-intensity sectors, with commercial incentive not to look hard. Audit exposure: LLM classification is non-deterministic, so re-running on the same ledger can produce a different total — fatal for year-on-year comparability and assurance, and mitigable by temperature-zero, seeded, versioned and cached decisions, which is barely discussed anywhere. Regulatory non-compliance where assured Scope 3 figures are required. False comparability between firms using different vendors' classifiers while both cite "the spend-based method". And lock-in, because classifiers, training data and concordances are all commercially held.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Demonstrated in practice]** **Automated price-basis, inflation and currency adjustment in spend-based EEIO.** Mostly deterministic arithmetic that is routinely done wrong or skipped, with a narrow and appropriate AI role: detecting *which* adjustments a given ledger needs (currency, year, price basis, whether the amount includes tax or freight) from unstructured invoice metadata. At least one commercial API automates inflation correction, currency conversion, trade and transport margins, and basic↔purchaser conversion, with documented NACE→EXIOBASE lookups and explicit EXIOBASE version support [climatiq-guide] *(vendor, unverified)*. These adjustments routinely move results by tens of percent. **The correct recommendation is "automate deterministically; use AI only for metadata detection" — and the correct research recommendation is to quantify how often the basic-vs-purchaser-price error is actually made in the wild, because we believe it to be one of the largest unquantified systematic errors in commercial carbon accounting.**
- **[F: high · I: high · Demonstrated in practice, speculative on the rule]** **Supplier entity resolution and supplier-specific factor substitution.** Fuzzy matching, embeddings and graph-based record linkage reconcile supplier names across ledgers, registries, disclosure databases and product-footprint exchanges; a decision layer then chooses between supplier-specific and sector-average factors [climatiq-guide; pact-spec]. **Substitution is the *only* mechanism by which spend-based Scope 3 improves over time**, and it is the explicit direction of travel in practice. Three risks are specific and severe: supplier-specific footprints are themselves often EEIO-derived, so substitution may replace one average with another; boundary mismatch between a supplier's cradle-to-gate figure and the buyer's category definition creates double counting or omission; and coverage change across years makes *methodological* reductions look like real ones.
- **[F: high · I: high · Demonstrated in practice for the standard; speculative for the AI layer]** **AI validation and secondary-data flagging inside product-carbon-footprint exchange protocols.** A machine-readable, versioned exchange specification with lifecycle status, CPC classification, biogenic and land-use-change fields, an OAuth-secured REST API and an asynchronous events endpoint provides the substrate; three fields make it directly relevant to EEIO: **`primaryDataShare`**, **`secondaryEmissionFactorSources`** (where EEIO enters the record explicitly, by name and version), and **`DataQualityIndicators`** on technological, temporal, geographical, completeness and reliability dimensions, optional until 2025 reference periods and mandatory thereafter [pact-spec; pact-methodology; pathfinder-v2]. Three AI roles follow: schema-constrained translation of heterogeneous supplier documents into the structure; anomaly detection over received footprints using EEIO sector distributions as the reference prior; and automated provenance and quality classification. **This is the single best available mitigation for false comparability, because it makes the primary-versus-EEIO boundary explicit, structured and auditable rather than inferred.** But the spec standardises *format*, not *method*: two conforming footprints can still be methodologically incomparable, and `primaryDataShare` is self-reported with no verification mechanism.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Adjacent, with a sobering result]** **Retrieval-augmented methodology assistants for standards-compliant EEIO use.** The methodology corpus (greenhouse-gas protocols, European sustainability reporting standards, ISO series, product-footprint methodologies, border-adjustment guidance) is long, cross-referential, frequently updated and internally inconsistent, and practitioners' questions are retrieval-and-synthesis questions with citable answers. The nearest peer-reviewed evidence is a four-stage RAG pipeline over 33 documents assessing social life-cycle topics: **50% concordance between AI and manual assessments across ten social topics, with two topics yielding no AI assessment at all due to insufficient document content** [cole25]. Three divergence causes are identified: tacit knowledge gaps; evidence weighting — **humans instinctively prioritised negative findings while the model treated evidence neutrally, with the authors noting the AI was in some cases "more objective and fair"**, an unexpected argument *for* AI in a field prone to motivated reasoning; and stakeholder-perspective shifts. The honest reading: RAG-based *retrieval* is reliable, RAG-based *judgement* is not yet, and confidently wrong methodology advice carries regulatory and liability consequences.
- **[F: high · I: high · Demonstrated in practice; the highest-stakes entry]** **Border-adjustment embedded-emissions determination.** Regulators publish default embedded-emission values for transitional periods and maintain an evolving implementing-act framework [ec-cbam-defaults; ec-cbam-news; ec-cbam-legislation]; the AI roles are document extraction from installation reports and verification statements, anomaly detection comparing declared actual emissions against defaults and against sector/country EEIO priors, and classification of goods to customs and category codes. **This is the strongest incentive yet created for accuracy in embedded-emissions accounting — and the strongest incentive yet created for manipulation.** Defaults are deliberately conservative policy instruments rather than best estimates, so comparing actual data to them is not a statistical test. Liability exposure is direct and financial, and the same AI that detects resource shuffling can, deployed by the declarant, help optimise it.
- **[F: medium · I: high on a 5–10 year horizon · Demonstrated in practice, IO coupling speculative]** **Digital product passports as an IO-linkable data substrate.** Passports will produce structured product-composition data at a volume no statistical survey can match. The interesting direction is not extraction but **using aggregated passport data to validate and update IO coefficients, treating passports as a sample survey of physical composition** [hoganlovells-dpp; battery-passport-wiki] *(legal and vendor commentary, unverified)*. This is the most plausible route to a physical micro-data foundation under macro material accounts — the missing empirical base for physical IO, waste IO and firm-level bills of materials.
