Passport content is negotiated regulation rather than scientific specification; commercial confidentiality will restrict exactly the composition detail that matters; and **if AI is used to *populate* passport fields from estimates rather than measurements, the primary-data premise collapses silently.**
- **[F: high · I: high · Demonstrated in practice — the clearest case of AI supplying the physical layer IO cannot]** **Geospatial foundation models locating the physical assets that anchor commodity supply chains to places.** Two Earth-observation foundation models (AlphaEarth for candidate detection via random-forest classification over learned embeddings, Clay for spatial-context refinement) were used in a two-stage pipeline to map soy processing and storage facilities across Brazil, cross-referenced against official registers and plantation maps; the published result is **9,300 facilities at over 90% confirmation accuracy**, released in March 2026 and explicitly framed as supporting EU Deforestation Regulation enforcement and chain-of-custody [trase-ai; trase-fm]. **This matters to input–output economics for a structural reason, not a topical one.** MRIO attributes deforestation to consumption through national-sector averages; due-diligence law demands plot-level and facility-level evidence. Asset-level maps are the missing middle layer — they convert an IO hotspot into a set of named, located facilities that can be checked — and they are the natural join key between IO accounts, firm-level databases and physical monitoring. Two limits deserve stating plainly. The confirmation accuracy is a *detection* metric published in a press release rather than a peer-reviewed evaluation, and no precision/recall breakdown or false-negative rate is available; and detecting a facility is not tracing a flow — the trade attribution that turns facilities into supply chains still rests on customs and logistics data with all their asymmetries. The dual-use dimension is also real: an open map of who processes what where is a commercial-intelligence asset as much as an enforcement one.
- **[F: high · I: high · Half proven, coupling speculative — with the report's most insidious failure mode]** **Social-risk and forced-labour screening: IO hotspotting plus NLP evidence.** A two-stage architecture playing to each method's strength: IO and social hotspot databases identify where in the upstream structure risk-hours concentrate, a structural question IO answers uniquely well; NLP over news, NGO reports, court records and corporate disclosures then searches for actual evidence at those nodes [benoit-norris12 *(citation unverified)*; ijlca-clothing-slca *(citation unverified)*; nlp-modern-slavery *(citation unverified)*; cole25; pspc-forced-labour]. Due-diligence law legally requires companies to identify risk deep in supply chains where they have no visibility, which is IO's comparative advantage. **Three risks, one of them structural and severe.** The gap between sector-country *average risk* and firm-specific *incidence* is enormous and routinely elided, creating defamation and commercial-harm exposure for suppliers flagged on averages. "We screened 100% of our supply chain" using sector averages is compliance theatre. **And NLP evidence is biased toward jurisdictions with a free press and active NGOs, systematically *under*-detecting risk in the most repressive jurisdictions — a bias running exactly opposite to the true risk distribution, so the tool is least sensitive where harm is most likely, failing in a way that presents as success.**
- **[F: medium · I: high · SPECULATION, capability adjacent]** **AI agents in assurance and audit of EEIO-derived figures.** Assurance is largely structured re-performance and consistency checking, and the auditor's core question — would a different reasonable choice have produced a materially different number? — is a **method-choice sensitivity analysis that is cheap to automate and almost never done manually.** We rate this the single most valuable and most neglected check on EEIO-based disclosure. It would also detect year-on-year methodological drift disguised as emissions reduction. But both peer-reviewed agentic results available (19% deviation; 50% concordance) are well short of assurance-grade reliability [zhang26; cole25; atlas]; non-determinism conflicts with audit reproducibility; and **an agent auditing a pipeline built by similar models shares its blind spots — correlated failure is the deep problem, and it is the same hazard as validation circularity wearing professional clothing.**
- **[F: medium-high · I: high · Adjacent; the most consequential adoption pathway]** **ML inside national statistical offices: supply-use and environmental-account compilation.** NSOs compile the tables on which all EEIO work depends and hold exactly the labelled historical data supervised learning needs. The clearest public analogue on the environmental side is a mature open pipeline attributing flows of resources, wastes, emissions and losses to sectors and feeding an EEIO model builder — **and its documentation mentions no ML at all: the process is manual curation** [flowsa; useeior]. That is a precise statement of the gap: the most mature public environmental-to-sector attribution pipeline in EEIO is entirely deterministic and hand-curated. Meanwhile the official-statistics system is building the ML versions (§14). The publication lag of official IO tables is arguably the single largest practical limitation of the entire field, and this is where it can be attacked. **The risk is correspondingly large: an opaque model embedded in official statistics is a transparency and democratic-accountability problem that propagates into every downstream use worldwide, and divergent national adoption would create cross-country incomparability that MRIO then bakes into international footprints — a new and hard-to-detect error channel.**
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO, mature]** **Biodiversity, water and land footprint coupling with ML-derived environmental layers.** Species-distribution models, land-cover classifiers and hydrological products supply spatially explicit layers that are increasingly ML-derived by construction; MRIO attributes them to consumption. This is a productive literature with high-profile outputs and, importantly, reusable published characterisation-factor datasets [wiebe-wilcove25; nee-species-hotspots *(citation unverified)*; ibif-sdata *(citation unverified)*; biodiv-agri-sdata *(citation unverified)*; extinction-risk-footprints *(citation unverified)*; natsust-agrifood *(citation unverified)*; natfood-oilcrops *(citation unverified)*]. Disclosure frameworks are creating demand for exactly these metrics. Species range and intactness layers are themselves models with large, rarely propagated uncertainty; MRIO sector aggregation forces crops and land uses into coarse categories; and biodiversity characterisation factors embody contested normative choices, so a growing menu of factors is also a growing menu of favourable selections.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Unverified in this study]** **Coupling EEIO with health, nutrition and disease burden.** ML for exposure-response surface estimation and for downscaling sector-level emissions to the spatial resolution health models require; NLP for extracting nutrition composition data; emulation of expensive atmospheric-chemistry chains. Health endpoints are far more policy-salient than tonnes of CO₂e, and consumption-based health footprints reframe responsibility in ways emissions footprints cannot. **We flag this honestly as the one substantive area where our reviewers could not verify a single primary source before budget exhaustion; the underlying EEIO–health literature is known to be substantial and this entry should be treated as a research lead, not a finding.** Long causal chains compound uncertainty multiplicatively, and attributing deaths to a country's or company's consumption is inflammatory and liability-exposing on evidence with very wide uncertainty.


---


# Part D — Validation and communication


## 11. Validation and quality assurance

Input–output analysis has a structural advantage over almost every other machine-learning application domain, and the field does not exploit it: **exact invariants make a large class of errors decidable rather than merely implausible.** Row and column balance, supply equals use, the Leontief identity, footprint totals reconciling to national totals, mass balance in physical layers — these are hard, checkable, public. The organising principle for AI adoption in IO should therefore be: *let AI generate, let invariants judge, and never let a model be the sole judge of another model's output.* Everything else in this section follows from that sentence.

- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO — the highest-return, lowest-risk item]** **Machine-checkable structural invariants as an automated validation harness.** One open EEIO stack ships a validation harness that recomputes flow totals from the satellite matrix, market-shares matrix, total-requirements matrix and demand vector and checks agreement within 1% — reported passing for **1,118,742 commodity flows with zero failures** — and, crucially, *informatively fails* for two non-domestic commodities, with the documentation stating that results for those sectors are not recommended for use [useeior; ingwersen22]. Another validates its mathematical functions against published textbook results, maintains a pseudo-MRIO system for regression testing, reports ~90% test coverage and runs continuous integration [stadler21]. **The informative-failure pattern is exactly the right design: the check does not merely pass or fail, it tells the user which results are unsafe.** This is the hard substrate on which any ML-generated data must land, and it is the single most effective mitigation for validation circularity. Two honest limits: invariants constrain but do not determine — RAS produces perfectly balanced tables from a wrong prior, every time — and tolerances such as 1% are conventional, not derived from an error model. **The corresponding risk is cultural: "all checks green" is a powerful and misleading signal that can crowd out substantive scrutiny, and an LLM-generated check suite that encodes a misunderstanding of the accounting framework and then certifies it is strictly worse than no check.**
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent, and the best available answer to the ground-truth problem]** **Metamorphic testing: validate against invariances rather than against truth.** The obstacle that blocks conventional evaluation of every ML component in this map is that there is no true table to score against, and the tables that exist are partly model output. Metamorphic testing sidesteps it: instead of asking whether the output is correct, it asks whether the output *changes in the way it must* under transformations whose effect is known exactly. Input–output analysis is unusually rich in such relations, all of them cheap to evaluate and none requiring a label. Rescaling all monetary units must leave every physical footprint unchanged. Permuting sector labels must permute every result identically. Aggregating two sectors and then computing must equal computing and then aggregating, for any linear indicator. A footprint computed through the Leontief and the Ghosh formulations must agree wherever both are defined. Inserting a sector with zero output must change nothing. Scaling final demand by a constant must scale a linear footprint by the same constant. Each of these is a test that a learned imputer, a learned updater or an LLM-generated analysis pipeline can fail, and failure is diagnostic rather than ambiguous — an ML gap-filler that has latched onto sector ordering, or an agent that has silently mixed price bases, fails the permutation or the rescaling test loudly while scoring well on any accuracy metric. **We rate this the single most useful validation idea available to the field and the cheapest to adopt: it requires no new data, no benchmark and no ground truth, only the discipline of writing the invariances down.** Two honest limits: passing every invariance test does not make an estimate correct, only structurally sane; and some invariances hold only under conventions (valuation basis, treatment of negatives, secondary production) that must be stated before the test means anything.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO — the field's noise floor]** **Monte Carlo uncertainty propagation with empirically grounded, correlated priors.** Restated from §1 because it belongs here as the benchmark every validation claim must clear: median country-level CVs of 4/12/33% and median sector-level CVs of 94/100/113% for CO₂/CH₄/N₂O, with Dirichlet sampling preserving the correlation induced by disaggregating a reported national total into sectoral cells [schulte24; schulte-zenodo]. **Two propositions follow that the field has not internalised.** First, an ML method that improves an imputation by 10% is invisible against a 94% CV, so accuracy claims must be sized against this. Second, the finding that uncertainty *decreases* from emission accounts to consumption-based footprints through supply-chain cancelling-out is genuinely reassuring for aggregate consumption-based accounting and deserves to be much better known — **and it needs replication on other satellite accounts and databases before it is relied upon.** The study covers the satellite side only; monetary-table, trade-linkage and rest-of-world uncertainty are not included, so these are lower bounds.
- **[F: high · I: high but badly under-exploited · Proven in IO]** **Native per-cell uncertainty layers produced by the compilation optimiser.** One global database assigns uncertainty levels to each primary data point, lets less-uncertain data override more-uncertain data during construction, and **ships standard-deviation tables with the release** — and gives a memorable physical intuition: uncertainty in weakly constrained parts of the table (low-income economies, small industries) "budges" when constraints are applied elsewhere, "very much like a water bed" [gloria059]. It covers the *monetary* table, complementing the satellite-side coverage above. **Almost no downstream footprint study uses these tables. Making them first-class objects in standard tooling is a modest engineering job with a large credibility payoff.** The circularity caveat is real: reliability weights set by the same team that chooses the constraints will look small wherever the compiler felt confident, and in a water-bed system a standard deviation reported for country A can be an artefact of a constraint imposed on country B.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Proven classically, unpublished at modern scale]** **Bayesian balancing as the coherent joint production of estimate and uncertainty.** Covered as a compilation method in §1; as a *validation* method its value is that it returns a posterior over tables — exactly the object downstream Monte Carlo requires — and handles conflicting sources by explicit likelihood weighting rather than arbitrary precedence rules, while formalising the "assign uncertainty to each primary data point" logic that leading compilers already implement deterministically [rodrigues14; schulte26-bayes; gtap-bayes-reconcile *(citation unverified)*; ecmod-estimation-errors *(citation unverified)*]. **The honest scale problem: the ratio of unknowns to primary observations in a full global MRIO is of order 1000:1 or worse, and a Bayesian veneer can lend false authority to what remains a hopelessly under-determined problem.** Very expensive posteriors also invite under-sampling, and the IO community has no established convergence-diagnostic culture to catch unconverged chains reported as converged.
- **[F: high · I: high · SPECULATION for IO — a clean, cheap gap]** **Conformal prediction as a distribution-free wrapper on every ML-imputed cell.** The need is thoroughly documented in the field's own literature: the gap-filling benchmark explicitly lists "does not quantify uncertainty" as a limitation [cullen24], the data-centric critique records 25.82% split-to-split swings and cross-database degradation from 34.68% to 50.6% [zhao25], and reviews call for reporting calibration metrics and prediction-interval coverage rather than point accuracy [wang25]. Conformal is cheap (one calibration pass), model-agnostic, has mature libraries, and converts an unfalsifiable point imputation into a checkable empirical claim; Mondrian variants give per-sector or per-income-group coverage, exposing exactly the differential reliability that recurs throughout this report. **No published application to IO cells exists.** Two failure modes must be stated whenever it is used: marginal coverage is not conditional coverage, and exchangeability lapses silently under the distribution shift that is *normal* when transferring across databases or vintages.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Adjacent, with a decisive caveat]** **Ensemble anomaly detection with consensus voting.** Five detectors with a ≥3-of-5 consensus rule, applied to European regional statistics, integrate with existing validation workflows and are reproducible and scalable [oancea26]. Consensus suppresses the method-specific false-positive modes that make single-detector deployment unusable at statistical-office scale. **The decisive caveat is the paper's own: flagged anomalies "do not necessarily signal data quality issues; rather, they reflect meaningful structural divergence".** Capital cities are flagged because they genuinely are unusual; disadvantaged regions are flagged because of persistent disadvantage. Without a labelled error set you cannot estimate precision. And detectors are highly sensitive to feature space and scaling, which is severe when values span twelve orders of magnitude.
- **[F: high · I: high · SPECULATION — the missing evaluation protocol]** **Adversarial corruption benchmarks for IO error detection.** Inject known, realistic corruptions into a table with known ground truth — transposed digits, unit errors of 10³, misallocated re-exports, a sector's coefficients swapped with a neighbour's, a satellite account scaled by a wrong conversion factor — then measure detector recall at fixed false-positive rate. **This is the only way to obtain precision and recall for IO error detectors, because real labelled error corpora do not exist, and without it every anomaly-detection claim in this domain is unfalsifiable.** Ground truth is available by construction and open data give a legally redistributable base [exioml-sdata; oancea26]. A strong prior worth testing: consistent unit errors and systematic misallocation are probably near-invisible to distributional outlier detection because they preserve the shape of the distribution. *One practical note from a reviewer worth acting on: the issue trackers of the main open IO software packages are the closest thing available to a real labelled error corpus and have never been mined.*
- **[F: medium-high · I: high · Speculative method on a demonstrated base]** **Cross-database triangulation as a learned error signal.** Treat divergence between independently compiled MRIOs for the same country-sector-year as a noisy label meaning "at least one of these is wrong here", then learn which structural, metadata and provenance features predict large disagreement — gradient-boosted trees with attribution, because interpretability is essential if compilers are to act on the output [giljum19; owen16; tukker18-owen; moran-wood14]. It would convert a scattered comparison literature into a routinely computable cell-level prior on where any given MRIO is least trustworthy. **Two objections must travel with it, and both are serious.** The databases are *not* independent — they share upstream sources — so agreement is not evidence of correctness and disagreement understates total error. And **a reliability score optimised against would produce Goodhart's law in its purest form: compilers converging their methods would reduce measured disagreement without reducing error, destroying the multi-model ensemble that is the field's best structural-uncertainty diagnostic.**
- **[F: medium · I: high · Demonstrated as practice, no protocol]** **Multi-database ensembles as an uncertainty statement.** Running the same footprint question through several MRIOs and reporting the spread captures compilation uncertainty that no within-database method can, is immediately intelligible to non-specialists, and is now mechanically tractable because standard tooling parses multiple databases [stadler21; giljum19]. **An ensemble band is the most honest single number a footprint paper can report, and its absence is the norm.** But the ensemble is small (4–6 members), unweighted, of mixed vintage, and non-independent — so the spread is a *lower* bound on structural uncertainty and cannot be read as a confidence interval.
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent, with a strong motivating base rate]** **LLM audit of methodology descriptions and reporting completeness.** Extract database name and *version*, sector and region resolution, allocation and import-proportionality assumptions, satellite source, software name and version from a methods section and score against a fixed rubric — text-to-schema extraction against an explicit rubric, where a wrong answer is cheaply falsifiable against the source. The motivating audit is quantitative: 60% of sampled LCA studies lack a complete transparent inventory account, 65% provide incomplete inventory data, **only 2% share any analysis script**, and reproduction error is under 1% with complete documentation but −20.97% when database versions differ and −12.54% when datasets are not fully documented — with a 32-element reporting framework proposed that is directly automatable [kamali26]. **Undocumented version drift alone exceeds most published effect sizes in this literature.** The model can verify only that a method was *stated*, never that it was *followed*, and authors will optimise for the checklist.
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO]** **Continuous integration, regression testing and code validation.** In a field where 2% of published studies share their analysis script, **the code *is* the unvalidated component.** The leading open tools already demonstrate the standard — textbook-result validation anchoring an opaque numerical library to a canonical published answer, pseudo-MRIO regression testing, high test coverage, automatic documentation rebuilds, and a JSON metadata store with a modification history recording aggregations, downloads and system changes as a first-class provenance object [stadler21; useeior; brightway *(citation unverified)*; mario *(citation unverified)*]. The gap is the long tail of bespoke research scripts, which is where most published footprints actually come from. **A specific hazard of AI assistance here: LLM-generated tests tend to restate the implementation rather than the specification, producing high coverage with near-zero diagnostic power.**
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Adjacent]** **Concept-drift and data-quality monitoring across statistical vintages.** MRIO compilation is a *recurring* production process with annual vintages, classification revisions and periodic benchmark revisions, all of which silently invalidate models trained on earlier vintages. Robustness against concept drift is a named quality dimension in the official-statistics ML quality concept [dumpert23], and the magnitude is quantified by the −20.97% version-drift figure [kamali26]. The hard part is institutional: someone must own the monitor between releases. And distinguishing genuine economic structural change from drift-induced model failure is the anomaly-versus-error problem one level up.
- **[F: mitigation medium · I: risk high · The largest systemic risk]** **Validation circularity.** This is a hazard rather than a technique. When the same model family imputes missing cells, flags anomalous cells, writes the validation checks and drafts the narrative, errors become self-confirming: an imputation drawn from the model's prior looks maximally plausible to a detector drawn from the same prior, *because* it is the model's prior. The generic mechanism is documented in the model-collapse literature and in synthetic-data policy analysis [model-collapse *(citation unverified)*; ada-lovelace-synthetic *(citation unverified)*]. **In IO specifically we found no study of the phenomenon, which is itself the finding.** Concrete instances already visible in the literature reviewed here: ML regional tables validated against survey tables produced under the same conventions; downscaled emissions validated against other downscalings sharing covariates; spend classifiers benchmarked on LLM-generated spend text; a firm-transaction production network validated against LLM-generated product networks; ML models trained and evaluated on a single MRIO's own construction. **Mitigation is architectural, not algorithmic:** validate ML-generated cells only against exogenous invariants and genuinely independent measurements; hold out truly external data such as a newly published national supply-use table; provenance-tag every cell as measured, estimated or model-imputed and carry the tag through to results; and require that the imputation model and the validation model do not share a training corpus. **Uncorrected, this produces MRIO databases that agree with each other and with their own uncertainty estimates while drifting together away from reality, with the drift concentrated in the data-poor regions that matter most for equity-relevant claims — and it interacts destructively with cross-database triangulation, which stops working while continuing to look reassuring.**


## 12. Data visualisation, communication and decision support

The binding constraint on the *use* of environmentally-extended input–output analysis has never been the availability of the data; it is the scarcity of people who can compute a footprint correctly. Language interfaces attack that constraint directly, and this is the area where AI could plausibly change who gets to use IO at all. It is also where the failure mode is worst: high fluency combined with low accuracy on compositional tasks defeats casual review, and the specific EE-IO errors — mixing producer and consumer accounting, mixing price bases, double-counting supply-chain stages — are silent and look exactly like correct answers.

- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent, gap in IO]** **Natural-language and text-to-code interfaces over IO databases.** Generating library calls rather than SQL is the right design, because MRIO results are matrix operations on typed objects, not row filters: the API enforces the accounting semantics, the generated code is human-readable and reviewable, and it executes in a sandbox whose output can be checked against the balance invariants of §11. The libraries provide exactly the clean, documented, high-level abstraction such an interface needs, including automatic database downloaders [stadler21; pymrio-autodownload; useeior]. We found no published natural-language interface to an MRIO database. **The sobering evidence is from an adjacent benchmark of multi-step analysis over structured data plus domain documentation: the best model tested reached 76.39% on easy tasks but only 14.55% on hard ones, with documented failures in planning, following implicit domain rules and multi-step instruction following** [dabstep]. EE-IO queries are squarely in the hard, domain-rule-laden category.
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Proven in a restricted domain — a claim in this report that peer reports correctly qualified]** **Knowledge-augmented LLM interfaces to footprint databases: the first published instance.** A "Carbon Footprint Wizard" combines retrieval-augmented generation over a curated knowledge base with tool access to life-cycle and input–output-derived footprint data for food products, presented as an interface that lets non-specialists obtain traceable footprint estimates [cf-wizard]. **We had stated that no published LLM interface to footprint databases existed; that claim was too strong and we withdraw it in this form.** The accurate statement is narrower and still holds: no published, evaluated natural-language interface exists over a *general-purpose multi-regional* EEIO system such as EXIOBASE, GLORIA or USEEIO, with reported accuracy on a benchmark of analyst-grade queries. The food-domain instance is a proof of concept in the easiest possible setting — a bounded product space, a well-curated factor base, and users whose questions are lookups rather than compositional analyses. Its main methodological contribution is architectural rather than empirical: knowledge augmentation plus tool access, rather than parametric recall, is the correct design, which is the same conclusion this report reaches independently below.
- **[F: medium · I: high if solved · Adjacent, negative-leaning]** **Agentic loops for multi-step footprint questions.** Plan, write code, execute, inspect, revise — attractive because real footprint questions decompose into a sequence of matrix operations plus judgement calls, execution gives a grounding signal, and the generated notebook is a reproducible artefact. The hard/easy gap above is the whole story: agents do well on single-retrieval tasks and fail on the compositional ones that constitute actual analysis, and prompt sensitivity means results are not stable across sessions, which is fatal for a validation-critical workflow [dabstep; dsbench *(citation unverified)*; datascibench *(citation unverified)*]. **The combination of high fluency and roughly 15% hard-task accuracy is worse than obvious incompetence, because it defeats casual review.**
- **[F: high · I: high · SPECULATION for IO; infrastructure fully demonstrated]** **LLM agents with tool access to IO models and APIs.** This is the design that puts the model where it is strong — translating a vague question into a specification, interpreting output — and keeps it away from where it is weak: arithmetic and factor recall, the source of hallucinated emission factors. **An agent that *computes* a footprint by calling a validated library cannot hallucinate the multiplier; an agent asked to recall one will.** The tool layer is mature and open: a Python MRIO toolbox with automatic parsers for EXIOBASE, WIOD, EORA26 and OECD tables, missing-table detection, classification modification, extension restructuring and automated report generation [stadler21; pymrio-autodownload]; an R package building and exporting US EEIO models with a validation harness [useeior; ingwersen22]; and a satellite-account pipeline feeding it [flowsa]. Multiple MRIOs can be run in parallel to expose database sensitivity automatically — something practitioners almost never do by hand. **Two structural warnings.** The reference public EEIO web API was **archived on 5 June 2025 and is no longer maintained** [useeio-api-archived], exactly when agentic tool use would make it most valuable, creating a vacuum that proprietary platforms will fill — lock-in by default. And the highest-resolution global MRIO lacks an open parser in the standard toolbox, making it least accessible to agentic tooling. **Reconciled position across reviewers: this is simultaneously the most under-exploited and the most risk-reducing opportunity in the communication domain, and it should be built.**
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Adjacent]** **Automated narrative generation with numeric grounding.** The numbers come from the model run, the language model writes only connective prose, and every numeral is checked against the source table before release. This scales dissemination across countries and languages, gives consistent framing across a database release, and — the underrated benefit — **can be forced to state uncertainty in every narrative, quoting the sector-level CVs that human-written summaries routinely omit** [unece-genai *(citation unverified)*; aiml4os-cros; schulte24]. **The limitation is precise: grounding checks catch fabricated *numbers*; they do not catch fabricated *causal claims* ("driven by increased manufacturing efficiency"), which are the part readers remember.** Attribution language in IO is technically delicate — a footprint change can come from the emission account, the technology matrix, the trade structure or the deflator — and generic prose will attribute confidently and wrongly.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Adjacent]** **Detecting fluent-but-wrong narratives.** Semantic entropy — clustering multiple sampled generations by meaning and measuring entropy over meanings rather than tokens — flags confabulation where the model is generating arbitrary content [semantic-entropy *(citation unverified)*; hallucination-survey *(citation unverified)*]. It is one of the few tools that addresses the specific failure mode that makes LLM communication of statistics dangerous. **But it detects *confabulation*, not *systematic error*: a model consistently wrong about producer-versus-consumer accounting will have low semantic entropy and sail through — and that is exactly the EE-IO failure mode that matters most.** A "hallucination-checked" label would therefore create unwarranted trust in precisely the errors the check cannot see.
- **[F: medium-high · I: medium · Partly speculative]** **AI-assisted visualisation of high-dimensional MRIO results.** Two distinct uses. Layout and chart-type selection is easy and low-value. **The substantive one is aggregation for legibility: using clustering or graph partitioning to reduce a 164×120 or 49×200 flow network to the 10–20 nodes a Sankey can carry — a genuine machine-learning problem (which aggregation preserves the most footprint-relevant structure?) rather than a design preference.** Aggregation choice is currently ad hoc and unreported, and it materially changes what a Sankey appears to show; making it an explicit, optimised, reported step would be a real methodological improvement [gloria059; exioml-sdata; sei-sankey *(citation unverified)*]. There is no aggregation faithful for all questions, so "optimal" is question-relative. **The standing risk is that aesthetically compelling diagrams are built on the least reliable numbers in the system — visual polish and epistemic warrant are inversely correlated here, and nothing in the chart signals it.**
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Proven method, speculative verbalisation]** **Structural path analysis as the native explanation engine.** SPA is not AI, but it is the field's own built-in explanation method: it decomposes a footprint into ranked supply-chain paths giving a faithful, additive, *exact* attribution — a stronger explanation object than any post-hoc ML attribution because it is derived from the model rather than approximating it [spa-review; spd-energyecon; owen16]. Paths map onto real procurement decisions ("your emissions run through this electricity mix via that textile stage"), which is the actionable form. **The strategic claim, which two reviewers reached independently: EE-IO should prefer SPA over SHAP wherever both are available.** The AI contributions are verbalisation of the top paths into readable prose and learned pruning to make extraction tractable at high resolution (§6). The honest limitation: top-*k* paths are a biased sample, over-representing short high-intensity chains and understating diffuse contributions.
- **[F: high · I: medium · Adjacent, abundant, and narrowly appropriate]** **Post-hoc attribution and counterfactual explanation as a communication device.** Appropriate for explaining an ML *component* inside an IO pipeline — why did the gap-filler impute this value? — which is a stated requirement in official-statistics quality frameworks, where explainability is one of six named dimensions [dumpert23; wang25]. Counterfactuals ("what would this emission factor have to be for the ranking to flip?") are a genuinely good decision-support object and map onto sensitivity analysis. **Inappropriate as a substitute for exact decomposition, and unreliable under the strong feature correlation normal in IO data** [shap-china-county; shap-road; shap-agri; comp-econ-shap *(citation unverified)*].
- **[F: high · I: high · Speculative product, proven components]** **Conversational copilots for policy analysts and procurement officers.** Retrieval-augmented models over a curated, validated EEIO model and its documentation, restricted to a fixed query vocabulary and calling into the model rather than reasoning freely. Everything needed exists: an open validated model with a published technical validation, an open toolkit, and a well-understood pattern [ingwersen22; useeior]. Procurement is the largest realistic application surface for EEIO and is currently served by consultants and spreadsheets. **The single most valuable design feature available is built-in humility: the validation harness's "results for these sectors are not recommended for use" signal can be surfaced directly to the user.** The single most common misuse — treating a sector average as a supplier-specific figure — is made *easier*, not harder, by a conversational interface, and the sector-level CVs mean the numbers a procurement officer most wants are the least reliable ones the system holds.
- **[F: high · I: high · Speculative form, proven ingredients — the cheapest large win]** **Dashboards that treat uncertainty and provenance as first-class content.** If uncertainty layers, standard-deviation tables, conformal intervals and provenance tags are computed but not displayed, none of the validation work in §11 reaches a decision-maker. All the ingredients already exist and are open [gloria059; schulte-zenodo; useeior; stadler21]. **Provenance tags — measured / estimated / model-imputed, carried through to results — directly mitigate validation circularity by making AI-generated content visible rather than laundered.** The design research problem is real: users routinely ignore uncertainty display, and making a CV of 94% consequential rather than decorative is genuinely hard. **The failure mode to avoid is uncertainty theatre: error bars shown but not propagated into the headline number or the ranking, so decisions are made on point estimates anyway while the interface claims rigour.**


---


# Part E — Computation and other aspects


## 13. Computational aspects

Computation is the quietest and, on our assessment, the second most under-exploited part of the map. Three mature techniques from numerical analysis and scientific machine learning — randomised linear algebra, automatic differentiation, and multilevel Monte Carlo — fit MRIO data almost perfectly, come with rigorous error bounds, require no training data, and have never been applied to it. They attack the compute cost that is the stated reason uncertainty reporting is not routine. Conversely, the most fashionable computational idea in this space — neural surrogates for the Leontief solve — is the one we rate lowest, because it replaces an exactly known cheap linear map with an inexact learned one and forfeits the field's strongest validation asset.

- **[F: high · I: high · Standard practice, under-documented]** **Iterative solution versus direct inversion.** For a single demand vector you never need the inverse, only the solve, and iterative methods cost O(nnz) per iteration against O(n³) for explicit inversion. The Neumann expansion has an additional property that is unusual and useful: **its partial sums are economically meaningful — they are the production layers — so truncation error has an interpretation.** At the scale of the largest global tables (roughly 19,680 endogenous sectors, ~2.13 billion elements in the full annual system, requiring machines with many terabytes of memory) explicit inversion is wasteful for most workflows and prohibitive inside Monte Carlo loops [gloria059; pscktoolkit *(citation unverified)*; gpu-solvers-review *(citation unverified)*]. **The risk is one-signed and therefore worse than noise: silent under-convergence systematically *understates* footprints, and biases exactly the long, diffuse supply chains consumption-based accounting exists to capture.** Any truncation must report its order and residual.
- **[F: medium-high · I: high · Adjacent, mature]** **GPU acceleration and sparse solvers.** MRIO transaction matrices are sparse and the workloads that matter (many right-hand sides, many Monte Carlo replicates) are embarrassingly parallel; the incidental benefit is that the machine-learning software stack has made GPU linear algebra accessible to non-HPC researchers, and GPU-accelerated *selected* inversion is directly relevant because most EE-IO applications need selected entries or columns of `L` rather than all of it [gpu-solvers-review *(citation unverified)*; selected-inversion *(citation unverified)*; gloria059]. **A specific and under-appreciated hazard: reduced-precision arithmetic (the default in ML-derived stacks) silently violates the balance invariants — the checks would fail at 1% tolerance for the right reason, and a researcher who loosens the tolerance to make them pass has broken the accounting.** Sparsity structure is also irregular (dense within-country blocks, sparse trade blocks), so a blocked dense-within/sparse-between formulation is probably the right structure and we are not aware of published work exploiting it.
- **[F: high · I: high · SPECULATION — highest-conviction unexploited computational item]** **Randomised numerical linear algebra and sketching.** Randomised SVD, random embeddings, sketch-and-solve, Nyström, CUR and single-pass streaming sketches are mature, implemented and come with rigorous probabilistic error bounds; streaming variants store O(k(m+n)+s²) instead of mn with expected error at most a small constant times the optimal low-rank error, plus *a posteriori* error estimation for rank selection [martinsson-tropp20; tropp-streaming; halko *(citation unverified)*]. **The data are exactly the shape these algorithms were designed for, the single-pass property directly addresses the I/O constraint, approximation quality is *measured* rather than assumed — and we found no application to MRIO whatsoever.** A sketch of an MRIO could also be distributable at a fraction of the size of the full table with certified error, which might partly circumvent licensing constraints (a legal question we cannot resolve). **The failure mode is precisely targeted at the wrong people: error bounds are in spectral or Frobenius norm over the whole matrix, but EE-IO users care about specific small entries, so a small global error can coexist with large relative error on the small cells that matter for low-income economies and small sectors — degrading exactly the results equity-focused research depends on, invisibly, while the global metric looks excellent.**
- **[F: medium · I: medium-high · SPECULATION]** **Low-rank and tensor decomposition for compression and completion.** Treating the annual series as a third- or fourth-order tensor makes consecutive-year redundancy exploitable; the largest global series contains roughly 80.9 billion elements across 1990–2028, a compelling compression target, and tensor completion is the natural formalism for the missing-year and missing-country problems [gloria059; temporal-tensor-ode; tensor-pca-econ *(citation unverified)*]. Non-negative variants are the right choice and are slower. **Balance identities are not preserved by unconstrained decomposition, so a post-hoc balancing step is needed and reintroduces the problems it was meant to avoid; and low-rank completion imposes the average technology on countries without data — an unusually explicit form of the "developing countries look like the average" bias, now with the imprimatur of a machine-learning method.**
- **[F: high · I: medium · Adjacent — and we rate this lower than most would]** **ML surrogates to avoid repeated Leontief solves.** The honest accounting: a surrogate replaces a linear solve, and a linear solve is already cheap relative to training a surrogate. It pays only where the *system* is nonlinear or where `A` itself changes (endogenous technology, price feedbacks, MRIO–CGE hybrids). Direct evidence that simple models suffice on IO-shaped data comes from the field's own benchmark, where tree ensembles match neural networks at lower compute cost [exioml-sdata; natcomms-impact-ml *(citation unverified)*]. **Approximating an exactly known linear map with an inexact learned one destroys the balance identities and the additive decomposability that makes structural path analysis possible, and a surrogate footprint cannot be audited against national totals — forfeiting the field's strongest validation asset for a speedup it usually did not need. Unmotivated surrogate use should be treated as a methodological red flag.**
- **[F: high · I: high · SPECULATION — the other highest-conviction item]** **Automatic differentiation through the IO pipeline.** Reverse-mode AD applied to the whole chain — concordance, balancing, inversion, extension, aggregation — yields exact gradients of any scalar output with respect to *all* inputs at roughly the cost of one forward evaluation, where finite differences would cost O(n) evaluations. There is even a closed-form adjoint for the Leontief inverse (`dL = L dA L`), so AD and analysis agree. **Four capabilities unlock at once:** exact global sensitivity ranking (which of ~10⁸ cells matters for this footprint?), which directly serves compilation triage; first-order variance propagation without Monte Carlo; gradient-based balancing and calibration as constrained optimisation, generalising RAS to arbitrary objectives; and end-to-end training in which the accounting identities sit *inside* the computation graph, so learned components are forced to respect them. The adjacent proof is the three-orders-of-magnitude calibration result [hamid25], with established gradient-assisted calibration for agent-based models [icaif-gradient-calibration *(citation unverified)*] and mature tooling [jax-autodiff; quantecon-diff]. **It requires no new statistics and no training data — only re-implementation.** Limits: reverse-mode memory at MRIO scale needs checkpointing or implicit differentiation through the balancing routine; discrete concordance steps are non-differentiable and must be held fixed; and first-order sensitivity is local, so gradient magnitude read as importance without regard to each input's plausible range is a category error.
- **[F: medium-high · I: high · Demonstrated problem, speculative fix]** **Reducing the compute cost of Monte Carlo uncertainty.** N replicates × one global solve each is the dominant compute cost in the field and is the stated reason uncertainty reporting is not routine [schulte24; gloria059]. **Multilevel Monte Carlo is an unusually natural fit: an aggregated MRIO is cheap and highly correlated with the full-resolution result, which is exactly the condition under which MLMC delivers order-of-magnitude savings — and aggregated versions of every major MRIO already exist.** Combine with control variates, quasi-Monte Carlo, AD-based first-order screening and sketching to shrink each replicate. No published multilevel or control-variate Monte Carlo for MRIO was found. **Two risks: under-resourced Monte Carlo with too few replicates reported as converged, in a field with no norm for reporting the Monte Carlo standard error of its own estimate; and accelerations that bias the tails, where the policy-relevant risk lives, while looking fine on the mean.**
- **[F: high · I: high · Proven in IO — a distinctive strength of this community]** **Virtual laboratories: move code to data.** A shared cloud environment holding the data and the compilation code, in which many teams build customised MRIOs from a common root, solves the data-transfer problem, the reproducibility problem and, potentially, the licensing problem simultaneously — and the IO community has operated this model for a decade [vlab-stoten *(citation unverified)*; global-mrio-lab *(citation unverified)*; virtual-ielab *(citation unverified)*; piolab *(citation unverified)*], with a directly analogous experimentation "sandpit" now being built for the European Statistical System [aiml4os-cros]. **This is the only workable architecture for confidential microdata *and* licensed MRIOs *and* equitable access, and it is the single most effective lever for including under-resourced groups. What is missing is GPU provisioning and machine-learning tooling inside these labs.** The governance risk is real: if one lab becomes the de-facto standard, its methodological choices become invisible defaults across the literature and the multi-database ensemble collapses.
- **[F: medium · I: high · Documented constraint]** **Memory, storage and I/O.** Chunked columnar formats, out-of-core and streaming algorithms, compression and lazy evaluation. The constraint is documented and severe — billions of elements per year, terabytes of memory, and gigabyte-scale CSV footprint-network files at ordinary resolution [gloria059; exioml-sdata; stadler21; pymrio-autodownload]. **Moving to chunked binary columnar formats would deliver order-of-magnitude reductions in load time and storage at no methodological cost — a pure engineering win that would materially widen participation.** The counter-argument deserves recording: CSV's virtue is that it is readable by anyone with anything, which matters for equity. **The risk if nothing is done is data availability becoming *nominal* — technically open but practically inaccessible without terabytes of local storage and fast networking, a licensing-neutral but equally effective exclusion mechanism.**
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Adjacent, mixed results]** **AI-assisted research software engineering.** The IO community is small, has essentially no research-software-engineer career path, and spends a large fraction of its effort on undifferentiated, well-specified, testable plumbing — parsers for heterogeneous national tables, concordances, tests, documentation. That is where code assistants pay [scireplicate *(citation unverified)*; llm-code-agents-survey *(citation unverified)*; dabstep]. **The danger is inverted relative to intuition: impact is highest on the plumbing and lowest — and most dangerous — on the accounting logic, because generated code encodes plausible-looking but wrong conventions (basic versus purchaser prices, industry versus product technology assumptions) with total fluency, and those errors are semantically deep and syntactically invisible. The novel systemic risk is correlated failure: a subtle convention error propagating across many projects that all used the same assistant, in a field accustomed to idiosyncratic individual errors.**
- **[F: high · I: medium direct, high normative · Adjacent]** **Reflexive accounting for the field's own compute.** EE-IO is the discipline that measures the environmental footprint of economic activity; it should measure its own. The point has bite rather than being merely decorative: the finding that tree ensembles match deep learning at lower computational cost on IO-shaped data is a normative result for this community as well as a technical one [exioml-sdata; wang25]. We found no IO paper reporting its own compute footprint. **Attribution of shared-cluster and cloud energy is genuinely hard and figures will be rough; and a compute-frugality norm could be misused to discourage legitimate large-scale work or to penalise groups on carbon-intensive grids, which correlates with the Global South.**


## 14. Other: research infrastructure, benchmarks, and the meta-layer

Two things belong here that fit nowhere else and that we judge to be more important than several of the modelling opportunities above: the near-total absence of benchmarks and evaluation protocols, and the institutional disconnection between academic input–output research and the official-statistics system that is already deploying AI upstream of it.

- **[F: high · I: high · One instance in IO]** **Open, ML-ready benchmark datasets derived from MRIO.** The field's only purpose-built open ML benchmark resource restructures an openly licensed MRIO into ML-ready form — 49 regions × 28 years, hundreds of emission and resource types, both product-by-product and industry-by-industry formats, with a GPU toolkit, a reproducible eight-model regression baseline and open licensing — and its authors nominate anomaly detection, time-series forecasting, sector classification and supply-chain optimisation as further tasks [exioml-arxiv; exioml-sdata]. A companion open classification dataset extends this to the retrieval framing [exionaics]. **Three limitations are structural and matter more than the dataset's virtues.** It is built on one database, so it cannot test cross-database generalisation — which is where the action is. Sectoral GHG regression is a weak proxy for any real IO compilation task; success on it does not imply usefulness for table construction. **And the deepest risk is benchmark-driven distortion: if the machine-learning community optimises a task that is not the compilation problem, the field gets impressive numbers on the wrong question, and one database's compilation choices become the unexamined defaults of every learned model.**
- **[F: high · I: high · Demonstrated recipe, unapplied to IO's real problems]** **Benchmark infrastructure built on synthetic data to circumvent confidentiality.** The spend-classification benchmark is the existence proof that the recipe works: LLM-generated synthetic line items conditioned on rules observed in a real de-identified dataset, human-reviewed, validated against an internal private benchmark, with a metric suite that includes **emission-factor RMSE — which measures what actually matters (error in the emissions number) rather than mere label accuracy, and which other benchmark designers should copy** [atlas; watershed]. Its authors honestly report that the synthetic benchmark is easier than reality. **The recipe generalises directly and has not been applied to the tasks that matter most: there is no public benchmark for process↔IO concordance, none for hybridisation double-counting correction, none for error detection with ground truth, none for supplier entity resolution. Building one of these is, on our assessment, the highest-value and lowest-cost intervention available to this research programme.**
- **[F: high · I: high · Adjacent, directly transferable]** **A quality framework for machine learning in statistical production.** One already exists, is short, and maps almost directly onto MRIO compilation: six dimensions of accuracy (assessed through quality measures, confidence intervals **and subgroup evaluation**), robustness (stability under perturbation, data-sensitivity testing, concept-drift detection), explainability, reproducibility (frozen datasets, versioned code, comprehensive documentation), timeliness and punctuality, and cost-effectiveness — explicitly linked to existing official-statistics quality frameworks rather than creating a parallel regime [dumpert23; unece-ml-pub]. **Subgroup evaluation under "accuracy" is precisely the discipline that would catch the differential-reliability problems that recur in almost every section of this report.** Mapping this framework onto MRIO compilation would be a genuinely useful and quick piece of work. Two caveats: it was written for production processes rather than research pipelines where nobody owns the process, and it predates generative AI.
- **[F: high · I: high · SPECULATION for IO; instruments established]** **Model cards, datasheets and an AI-content declaration for MRIO releases.** If ML-generated cells enter published tables, users need to know which cells, produced by what model, trained on what, validated how, with what known failure modes. The instruments are established [datasheets *(citation unverified)*; data-cards *(citation unverified)*] and the field already has the habit in embryo — standard-deviation tables shipped with a release, technical validation sections, modification histories [gloria059; ingwersen22; stadler21]. **We know of no MRIO release shipping a model card. A one-page AI-content declaration per release would be a substantial improvement over current practice, which is silence.** The risk to avoid is transparency substituting for standards: a card that discloses AI use and thereby normalises it without any accompanying quality requirement, or partial disclosure that implies the undisclosed parts are measured.
- **[F: high · I: very high · Already under way, and largely invisible to the IO literature]** **The institutional bridge to official statistics.** The European Statistical System's AI/ML programme runs 2024–2028 across 15–16 countries with 13 work packages, including data editing (WP8), imputation (WP9), **mapping supply-chain networks from company-level data (WP11)**, large language models (WP12), synthetic data (WP13), a shared experimentation sandpit (WP3), standards and methodologies (WP5), and a training and knowledge repository (WP6) [aiml4os; aiml4os-cros; aiml4os-wp13]. The UNECE modernisation group has run a multi-country machine-learning project with published classification and coding results and workshops, plus work on generative AI for official statistics [unece-class; unece-ml-pub; unece-genai *(citation unverified)*; unece-ml-wiki *(citation unverified)*], and the UN big-data committee operates task teams including one on training and capacity [uncebd *(citation unverified)*; uncebd-training *(citation unverified)*]. **This is happening whether or not the input–output community engages with it, and WP11 in particular is an IO problem being solved outside the IO literature. The disconnection is itself a finding, and it is a communication failure on both sides: these outputs appear as workshop papers, wiki pages and project deliverables rather than journal articles, so academic IO researchers do not read them.**
- **[F: medium · I: high · Documented gap]** **Peer-review and publication norms.** Journals are the only realistic chokepoint at which reporting standards for AI-produced data can be enforced across a decentralised field, and current norms are demonstrably inadequate at the 2%-code-sharing base rate [kamali26; stodden *(citation unverified)*]. A mandatory reporting checklist — the 32 documented elements plus an AI-content declaration — would be cheap for authors and transformative for the corpus. Software-paper venues are a partial counterweight and are where the field's best-engineered tools are published [stadler21]. **Policies without enforcement change little, reviewers are unpaid and cannot execute code, and confidential data creates legitimate exemptions that become loopholes. Deploying AI-assisted review to fill the enforcement gap would inject fluent-but-wrong criticism at scale.**
- **[F: high · I: medium-high · Verified negative result]** **The absence of a synthesis literature is itself an opportunity.** Across five independent reviews and the principal investigator's own search, **we found no dedicated review of artificial intelligence for input–output economics.** The two systematic reviews of AI in industrial ecology do not identify IO as an adopting subfield [gong25; mensikova26], and the general AI-in-industrial-ecology agenda paper [donati22] predates most of the developments catalogued here. This report is, so far as we can establish, the first attempt at a comprehensive map, and the field would benefit from a peer-reviewed version of it plus a standing shared-task venue. *This negative result rests on bounded searching in blocked-publisher conditions and should be confirmed against Scopus/Web of Science before being asserted in print.*


---


# 15. Cross-cutting enabling conditions

These are the conditions that determine whether the opportunities above are realised, widened and accelerated — or whether the field acquires the appearance of AI adoption without the substance. On the reconciled view of all five reviews, **none of the binding constraints in AI-for-IO are algorithmic.**

**15.1 Data access and confidentiality.** The single largest quality upgrade available to MRIO is replacing proportionality assumptions with observed firm-to-firm transactions, and that data exists — in VAT records, customs declarations and business registers — and is confidential. Every ambitious use case in this report is gated on this condition rather than on method [aiml4os-cros; trusted-smart-stats *(citation unverified)*]. Statistical confidentiality law is jurisdiction-specific and generally strict, and cross-border linking — which is what MRIO needs — is the hardest case of all. **A specific disclosure hazard is under-recognised: supply-chain networks are extremely re-identifiable, because a firm's set of trading partners is close to a fingerprint, and standard cell-suppression disclosure control is inadequate for network data.** Privacy-preserving computation is the only credible technical route around this — federated training of trade-linkage models, differentially private aggregates, secure multiparty computation of cross-border matching statistics, all technically mature and institutionally endorsed [pet4os *(citation unverified)*; dp-smpc-fl *(citation unverified)*; dp-fl-review *(citation unverified)*] — with one structural caveat: **differential-privacy noise interacts badly with balance identities, since a differentially private IO table will not balance and the reconciliation step can undo the guarantee.**

**15.2 Licensing and openness.** The MRIO landscape is split between openly licensed and proprietary databases, and that split — not any technical factor — determines what AI-for-IO research is possible in the open. The clean natural experiment is the field's only open ML benchmark, built exclusively on the openly licensed database because its authors deliberately avoided proprietary inputs in order to release under an open licence, and therefore **structurally incapable of testing the cross-database generalisation that validation actually requires** [exioml-sdata; exioml-arxiv]. **The consequent risk is methodological monoculture: if all open ML work is built on one database, that database's compilation choices become the field's unexamined defaults and every learned model inherits them.** The derived-work status of a *trained model* under a restrictive or share-alike licence is legally unsettled and needs attention.

**15.3 Benchmarks, shared tasks and ground truth.** Fields that have benchmarks make cumulative progress; fields that do not accumulate anecdotes. AI-for-IO has one open benchmark resource, no shared task, no leaderboard, no agreed metric and — most importantly — **no validation benchmark with ground truth**, against an adjacent landscape where data-analysis agents, code agents and text-to-SQL each have several mature benchmark suites [exioml-sdata; dabstep]. The corruption-injection protocol of §11 is the missing piece. A plausible venue pairing is a datasets-and-benchmarks track submission alongside an International Input-Output Association special session.

**15.4 Provenance, metadata and reporting standards.** ML pipelines consume metadata programmatically, and provenance is the mechanism by which model-generated cells stay distinguishable from measured ones — the core mitigation for validation circularity. The pattern exists at the tooling level (JSON metadata stores, modification histories recording aggregations and system changes as first-class objects) but not as a community standard: **there is no community schema for an MRIO comparable to the exchange formats that exist in official statistics and in LCA, and the dominant LCA exchange format has no native IO representation at all** [stadler21; zhao25; ingwersen22; olca-schema]. Per-cell provenance at billions of elements is a genuine storage and tooling problem, and the practical failure mode is that tags exist in the file format and are dropped by every downstream tool.

**15.5 Accounting-consistency machinery as shared infrastructure.** The hard-constraint projection layer described in §7 is not a research result to be published once; it is a *component* that should exist as maintained open software and be a default in every learned pipeline in this field. Combined with the invariant-checking harnesses that already exist, it converts the field's principal objection to machine learning into a solved engineering problem [kkthpinn; useeior; stadler21].

**15.6 Uncertainty as a default rather than a research topic.** Three things must become routine simultaneously: propagation (Monte Carlo with correlated priors, made affordable by the computational methods in §13), calibration (conformal or Bayesian intervals on every model-produced cell), and *display* (uncertainty and provenance as first-class dashboard content). Any one alone fails: propagation without display is invisible, display without propagation is theatre [schulte24; gloria059; cullen24].

**15.7 The institutional bridge.** Covered in §14. The practical recommendation is concrete: the IO research community should read the official-statistics AI literature, adopt its quality framework rather than inventing one, and engage with the work package that is mapping firm-level supply-chain networks — because that work will define the microdata foundation of future IO tables [aiml4os-cros; dumpert23].

**15.8 Skills, careers and interdisciplinary capacity.** The binding constraint is the very small number of people who understand both IO accounting conventions and modern machine learning. **Every characteristic failure catalogued in this report is an interdisciplinary error that neither community would make alone** — post-hoc attribution used where an exact decomposition exists; surrogates replacing exact linear algebra; anomaly detectors treated as error detectors; reduced precision breaking balance checks; basic-price intensities applied to purchaser-price spend. There is no career path for the hybrid role in either community: IO departments do not hire ML engineers and ML groups do not value national-accounting expertise. **The most dangerous training outcome is tool use taught without accounting semantics, which produces confident misuse at scale — and which a good language interface would accelerate** [uncebd-training *(citation unverified)*; aiml4os-cros; wang25].

**15.9 Compute, funding and infrastructure.** MRIO databases are expensive public goods with fragile funding, and AI raises the fixed cost (compute, engineering, ML expertise) while the marginal benefit accrues diffusely to users who do not pay. **Without sustained institutional funding, licensing asymmetry is the predictable equilibrium rather than a policy choice.** Research funders reward novelty, not maintenance, and database compilation is the least fundable and most necessary activity in the field. The four-year multi-country statistical AI programme is a concrete counter-example showing sustained public infrastructure funding is possible in this domain [aiml4os; gloria059].

**15.10 Equity — and why it is a validity condition, not only a fairness condition.** MRIO's largest data gaps are in low- and middle-income countries; those gaps are exactly what ML gap-filling would fill; and the researchers with the local knowledge to judge whether an imputed value is plausible are the ones least likely to have the compute, the licences, the storage or the training to participate. **The least reliable parts of the global system get imputed by the people furthest from them.** The asymmetry is already documented inside the uncertainty data itself: authoritative uncertainty estimates exist for Annex-I countries via national inventory reports and must be substituted from a global gridded product elsewhere, and compilation documentation states plainly that uncertainties are largest in low-income economies and small industries [schulte24; gloria059; gpu-access *(citation unverified)*; compute-north-south *(citation unverified)*]. Combined with validation circularity, this yields footprints that are systematically most confident exactly where they are least warranted. **Concrete levers exist and are cheap: open licensing (demonstrated to work), cloud lab access, compute-frugal methods (tree ensembles are competitive), and lightweight data formats.**

**15.11 Regulation.** EE-IO outputs increasingly feed regulated processes — sustainability reporting, Scope 3 disclosure, border carbon adjustment, green public procurement — and once an AI system produces or materially shapes such a number, obligations on risk management, data governance, technical documentation, human oversight and accuracy may attach [ai-act-art6 *(citation unverified)*; ai-act-annex3 *(citation unverified)*]. **We flag explicitly that the application of AI regulation to environmental accounting is untested and that our reading is an interpretation, not a legal opinion:** a footprint-estimation model is not obviously within the high-risk categories, but ESG scores derived partly from EEIO could reach regulated pathways indirectly, and general-purpose-model transparency obligations are more likely to bite in practice. **The constructive point is that regulatory pressure would force exactly the documentation, uncertainty reporting and human-oversight practices this report argues for on scientific grounds — a rare case where compliance and good science point the same way.** The corresponding risk is a two-tier world in which EU-regulated AI-in-IO carries documentation burdens while equivalent non-EU work does not, distorting the multi-database ensemble and the licensing landscape simultaneously.


---


# 16. Cross-cutting risk register

Stated once, sharply, because these recur across most sections and are more important than any individual use case.

1. **Validation circularity.** The largest systemic risk. When AI generates and AI validates, errors become self-confirming. Mitigation is architectural: exogenous invariants only; genuinely external held-out data; per-cell provenance tags; non-shared training corpora between generating and validating models. Its geographic gradient makes it an equity problem as well as an epistemic one.
2. **Error propagation is non-linear *and correlated*.** An imputed cell enters `L = (I − A)⁻¹` and is amplified through infinite-order chains; worse, a *single model* imputing many cells makes those errors correlated, and correlated errors do not average out in aggregation. **The standard defence — "small errors in a big table wash out" — fails exactly when ML is used.**
3. **Spurious precision.** Sector-level GHG-account CVs near 100% coexist with sectoral footprints published to three significant figures. ML makes it cheap to produce many more such points.
4. **Entrenchment of bias.** Every supervised method learns from where data already exist: rich countries, large listed firms, formal VAT-registered transactions, English-language descriptions, European self-reporting cities. Predictions everywhere else are extrapolations dressed as estimates.
5. **Homogenisation through regularisation.** Low-rank completion, smoothing priors, embedding retrieval and anomaly-driven editing all pull estimates toward a central tendency, systematically erasing the structural distinctiveness of small and unusual economies — the opposite of what regional and development applications require.
6. **Loss of accounting consistency.** The property that makes IO authoritative. Hard-constraint layers are the fix; nothing in Tier 3 should be published without them. **And its inverse: a perfectly balanced but wrong table is more dangerous than an obviously unbalanced one, because balance is the field's main sniff test.**
7. **False comparability.** AI makes it cheap to produce numbers that look alike — same units, same standard name, same schema — while resting on different concordances, vintages, regionalisation choices and primary-data shares. Structured, machine-readable provenance fields are the best available mitigation.
8. **Non-determinism versus assurance.** Re-running an LLM classifier on the same ledger can produce a different total. This is fatal for year-on-year comparability and for assurance, and the obvious mitigations — temperature-zero, seeded, versioned and cached decisions — are barely discussed anywhere.
9. **Split variance and unreported model-selection noise.** A documented 25.82% performance swing across random train/test splits means reported accuracies in this literature should be treated as upper bounds unless split variance is reported.
10. **Transfer failure across statistical systems.** Assume by default that a model fitted in one country's statistical system does not transfer; the firm-network literature demonstrates it directly.
11. **Causal over-reading of feature attributions.** Endemic in the emissions-driver literature; attributions are routinely reported as policy levers with minimal identification discussion.
12. **Assumption laundering through opaque emulators.** An emulator strips away the documentation, caveats and provenance that make a scenario interpretable while keeping its numerical authority.
13. **Sensitive outputs.** Firm-named systemic-risk rankings, critical-link target lists, imputed forced-labour intensities, city footprint rankings, and attributions of specific extinctions or deaths to specific supply chains all carry real-world consequences disproportionate to their validation.
14. **Silent under-detection.** The most insidious pattern: a screening system whose sensitivity is lowest exactly where harm is greatest — NLP forced-labour evidence in repressive jurisdictions being the clearest case — failing in a way that presents as success.
15. **Adversarial and incentive effects.** Where the estimated quantity has financial consequences — customs classification, Scope 3 disclosure, border adjustment — a published model becomes a target for gaming. This risk class is largely absent from the IO literature's vocabulary.
16. **Lock-in and infrastructure fragility.** The reference public EEIO API is archived; the highest-value training data are commercially held; the highest-resolution MRIO lacks an open parser. Vacuums in public infrastructure are filled by proprietary platforms, and the validation norms of §11 then become trade secrets.
17. **Model monoculture and correlated failure.** If every compiler, consultancy and auditor fine-tunes the same two or three foundation models, their errors do not average out — they align. The field's implicit assumption that independent teams provide independent checks fails silently, because the teams are independent and their models are not. This is validation circularity operating at the level of the whole field rather than a single pipeline, and it is the reason assurance by a provider using the same model family as the preparer provides less comfort than it appears to. Mitigation is deliberate diversity of model family and training corpus between the producing and the checking step, stated as a requirement rather than left to procurement.
18. **Deskilling.** The capability to produce and check an estimate by hand is what makes an anomaly noticeable. It is lost by attrition rather than by decision, over a period longer than any single project, and it is unrecoverable on the timescale at which a problem is discovered. The specific hazard is the interaction with the risks above: the staff who would notice a drifting imputation are the ones whose task the imputation replaced. Statistical offices and compilers should treat retained manual capability as infrastructure with a maintenance budget, not as redundancy to be eliminated.
19. **Dual use.** Fine-grained AI-derived estimates are intelligence products as well as analytical ones. Reconstructed firm-level networks name suppliers; chokepoint analyses are target lists as much as resilience assessments; facility-level asset maps derived from geospatial foundation models are commercial reconnaissance. The IO field has no norms for this because, at sector resolution, the question did not arise. It arises now, and the appropriate response is a publication convention — aggregate or withhold identifying detail absent a specific public-interest justification — established before rather than after the first incident.


---


# 17. Prioritised recommendations and next steps

Ordered by expected value per unit of effort and risk, not by intellectual appeal. Items 1–5 are things this project could start immediately; 6–10 require a consortium or an institutional partner; 11–14 are field-level.

**1. Build the accounting-constraint layer and make it a shared component.** Implement a fixed linear projection onto the affine feasible set defined by the IO balance identities, as a differentiable layer usable inside any learned model, released as maintained open software with tests against known-balanced tables. *Why first:* it is a weekend of mathematics and a month of engineering, it removes the field's principal objection to machine learning, and it is a prerequisite for a dozen other items. *Deliverable:* a package plus a short methods paper demonstrating that constrained and unconstrained variants of the same imputation model differ in exactly the way predicted.

**2. Re-implement a reference IO pipeline in an automatic-differentiation framework.** Balancing, inversion, extension multiplication and aggregation in JAX or PyTorch with a custom adjoint for the Leontief inverse. *Why:* no new statistics, no training data, one re-implementation, and it unlocks exact global sensitivity ranking, first-order variance propagation, gradient-based generalised balancing and end-to-end training with the identities inside the computation graph. *Deliverable:* the exact sensitivity of a published national footprint to every coefficient, at the cost of one forward solve — a figure the field has never seen.

**2b. Write down the invariance suite and run it against everything already published.** Rescaling, label permutation, aggregate-then-compute equivalence, Leontief–Ghosh agreement, zero-sector insertion and demand-scaling linearity form a metamorphic test battery that requires no ground truth, no labels and no new data (§11). Applied retrospectively to published ML-in-IO components it would establish, cheaply and for the first time, whether any of them have learned artefacts of table construction rather than economic structure. **This is the lowest-cost item in this list and, on our assessment after the peer comparison, among the three most valuable.**

**2c. Replicate and extend the constraint-embedded generative result to multi-regional scale.** [depretis26] demonstrates a balancing operator inside a learned generator on single-region and world tables; the open question is whether the same construction survives the constraint set, heterogeneous cell reliabilities and trade blocks of a full MRIO, and whether the residual-correction form of [zhao22-dl] or the generative form scales better. Both papers are reproducible in principle; neither has been independently replicated. **A head-to-head replication against GRAS and Improved-RAS baselines, with reported split variance, would be the most useful single empirical paper the field could publish in the next year.**

**2d.
