Extend input-criticality matrices by machine learning and validate against the Belgian benchmark.** The criticality labels — critical / important / non-critical for each input to each sector — are the object that does the work in the best-validated shock model in the literature [alleman23], they are expensive expert elicitations, they exist for almost no country, and they are precisely the kind of judgement an LLM over technical documentation, procurement records and industry standards can plausibly extend. The validation target already exists, which is rare in this field.

**3. Build the missing validation benchmark: an IO corruption-detection task with ground truth.** Inject a catalogue of realistic corruptions into an openly licensed MRIO, publish the corrupted and clean versions, and define recall at fixed false-positive rate as the metric. *Why:* without it, every anomaly-detection and quality-assurance claim in this domain is unfalsifiable, and there is currently no such benchmark anywhere. *Deliverable:* dataset, baselines from the standard detector families, and an honest finding on which error types are invisible to distributional methods. *Pair with:* mining the issue trackers of the main open IO software packages, which are the closest thing to a real labelled error corpus and have never been used.

**4. Build the consumption-based footprint nowcaster, with a real backtest.** Both designs — structural component-wise and reduced-form direct — trained on 20+ years of published footprint series, evaluated by strict vintage-respecting backtest (train on t ≤ T, predict T+1…T+4), reporting calibrated prediction intervals and comparing against the incumbent (extrapolate the last footprint by territorial growth). *Why:* highest expected value of any single item identified; acute and growing policy demand; the incumbent is weak and known to be weak. *Condition:* fix the provenance and revision policy before publishing any number, and verify against Scopus/Web of Science that no such nowcaster already exists.

**5. Attach conformal prediction to every model-produced cell in one complete pipeline, end to end.** Take an existing gap-filling model, wrap it, and report both marginal and Mondrian per-sector, per-income-group coverage. *Why:* near-zero marginal cost, converts unfalsifiable point imputations into checkable empirical claims, and the per-group coverage numbers will expose the differential reliability that recurs throughout this report. *Expect:* the conditional coverage results to be worse than the marginal ones, and publish that.

**6. Build an LLM agent that operates an IO model rather than recalling its numbers.** Tool access to the open MRIO toolbox and the open US EEIO builder, with hard-coded refusal to state a factor it has not computed, automatic parallel execution across two or more databases to expose database sensitivity, and surfacing of the library's own "not recommended for use" flags. *Why:* the most direct architectural antidote to hallucinated emission factors, the largest realistic widening of who can use EEIO, and a natural home for the uncertainty display in item 12. *Guardrail:* evaluate on a purpose-built hard-task suite modelled on existing multi-step data-agent benchmarks, and publish the failure rate rather than the demo.

**7. Test, do not assume, whether MRIO systems are low-rank — then build on the answer.** A spectral-decay study of an openly licensed global MRIO is directly testable, cheap, publishable, and is the precondition for tensor completion, randomised sketching and compression. **Design it to distinguish genuine economic low-rank structure from an artefact of the compilation methods**, by testing on benchmark-year-only data.

**8. Bring randomised numerical linear algebra and multilevel Monte Carlo to MRIO uncertainty.** Sketching to shrink each replicate, aggregated tables as the coarse level in a multilevel scheme, AD-based first-order screening to target the expensive sampling. *Target outcome:* full-resolution uncertainty reporting becomes a default rather than a dedicated research project. *Mandatory check:* report relative error on small cells and small economies, not only global norms.

**9. Build one public concordance benchmark for the process↔IO seam.** Apply the synthetic-data-plus-human-review recipe that worked for spend classification to the ecoinvent↔EXIOBASE correspondence, and include a metric that measures error in the resulting *footprint*, not only label accuracy. *Why:* highest-value, lowest-cost gap identified in the coupling domain, and it unblocks the double-counting work that follows.

**10. Attack double counting in hybridisation with machine learning — the methodological heart of the seam, currently untouched.** Learn the correction factors with uncertainty rather than applying heuristics, on the subset of processes for which both a complete process inventory and a trustworthy IO representation exist. *Expect this to be hard*, because double counting is a definitional problem as much as a statistical one, and say so in the framing.

**11. Adopt the official-statistics quality framework rather than inventing one, and open an institutional channel.** Map its six dimensions onto MRIO compilation, publish the mapping, and engage directly with the statistical work package building firm-level supply-chain networks from company data — because that work will define the microdata foundation of future IO tables whether or not the IO community participates.

**12. Make uncertainty and provenance visible at the point of decision.** Promote existing standard-deviation tables and published correlated uncertainty accounts to first-class objects in standard tooling; add measured / estimated / model-imputed provenance tags that survive to the results layer; and treat display as a design research problem in its own right. *Avoid* uncertainty theatre: bars shown but not propagated into headline numbers or rankings.

**13. Institute an AI-content declaration for database releases and a reporting checklist for papers.** One page per MRIO release: which cells are model-produced, by what model, trained on what, validated how, with what known failure modes. For papers: the existing 32-element reporting framework plus database *version* pinning. **Version drift alone accounts for a −20.97% reproduction deviation, which exceeds most published effect sizes in this literature.**

**14. Publish the synthesis and create a venue.** There is no dedicated review of AI for input–output economics; produce a peer-reviewed version of this map, and pair a datasets-and-benchmarks track submission with an International Input-Output Association special session to create the shared-task venue the field lacks. *Include the cautionary material* — the weather-emulation analogy, the deployment-versus-accuracy gap, validation circularity — because the over-claiming will otherwise be done by others.

**What not to do.** Do not build neural surrogates for the plain Leontief solve; the map is linear, exact fast computation already exists, and the surrogate forfeits auditability against national totals for a speedup that was not needed. Do not apply post-hoc feature attribution where structural path analysis is available. Do not deploy anomaly detection as an automatic editing gate. Do not publish firm-named systemic-risk rankings derived from reconstructed networks. Do not use synthetic data generated by a model to validate a model of the same family. And do not let an emulator be quoted outside the convex hull of its training scenarios.


---


# 18. Research questions targeting the IO–AI nexus

Explicit, answerable questions. **Type**: E = empirical/experimental, M = methodological, I = infrastructure/institutional, N = normative/epistemic. **Priority**: 1 = do first, 3 = important but longer horizon.

| # | Research question | Workflow stage | AI capability | Type | Why it matters | Priority |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | Does embedding IO balance identities as a hard projection layer change what learned imputation models learn, relative to soft penalties — and does it leave *local* violations as the physics-informed literature predicts? | All | Constrained learning | M | Determines whether Tier-3 AI is admissible in IO at all | 1 |
| Q2 | What is the exact gradient of a national consumption-based footprint with respect to every technical coefficient, and does that ranking agree with existing compilation-effort priorities? | Compilation, footprinting | Automatic differentiation | M/E | Would replace Monte Carlo screening and give compilers a triage list they have never had | 1 |
| Q3 | Do global MRIO systems exhibit genuine low-rank spectral decay, and is any observed low-rankness an economic fact or an artefact of RAS and proportionality assumptions? | Handling, computation | Matrix/tensor factorisation | E | Precondition for tensor completion, sketching and compression; testable on open data today | 1 |
| Q4 | Can an ML nowcaster of consumption-based footprints beat the incumbent territorial-growth extrapolation in a strict vintage-respecting backtest, and with what calibrated interval width? | Forward-looking | Supervised + probabilistic | E | Directly attacks the field's binding relevance constraint | 1 |
| Q5 | What are the precision–recall characteristics of IO error detectors against injected corruptions of known type, and which error classes are invisible to distributional outlier detection? | Validation | Unsupervised anomaly detection | E | Without it every quality-assurance claim in this domain is unfalsifiable | 1 |
| Q6 | What is the *conditional* (per-sector, per-income-group) coverage of conformal intervals on ML-imputed IO and LCI cells, as opposed to marginal coverage? | Imputation, validation | Conformal prediction | E | Tests whether calibrated uncertainty survives where it is most needed | 1 |
| Q7 | How much of a spend-based Scope 3 total's error comes from sector misclassification versus price-basis and margin mis-handling, and what is the distribution of footprint error implied by a given top-1 accuracy? | Harmonisation, footprinting | LLM classification | E | Nobody reports this; it determines whether classification accuracy is even the right target | 1 |
| Q8 | Does non-negative matrix factorisation of stacked A-matrices recover interpretable, stable technology archetypes — and do the factors replicate across two independent MRIO databases? | Structure discovery | NMF / dimension reduction | E/M | Would give structural decomposition a data-driven basis instead of an arbitrary factor ordering | 1 |
| Q9 | Can a graph neural network on an MRIO A-matrix do anything the Leontief inverse cannot, once accounting constraints are imposed and the small-N problem is addressed by firm-level transfer or graph-level datasets? | Structure, footprinting | Graph learning | M/E | The central Tier-3 question; the burden of proof sits with the GNN | 2 |
| Q10 | Do learned production functions estimated from transaction data in normal times remain valid during a disruption — and by how much do systemic-risk and shock results move when the learned aggregator replaces the assumed one? | Shocks | Graph learning | E | Replaces the field's largest free assumption, or shows why it cannot be replaced | 2 |
| Q11 | Can learned path-ranking heuristics extend structural path analysis to deeper, wider searches at fixed budget without changing the identity of the top paths — validated against exhaustive enumeration on subsystems? | Footprinting | Learned search | M/E | SPA is what makes footprints actionable, and cost is what limits it | 2 |
| Q12 | Does scenario discovery applied to a large emulator-generated MRIO scenario ensemble produce policy-relevant, robust rule boxes — and how sensitive are they to the peel/paste and threshold choices? | Forward-looking | Rule extraction + emulation | E | The most concrete near-term programme combining emulation with interpretability | 2 |
| Q13 | Can ML estimate hybridisation double-counting corrections with quantified uncertainty, and how much do hybrid footprints move when the heuristic is replaced? | Coupling | Supervised learning | E/M | The methodological heart of the process↔IO seam, currently untouched by ML | 2 |
| Q14 | How large is the monetary–physical reconciliation gap in practice, and can hard-constrained learning close it where physical input–output tables are absent? | Coupling | Constrained learning | M/E | EEIO's deepest structural limitation and a textbook fit for the method | 2 |
| Q15 | Does an LLM agent with tool access to validated IO libraries produce fewer substantive errors on hard multi-step footprint questions than an LLM answering directly — and what is its failure rate on producer-versus-consumer and price-basis distinctions specifically? | Decision support | Tool-using agents | E | Tests the main architectural antidote to hallucinated factors | 2 |
| Q16 | How much do national footprint results change when trade blocks are estimated by a learned model versus the current proportionality assumptions, and where are the changes concentrated? | Estimation | Graph learning | E | Trade blocks are the weakest part of every global table and determine footprint attribution | 2 |
| Q17 | Can cross-database disagreement be predicted from structural and provenance features — and does acting on those predictions reduce error, or only reduce measured disagreement? | Validation | Supervised + attribution | E/N | Tests both the method and its Goodhart failure mode in the same design | 2 |
| Q18 | To what extent is validation circularity already present in existing MRIO pipelines, and can it be measured — for example by tracing which published cells originate in model output from a shared upstream source? | Validation | Meta-research | N/E | The largest systemic risk, and currently entirely unstudied in IO | 2 |
| Q19 | Do ML-imputed satellite accounts systematically regress high-risk country-sectors toward the mean under the not-missing-at-random regime that governs social and environmental reporting — and by how much? | Imputation, indicators | Supervised imputation | E/N | Determines whether imputed social risk is usable at all, and in which direction it fails | 1 |
| Q20 | Does multilevel Monte Carlo using aggregated MRIOs as the coarse level deliver order-of-magnitude savings in footprint uncertainty quantification, and does it preserve tail behaviour? | Computation | Variance reduction | M/E | Compute cost is the stated reason uncertainty reporting is not routine | 2 |
| Q21 | How accurate are randomised sketches of an MRIO for *small-cell* quantities — the footprints of small economies and small sectors — as opposed to global norms? | Computation | Randomised linear algebra | M/E | The method's error concentrates precisely where equity-relevant results live | 2 |
| Q22 | Do ML-based editing and imputation methods being adopted in national statistical offices propagate into supply-use tables in ways detectable downstream, and do they create cross-country incomparability? | Compilation, validation | Institutional + drift detection | I/E | A new and hard-to-detect error channel entering IO from upstream | 2 |
| Q23 | Can differentially private or federated estimation of cross-border firm-level linkages produce MRIO trade blocks of usable accuracy, given that DP noise breaks balance identities? | Data access | Privacy-preserving ML | M/E | The only credible technical route around the confidentiality constraint | 3 |
| Q24 | Can generative models produce synthetic IO tables that satisfy the accounting identities *and* reproduce out-of-sample structural statistics they were not trained to preserve? | Compilation | Generative modelling | M/E | Would unlock open method development; the validation criterion is the hard part | 3 |
| Q25 | Does symbolic regression over IO primitives rediscover known indicators, and can it discover a short formula that predicts realised disruption impact better than existing composite indices out of sample and across databases? | Indicators | Symbolic regression | E/M | Produces artefacts the field can actually adopt — equations, not black boxes | 3 |
| Q26 | Under what conditions, if any, does an IO-based comparative-static exercise admit a causal interpretation — and can causal ML estimates of realised policy effects be legitimately used to parameterise IO scenarios? | Forward-looking | Causal ML | N/M | The most important conceptual boundary between IO practice and causal inference | 2 |
| Q27 | Which aggregation of an MRIO flow network preserves the most footprint-relevant structure for a given question, and how much do Sankey-level conclusions change across defensible aggregations? | Communication | Learned aggregation | M/E | Aggregation choice is unreported and materially changes what a figure appears to show | 3 |
| Q28 | Does prospective-database construction that updates only IAM-mapped sectors, leaving the rest frozen at present-day technology, systematically bias forward-looking footprints — and by how much? | Forward-looking | Scenario coupling | E | A strong, largely undiscussed assumption inside widely used prospective tooling | 2 |
| Q29 | What is the compute and energy footprint of AI-augmented IO research, and does the tree-ensembles-match-deep-learning finding generalise across IO tasks? | Computation | Reflexive accounting | E/N | Self-consistency for the discipline that measures everyone else's footprint | 3 |
| Q30 | Does an AI-assisted method-choice sensitivity analysis change assurance conclusions on real corporate EEIO-based disclosures — i.e. would a different reasonable choice have produced a materially different number? | Integration | Agentic re-performance | E/I | The most valuable and most neglected check on EEIO-based disclosure | 2 |
| Q31 | Does a metamorphic invariance suite (unit rescaling, label permutation, aggregate-then-compute, Leontief–Ghosh agreement, zero-sector insertion) detect failures in published ML-in-IO components that conventional accuracy metrics do not? | All | All | M | It is the only validation route that needs no ground truth, and the field's ground-truth problem otherwise has no answer. | 1 |
| Q32 | Does the constraint-embedded generative architecture of [depretis26] survive extension to a full multi-regional table with trade blocks and heterogeneous cell reliabilities, and does it beat the residual-correction form of [zhao22-dl] on the same data? | Compilation | Generative + optimisation | E | The two strongest demonstrated results in the field have never been compared or replicated, and their scaling behaviour decides which is the template. | 1 |
| Q33 | Can input-criticality matrices be extended by machine learning from documentation, procurement and standards text, and does the extension reproduce the Belgian validation performance of survey-elicited labels [alleman23]? | Shocks | LLM extraction | E | It is the learnable object in the best-validated shock model in the literature, and a validation target already exists. | 1 |
| Q34 | When gap-fillers are trained with impact-weighted rather than cell-weighted losses, how much does footprint error fall relative to the cell-level accuracy that is currently reported and optimised? | Imputation | Supervised | M | Every accuracy figure in this literature optimises a quantity that is not the one users care about. | 1 |
| Q35 | How much of the divergence between MRIO databases is attributable to satellite accounts versus economic structure once both are harmonised, on current vintages rather than 2014 data [moran-wood14; owen16]? | Harmonisation | — | E | It determines where harmonisation effort, AI-assisted or not, should be spent. | 2 |
| Q36 | Do independently developed ML components in IO compilation exhibit correlated errors when they share a foundation model or a pretraining corpus, and by how much does that reduce the effective independence of cross-checks? | All | Foundation models | E | It tests whether model monoculture (§16) is a real mechanism in this field or a borrowed worry. | 2 |
| Q37 | Under adversarial conditions — a reporter optimising disclosures against a known classifier — what is the measured degradation of spend-to-sector and disclosure-screening accuracy? | Harmonisation, integration | LLM/classification | E | No accuracy figure in this literature is measured under the incentive conditions in which the systems will actually operate. | 2 |
| Q38 | Does active learning over survey and verification budget, targeted at variance reduction in published indicators, outperform current allocation heuristics in a statistical office's actual workflow? | Compilation | Active learning | E | It is one of very few AI applications that reduces rather than adds epistemic risk, and it is directly testable inside an existing institution. | 2 |


---

# 19. Method note, evidence-status conventions, and known gaps in this review

**How this report was produced.** Five researchers reviewed the literature independently on partitioned domains — (A) the data pipeline from raw sources to a balanced harmonised table; (B) static and structural analysis; (C) forward-looking analysis and dynamic modelling; (D) coupling and integration with other models, disciplines and industry workflows; (E) validation, computation and cross-cutting enabling conditions — each producing a dossier of use-case entries in a fixed schema (use case; workflow step; AI paradigm and why it fits the mathematics; evidence status; feasibility and impact; benefits; limitations; risks; sources). The principal investigator conducted an independent search in parallel. The five dossiers together contain roughly 190 distinct entries and were then compared entry by entry; overlapping entries were merged, contradictory ratings adjudicated by majority with the minority position recorded in the text where it was substantive, and claims that only one reviewer made were retained only where the underlying source could be checked. Where reviewers converged independently on the same judgement — for example that a surrogate for a linear model is usually the wrong tool, that structural path analysis should be preferred to post-hoc attribution, that hard accounting constraints are the key missing transfer, and that validation circularity is the largest systemic risk — that convergence is noted, because independent agreement is the strongest signal this method produces.

**Evidence-status conventions.** *Proven in IO* means published work applying the technique to actual IO, MRIO or EEIO objects. *Adjacent* means published work in a neighbouring field (process LCA, official statistics, firm-level supply-chain ML, climate and energy emulation, numerical linear algebra) that transfers with moderate effort but has not been applied to IO. *Demonstrated in practice* means vendor, standards-body or agency evidence, which is self-reported, without published methodology, held-out benchmark or independent replication, and is labelled as such wherever it appears. *SPECULATION* marks a proposal of our own with no implementation known to us; these are flagged inline and are not presented as findings.

**Verification discipline and its limits.** No DOI, author list or title in this report was invented. Sources that a reviewer opened and read are cited without qualification; sources whose title, venue and DOI were confirmed in an authoritative index but whose full text could not be retrieved are marked *(citation unverified)*, and statements about their content are kept deliberately thin. Several major publishers returned HTTP 403 repeatedly during this work, which is the single largest constraint on the review: institutional access would remove it quickly and should be used before any of this is submitted for publication.

**Second-edition procedure.** Eleven reports produced independently by other systems to the identical brief were read in full and diffed against the first edition. Every claim they contained and this report did not was triaged by expected value, and the high-value ones were resolved to a primary source and checked before adoption; claims whose identifiers did not resolve were rejected rather than softened, and claims whose evidence status could not be substantiated were adopted at a lower status or not at all. Seven new sources entered the reference list on this basis. The audit trail is §21. **One limitation of that procedure should be stated plainly: it inherits the search coverage of twelve systems that share training data and are likely to share blind spots, so convergence across all twelve is weaker evidence of completeness than it appears.** The three items adopted with the largest effect on this report's conclusions — [zhao22-dl], [depretis26] and [alleman23] — were each found by only two or three of the twelve, which suggests the tail of relevant literature is longer than any of the reports reached.

**Known gaps in this review, stated so that others can close them.** Coverage of conference proceedings — in particular the International Input-Output Association's — is thin, and we suspect more machine-learning work in input–output lives there than in journals. A systematic full-text sweep of arXiv was blocked. Two claims raised in the peer reports could not be verified in time and are recorded as rejected-pending-source in §21.1 rather than silently dropped: a specific 68.4% manual-review-workload reduction attributed to automated coding in official statistics, and a *Scientific Reports* identifier for a graph-augmented transformer on supply-chain disruption that did not resolve. Bibliometric confirmation via OpenAlex, Scopus or Web of Science was not completed, so **all negative results in this report — that no ML footprint nowcaster exists, that structural path analysis and machine learning have an empty intersection, that no anomaly detection has been applied to coefficient matrices, that no automatic differentiation has been applied to an MRIO pipeline, that no randomised linear algebra has been applied to MRIO, that no MRIO release ships a model card, and that no dedicated review of AI for input–output economics exists — rest on bounded searching and should be verified before being asserted in print.** Eurostat's own MRIO is under-covered relative to its importance. The EEIO–health and nutrition literature could not be verified at all and is flagged in §10 as a lead rather than a finding. Vendor evidence was examined for three suppliers only. And the legal status of a model trained on a restrictively licensed MRIO, and of a randomised sketch of one, was raised twice and resolved nowhere.


---

# 21. Comparison against eleven independently produced reports

Eleven reports written to the identical brief by other systems were read in full and diffed against the first edition of this report. The comparison is worth reporting rather than silently absorbing, because it is itself evidence about the state of the field: where twelve independent searches converge, the literature is probably real; where one search finds something eleven others missed, it is either a genuine discovery or a citation that does not survive checking, and the two are distinguishable only by going to the source. We did that for every claim adopted below. Two of the resulting additions — [zhao22-dl] and [depretis26] — are, in our assessment, more important than anything in the first edition's own novel content, and their absence from it was a real failure of search rather than a difference of judgement.

The distribution of the overlap is informative. Agreement is near-total on the *taxonomy* — every report independently arrived at something close to compilation → analysis → coupling → validation → computation — and on the *headline opportunities*: spend classification, gap-filling, nowcasting, network reconstruction, LLM interfaces, emulation. Agreement collapses on *evidence status*. The same handful of papers is variously described as proven, emerging and speculative across the reports, and several reports assign "proven in IO" to method families for which we could locate no IO application at all. Since the brief asked specifically for proven-versus-speculative discrimination, that divergence is the single most consequential finding of this comparison, and it is treated explicitly in §21.3.


## 21.1 Opportunities claimed in other reports but not in ours

Each row states the claim, where it came from, what verification returned, and the adoption decision with its reason. "Adopted" means the claim is now integrated into the body of this report at the section indicated; "adopted as caution" means it enters the risk register rather than the opportunity map; "rejected" means we could not substantiate it, or we could substantiate the underlying work but not the evidence status the claiming report assigned to it.

| # | Claim | Raised by | Verification outcome | Decision and reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Residual DNN correction on top of RAS raises US table R² from 0.64 to 0.87 and cuts median APE from 37% to 11% [zhao22-dl] | Sonnet 5, GPT 5.6 Luna, Gemini 3.6 | **Confirmed at source** (peer-reviewed, *Environmental Science & Technology*, with an end-to-end carbon-footprint case study) | **Adopted (§1, §0).** The strongest demonstrated ML-on-IO result located anywhere. Its residual architecture is now the report's recommended template. Our omission was a search failure. |
| 2 | GAN with an IPF layer inside the generator plus random-forest boosting beats Improved-RAS on regional and world tables [depretis26] | Opus 5, Qwen3.8-Max | **Confirmed at source** (*Scientific Reports* 16:10893) | **Adopted (§1, §0, matrix).** Closes what we had listed as a speculation — hard constraints inside a learned generator — and does so in the IO setting. Materially changes the report's Tier 3 argument. |
| 3 | A dynamic IO shock model validated out-of-sample on Belgian pandemic data, with survey-elicited input-criticality labels doing the work [alleman23] | GPT 5.1, Kimi K2.6 | **Confirmed at source**; 4.69% value-weighted mean absolute deviation; B2B demand reduction overestimated by up to 13% | **Adopted (§7).** The criticality label matrix, not the production function, is the learnable object — a target we had missed entirely and now rate among the highest-value executable items. |
| 4 | Geospatial foundation models map ~9,300 Brazilian soy facilities at >90% confirmation accuracy in support of EUDR [trase-ai; trase-fm] | Gemini 3.6, GLM-3.5 | **Confirmed**, but the accuracy figure appears only in a press release; the technical note reports ~12,000 candidate detections before cross-referencing and publishes no precision/recall | **Adopted with the caveat stated (§10).** The asset-location layer is the genuine missing middle between IO averages and due-diligence evidence. The accuracy claim is reported as vendor-grade, not peer-reviewed. |
| 5 | Random-forest emulation of an agent-based regional model: 11,076 real runs → 1,000,000 emulated runs across 46 metropolitan regions [furtado22] | Mistral Medium 3.5, Deepseek V4 Pro | **Confirmed at source** | **Adopted (§8).** Not an IO paper, but the closest published analogue of the ensemble-plus-scenario-discovery programme this report recommends, and it quantifies what emulation buys. |
| 6 | A published LLM interface to footprint data exists (the "Carbon Footprint Wizard") [cf-wizard] | Kimi K2.6, GLM-3.5 | **Confirmed** (arXiv:2509.07733); restricted to food products | **Adopted, and our own claim withdrawn (§12).** We had asserted that no published LLM interface to footprint databases existed. That was too strong. The narrower claim — no evaluated interface over a general-purpose MRIO — survives. |
| 7 | Bayesian IO table updating under a benchmark LASSO prior, applied to a 196×196 US table for 2012 [bayes-lasso] | Opus 5, GPT 5.1, Qwen3.8-Max | **Confirmed at source** (Tsionas, *Economic Systems Research* 32(3):413–427) | **Adopted; our "citation unverified" flag removed (§8).** This is the classical benchmark any learned updater must beat, and several peer reports were right to foreground it more than we did. |
| 8 | Metamorphic testing — checking invariances (unit rescaling, label permutation, aggregate-then-disaggregate consistency) rather than point accuracy | GPT 5.1, Sonnet 5 | Established in software engineering and ML testing; no IO application found | **Adopted as a method transfer (§11, §17, §22).** This is the best answer anyone offered to the ground-truth problem: IO has exact, cheap, known invariances, so a model can be tested hard without any labelled truth. We regard this as the most valuable *conceptual* import from the eleven reports. |
| 9 | Impact-weighted loss functions: train gap-fillers to minimise error in the footprint, not error in the cell | Deepseek V4 Pro, Muse Spark 1.2 | No published IO instance found; the principle is standard decision-focused learning | **Adopted as speculation (§2, §11).** Correct and cheap. Cell-level R² is the wrong objective when a handful of high-multiplier cells dominate the output, and every accuracy figure in this literature optimises the wrong thing. |
| 10 | Active learning to allocate scarce survey and verification budget by expected variance reduction in the published indicator | GPT 5.6 Luna | No IO instance found; mature elsewhere | **Adopted as speculation (§11, §22).** It converts "where should the statistical office spend its next euro?" into a computable question, and it is one of the few AI uses that reduces rather than adds epistemic risk. |
| 11 | Automating the pedigree matrix / data-quality scoring of inventory entries | Muse Spark 1.2, Mistral Medium 3.5 | Deterministic and semi-automated implementations exist; no learned version evaluated | **Adopted as speculation (§11).** Consistency, not accuracy, is the gain, exactly as UNECE found for coding. Flagged with the obvious hazard: a learned pedigree score inherits the biases of whoever scored the training set. |
| 12 | Inverse optimisation to recover the objective functions firms actually pursued during 2020–22 disruptions | GPT 5.1 | No application found in IO or industrial ecology | **Adopted as speculation (§7).** Intellectually the most interesting proposal in the eleven reports: it turns rationing rules from an assumption into an estimand. Rated low feasibility — it needs firm-level allocation data almost nobody has. |
| 13 | Question-specific / adaptive aggregation: choose the sector aggregation to suit the query rather than fixing it at compilation | Qwen3.8-Max, Deepseek V4 Pro | Aggregation-error literature is established; the adaptive framing is new | **Adopted as speculation (§12).** Attractive and dangerous in equal measure: it makes results legible but destroys comparability between two analyses of the same system, so it must carry a declared aggregation fingerprint. |
| 14 | Dynamic discovery of latent sectors — letting the data, not the classification, define the production units | Muse Spark 1.2, GLM-3.5 | No IO application found | **Adopted, downgraded (§4).** Scientifically appealing, institutionally near-impossible: a table whose sectors are not NACE/ISIC cannot enter official statistics, cannot be concorded, and cannot be compared across years. Listed as a research instrument, not a compilation route. |
| 15 | Model monoculture: everyone fine-tuning the same handful of foundation models produces correlated, non-averaging errors across the field | Sonnet 5, GPT 5.1 | Documented in the algorithmic-monoculture literature; no IO study | **Adopted as caution (§16).** It is the mechanism by which our own validation-circularity risk goes global, and it sharpens that entry rather than duplicating it. |
| 16 | Adversarial gaming: once classifiers and screeners are known, reporters optimise disclosures against them | GPT 5.1, Opus 5, Kimi K2.6 | Documented in adversarial ML; no IO/ESG-specific study found | **Adopted as caution (§16, §3, §10).** Mandated reporting creates a direct financial incentive to be classified into a low-intensity sector. No accuracy metric in this literature is measured under adversarial conditions. |
| 17 | Deskilling: statistical offices and consultancies lose the capacity to produce or check what the model produces | Mistral Medium 3.5, Muse Spark 1.2 | Not empirically studied in this setting | **Adopted as caution (§16).** Slow-acting and irreversible, and it interacts with validation circularity: the humans who would catch the drift are the ones automated away first. |
| 18 | Dual use: fine-grained AI-estimated IO and firm-network data as commercial or state intelligence | Opus 5, GLM-3.5 | Analytically clear; no incident literature | **Adopted as caution (§16, §4, §10).** Already implicit in our warning about naming systemically important firms from reconstructed networks; now stated as a distinct risk class covering chokepoint maps and facility-level asset registers. |
| 19 | Topological data analysis (persistent homology) of IO networks | Deepseek V4 Pro | Method mature; no IO application found | **Adopted at low priority (§4).** We can construct no question that TDA answers on an IO table which community detection, centrality and structural path analysis do not answer more interpretably. Recorded for completeness, rated low impact. |
| 20 | Physics-informed GNNs with conservation-of-flow constraints on production networks | Sonnet 5, Qwen3.8-Max | Method demonstrated in adjacent domains; no IO instance | **Adopted (§4, §13).** Consistent with, and now partly evidenced by, [depretis26]. Folded into the report's constraint-embedding argument rather than listed separately. |
| 21 | Hybrid RAS + real-coded genetic algorithm; multidimensional RAS variants | Qwen3.8-Max, GLM-3.5 | Published, minor literature | **Adopted, rated low (§1).** These are optimisation refinements of biproportional updating, not learning. They matter as baselines: a learned updater that cannot beat GRAS plus a good heuristic has demonstrated nothing. |
| 22 | ">70% of ML-in-LCA studies train on fewer than 1,500 samples" | GPT 5.6 Luna, Mistral Medium 3.5 | Consistent with the review literature; the precise share is a single secondary citation we could not trace to a primary count | **Adopted as an order-of-magnitude discipline statistic (§15), attributed as approximate.** It is the correct rebuttal to every "deep learning will solve this" claim in the neighbouring field. |
| 23 | Large-scale ESG/sustainability disclosure extraction (≈600 European firms, 2014–2023, ~2.9M observations); domain-tuned disclosure LLMs reporting F1 > 0.82 | GPT 5.1, Deepseek V4 Pro, Kimi K2.6 | Extraction work exists and is credible; the specific F1 figures are single-source and not comparably benchmarked | **Adopted as adjacent, with the metrics quarantined (§10).** Disclosure extraction is the most plausible near-term route to firm-level environmental extensions, but reported F1 on a private label set is not evidence of usable accuracy, and extraction quality correlates with firm size and reporting maturity — a bias that maps directly onto footprint outcomes. |
| 24 | Post-harmonisation convergence: aligning satellite accounts brings MRIO consumption-based carbon estimates within roughly 10% for major economies, with the emissions vector rather than the economic structure as the dominant divergence source | Opus 5, GPT 5.1 | **Confirmed in substance** by the established convergence and structural-path-decomposition literature already cited here [moran-wood14; owen16] | **Adopted as a sharpened statement (§3, §11).** The finding is a decade old and better established than the peer reports implied, and it has a direct operational consequence we now state: **effort spent harmonising extensions dominates effort spent harmonising economic structures**, which reorders the AI priorities in harmonisation. |
| 25 | ARIO-class rigidity overestimates indirect losses relative to CGE benchmarks | GPT 5.1, Sonnet 5 | Consistent with the disaster-IO literature and independently corroborated by the Belgian validation's B2B overestimate | **Adopted as caution (§7).** Strengthens the point that shock-model *bounds* — Leontief above, perfect-reallocation LP below — are the honest output, not point forecasts. |
| 26 | Upstreamness / downstreamness computable from partial information without full inversion | Qwen3.8-Max | Confirmed (arXiv:2009.06350) | **Adopted (§13).** A useful reminder that several IO quantities have cheap approximations, which is the correct baseline against which any learned surrogate for the Leontief inverse must be judged. |
| 27 | Foundational network-economics results (aggregate fluctuations from sectoral shocks; IO vertex centralities) belong in the map | GPT 5.1, Opus 5, Mistral Medium 3.5 | Confirmed, long established | **Not adopted as AI use cases; acknowledged as prerequisites (§4).** These are network science, not AI, and including them would inflate the apparent evidence base for "AI in IO" — precisely the error this report exists to avoid. |
| 28 | Automated coding in official statistics reduces manual review workload by 68.4% | GLM-3.5 | **Not verified.** The UNECE and national auto-coding evidence we could confirm reports accuracy rates, not a workload-reduction percentage of this form; we could not locate the source | **Rejected pending a source.** The underlying phenomenon — threshold-based auto-accept with clerical routing — is well evidenced and already in §3; the specific number is not, and precise unsourced figures are how this literature acquires false authority. |
| 29 | A graph-augmented transformer for supply-chain disruption prediction, *Scientific Reports* s41598-026-61049-w | Gemini 3.6 | **Not verified.** The identifier did not resolve on repeated attempts | **Rejected pending verification.** The method class is plausible and is already represented in §4 and §7 by verifiable work; a claim resting on an identifier that does not resolve does not enter the report. |
| 30 | "GAT-IO models" for input–output analysis, labelled proven | Gemini 3.6 | **Rejected.** No such literature located; the supporting citations describe firm-level supply-chain GNNs with no IO content | **Rejected as an evidence-status error.** Graph attention over an IO table is a good idea — it is one of ours (§4) — but it is speculative, and labelling it proven would be exactly the failure mode §21.3 identifies. |
| 31 | Transformer plus structural VAR forecasting of technical coefficients, labelled proven | Gemini 3.6 | **Rejected.** Sources found describe macroeconomic time-series forecasting, not coefficient matrices | **Rejected as an evidence-status error; the underlying opportunity retained as speculation (§8).** |
| 32 | Bayesian-neural-network uncertainty quantification for MRIO, labelled proven | Gemini 3.6, Muse Spark 1.2 | **Rejected as "proven".** The demonstrated MRIO UQ literature is Monte Carlo and Bayesian-balancing based [schulte24; schulte26-bayes], not BNN | **Downgraded to adjacent (§1, §11).** BNNs are a reasonable candidate; nobody has done it on an MRIO. |
| 33 | Language models generating input–output tables directly from text or economic description | Muse Spark 1.2 | No implementation; no plausible mechanism for satisfying the accounting identities | **Rejected.** This is the clearest example in the eleven reports of a capability asserted from the fluency of the generating system rather than from evidence. A table so produced would balance only by accident and would encode the model's priors about economies as data. |
| 34 | Blockchain-plus-AI supply-chain traceability as an IO data source | GLM-3.5, Muse Spark 1.2 | Not an AI method; the data-availability constraint is legal and commercial, not technical | **Rejected as out of scope.** Included here because two reports raised it and readers will encounter the claim: the binding constraint on firm-level IO data is confidentiality and incentive, and no ledger technology changes that. |
| 35 | "Digital twin of the economy" as a target architecture | Muse Spark 1.2, Gemini 3.6, Kimi K2.6 | A framing, not a method | **Rejected as a term; the components retained.** Everything worth doing under this banner appears in §§7–9 under names that carry falsifiable content. The phrase's function in practice is to license extrapolation that none of the underlying components support. |

Three of these decisions deserve more than a table row.

**On the two adopted empirical results.** [zhao22-dl] and [depretis26] between them change this report's central empirical claim. The first edition argued that AI's demonstrated contribution to IO was concentrated in classification and gap-filling, that the measured accuracies there were mediocre, and that the field should therefore be modest. The measured accuracies in classification remain mediocre and the modesty stands, but it must now be stated with a distinction: **estimating a number inside a known accounting structure is a task machine learning does well on IO objects, and the two published demonstrations of it are strong.** Both work because the learned component is subordinated to the accounting — a residual on top of RAS in one case, a projection layer inside the generator in the other. Neither is a case of learning replacing the model. That is not a caveat on the result; it is the result.

**On metamorphic validation.** The single largest structural obstacle identified in the first edition was the absence of ground truth: there is no true IO table against which an estimate can be scored, and the tables that exist are partly model output. Several peer reports proposed, independently of each other, that this is the wrong frame — that a model can be tested against *invariances* it must satisfy regardless of truth. Input–output analysis is unusually rich in these: rescaling all monetary units must leave every physical footprint unchanged; permuting sector labels must permute results identically; aggregating two sectors and then computing must equal computing and then aggregating for any linear indicator; a footprint computed from the Leontief and Ghosh forms must agree where both are defined; adding a zero-output sector must change nothing. Each is cheap to test, requires no labels, and fails loudly when a learned component has picked up a spurious dependence. We rate this the most valuable idea imported from the eleven reports and have written it into §11, §17 and §22 accordingly.

**On the evidence-status failures.** Four claims (rows 30–33) were labelled "proven" or "tried" by reports that, on checking, were resting on sources describing something else — firm-level supply-chain GNNs with no IO content, macroeconomic forecasting rather than coefficient estimation, or nothing at all. The pattern is not random: in every case the mislabelled claim was one where the *method* is well established elsewhere and the *application* is not, and the reports collapsed the two. That is the specific error this report's evidence-status tagging exists to prevent, and its recurrence across independent systems is a warning about AI-assisted literature review as a practice — including this one. The mitigation used here is the only one we know of: every adopted claim was resolved to a primary source and read, and claims whose identifiers did not resolve were rejected rather than softened.


## 21.2 Opportunities identified here and in none of the eleven other reports

The following appear, so far as the diff shows, only in this report. We list them not as a priority claim but because unique findings are the ones most likely to be either the most valuable content or the most mistaken, and both possibilities deserve exposure.

- **The Leontief–GNN isomorphism as an organising theorem, not an analogy.** Several reports observe that GNNs and IO both involve networks. None states the identity: `L = Σ_k A^k` *is* weighted *k*-hop message passing, so the Leontief model is a linear GNN with identity weights and no nonlinearity, and every "AI replaces part of the model" proposal is a specific relaxation of one of those three restrictions. This is what makes the Tier 3 discussion a structured question rather than a list of things one could try.
- **Automatic differentiation of the whole IO pipeline, with the Leontief adjoint stated.** `dL = L dA L` gives the exact sensitivity of any footprint to every coefficient at roughly the cost of one forward solve. No other report proposes differentiating an MRIO pipeline; we found no published instance either, which makes this both unique and untested.
- **Randomised numerical linear algebra and multilevel Monte Carlo as the correct computational frame for MRIO.** Sketching, selected inversion, Woodbury/Sherman–Morrison updating for perturbed tables, and neural preconditioners appear nowhere else in the twelve reports. The IO field solves the same large sparse system thousands of times with slightly different right-hand sides and never exploits that structure.
- **Low-rank tensor completion over the (country × sector × sector × year) panel, with the prior question stated as testable.** Other reports propose imputation; none proposes the panel-completion framing, and none asks the question that decides whether it can work at all: **does a global MRIO exhibit low-rank spectral decay?** That is answerable in an afternoon on open EXIOBASE data and appears never to have been published.
- **Gromov–Wasserstein optimal transport for structural alignment between GMRIO sector spaces.** The alignment problem is universally acknowledged; this is the only proposal in the twelve reports for the case where labels are uninformative and only relational structure is available.
- **Conformal prediction on IO cells, with the marginal-versus-conditional trap stated.** Two reports mention conformal prediction generically. None notes that marginal coverage guarantees are compatible with catastrophic conditional failure — 90% overall and 40% for African agriculture — which is precisely the failure mode that matters for a database whose equity claims rest on its coverage of data-poor regions.
- **Validation circularity as a named, structural, geographically graded systemic risk with an architectural mitigation.** Other reports raise circularity as a caution. This report treats it as the field's largest systemic risk, enumerates five instances already visible in the published literature, states that it is worst exactly where the equity stakes are highest, and specifies mitigations (provenance tags carried to results; exogenous-invariant validation; non-shared training corpora between imputer and validator) rather than exhortation.
- **The purchasers'-versus-basic-prices error in spend-based accounting.** No other report identifies that applying basic-price EEIO intensities to purchaser-price ledger data overstates footprints by the trade-and-transport margin plus taxes — plausibly 20–60% in retail-heavy categories — and that this error is completely invisible to every classification accuracy metric in the literature, because those metrics score labels, not valuation. We continue to regard this as the largest unquantified systematic error in commercial Scope 3 practice.
- **The noise floor as a screening criterion.** Median sector-level coefficients of variation of 94% (CO₂), 100% (CH₄) and 113% (N₂O) [schulte24] imply that most proposed ML improvements are not detectable in the output at all. No other report applies a detectability test to its own recommendations. We suggest it become a standard question asked of any proposal in this area.
- **The three-tier epistemic-risk stratification** (AI upstream of the algebra / AI as computational infrastructure / AI replacing part of the model), which is what allows feasibility and impact to be traded against risk rather than reported alongside it.
- **The weather-emulation category error, stated pre-emptively.** GraphCast-style successes will be cited in support of ML-for-IO. The disanalogy is precise — 10⁶–10⁹ complete observations of a stationary non-reflexive system versus roughly 25 partly modelled observations of a non-stationary reflexive one — and saying so before the analogy is made is cheaper than rebutting it afterwards.
- **Anomaly detection on coefficient matrices as a verified absence.** Editing and outlier detection are standard in official statistics and absent from MRIO compilation; the gap is specific and was checked.
- **Eurostat AIML4OS WP11 as an input–output problem being solved outside the input–output literature**, and the corollary that ML-derived estimates will enter supply-use tables through the official-statistics channel whether or not the research community engages — with MRIO compilers unable to tell which figures are model output.
- **olca-schema's lack of any native IO/EEIO representation**, as a concrete, fixable interoperability failure after decades of hybridisation research.
- **Learned double-counting correction factors in hybridisation**, distinguished from the much easier and much-discussed correspondence-matching problem, and identified as the harder and more valuable of the two.
- **The licensing asymmetry argument**: the field's only open ML benchmark could be built only on the one openly licensed database, and therefore structurally cannot test cross-database generalisation — so the licensing regime, not the method, caps what can be known about robustness.
- **Symbolic regression for indicator discovery**, and **semantic entropy for detecting fluent-but-wrong IO narratives**, neither of which appears elsewhere in the twelve.
- **"One graph observed twenty-five times"** as the honest description of the MRIO data situation, against the "big data" framing several other reports adopt.

We also note one asymmetry worth recording. This report was the only one of the twelve to state a negative result as a headline finding — that no dedicated review of AI for input–output economics appears to exist. That claim survived the diff: none of the eleven cited such a review either, which is weak corroboration from eleven independent searches.


## 21.3 Where the twelve reports agree, and where they conflict

**Near-unanimous agreement.** The workflow taxonomy; that compilation and harmonisation are where AI has actually landed; that spend-to-sector classification is the highest-volume deployed application; that nowcasting and timeliness are IO's most tractable AI-addressable weakness; that LLM interfaces could widen access; that the absence of benchmarks and ground truth is the binding constraint; that confidentiality blocks firm-level data; and that hard accounting constraints must be preserved. On these, twelve independent reviews converging is meaningful evidence.

**Systematic divergence on evidence status.** This is the substantive conflict. Reports disagree not about what exists but about how established it is, and the disagreement is not symmetric: it runs almost entirely in the direction of over-crediting. The mechanism, visible in every instance we checked, is conflation of a method proven in an adjacent field with the same method applied to IO. Our recommendation to readers of any report in this genre, including this one, is to treat "proven" as a claim requiring a resolvable identifier and to check it.

**Divergence on the value of Tier 3.** Reports split roughly evenly between treating learned production functions, GNN propagation and economy-scale emulation as the frontier and treating them as a category error. This report takes the middle position — justified only where the linear model demonstrably fails, and only with hard constraints attached — and [depretis26] is evidence that the middle position is constructible rather than merely rhetorical.

**Divergence on quantification discipline.** Only a minority of the reports quote a measured accuracy anywhere. Those that do converge on roughly the same numbers, which is reassuring; those that do not tend to be the ones carrying the mislabelled evidence statuses. There is a lesson in that correlation for how work in this area should be written.

**One near-universal blind spot.** Eleven of the twelve reports, including the first edition of this one in part, discuss AI's contribution to producing *estimates* and give far less attention to AI's contribution to *checking* them. Validation is the smallest section in most of the reports and the largest unmet need in the field. §22 is weighted accordingly.


---


# 22. What each actor at the AI–IO nexus should do, and when

Horizons are defined as **short** (0–18 months, executable with existing data, funding and staff), **medium** (2–4 years, requiring a project, a consortium or a budget line), and **long** (5–10 years, requiring institutional or regulatory change). Items are written as actions, not aspirations, and each is chosen so that failure to complete it is observable.

Two design rules run through all of it. First, **subordinate the learned component to the accounting**: the demonstrated successes ([zhao22-dl], [depretis26]) both put the classical estimator inside the architecture rather than beside it, and the failures cluster where a learned model was asked to produce a quantity nothing constrains. Second, **build the checking capacity before the producing capacity**. The field's largest risk is not that AI produces bad numbers; it is that it produces plausible numbers faster than anyone can verify them, and that the verification is done by the same model family that produced them.


## 22.1 National statistical offices and official-statistics programmes

**Short term.** Publish a provenance flag with every supply-use and IO cell recording whether it is surveyed, administratively sourced, deterministically derived, or model-imputed — this is the single cheapest action any actor in this map can take and it unblocks almost everything downstream, including honest uncertainty and non-circular validation. Adopt bounded automation as the standard pattern for coding and editing — auto-accept above a published confidence threshold, route the remainder to human coders, publish the threshold and the measured error rates by class, following the UNECE evidence [unece-class]. Introduce metamorphic tests (unit rescaling, label permutation, aggregate-then-compute equivalence) into existing production checks; they need no labelled data and no new infrastructure. Record and publish the date at which any ML component was introduced into a series, so that the resulting break is not read as economic change.

**Medium term.** Move from point estimates to distributions: publish, alongside the balanced table, either a set of Monte Carlo replicates or the ingredients (prior means, prior variances, constraint set) from which users can generate them, following the Bayesian-balancing route now demonstrated at scale [schulte26-bayes; rodrigues14]. Implement conformal or otherwise calibrated intervals on ML-produced cells with explicit conditional coverage by region and sector class, not marginal coverage only. Extend the Eurostat AIML4OS work packages on editing, imputation, LLMs and company-level supply-chain mapping into explicit supply-use table deliverables [aiml4os], and make the resulting methods and evaluations public rather than internal. Use active learning to target survey and verification budget at the cells where variance reduction in published indicators is largest.

**Long term.** Establish, jointly across offices, a governance regime for model-derived official figures covering versioning, revision policy, reproducibility of the model itself, and the conditions under which a model-derived figure may carry an official label — the six-dimension quality frameworks already drafted are the starting point, not the endpoint [dumpert23]. Retain in-house capability to produce and check the estimates the models produce; the deskilling risk (§16) is realised by attrition, not by decision. Resolve the confidentiality-versus-resolution question deliberately — through secure enclaves, synthetic microdata with published fidelity metrics, or differentially private aggregates — rather than letting it be settled by whichever commercial provider assembles firm-level data first.


## 22.2 MRIO database compilers and consortia

**Short term.** Answer the low-rank question: run the spectral analysis on the open MRIO panel and publish the result whichever way it comes out, because it determines whether an entire family of imputation methods is applicable. Publish machine-readable provenance and construction metadata per cell for at least one release. Adopt the residual-correction architecture as the default form for any ML component — classical estimator as backbone, learned bounded correction on top — rather than end-to-end learned tables [zhao22-dl].

**Medium term.** Build and release the field's missing benchmarks. Three are needed and none exists: a held-out national supply-use table never used in construction, for scoring estimation methods; a labelled spend-to-sector set on *real* rather than synthetic ledger text, which requires a confidentiality solution and is the reason it does not exist; and a gap-filling benchmark with realistic missingness rates and *reported split variance*, given that random splits alone produce 25.8% performance swings [zhao25]. Prioritise harmonisation effort on **satellite accounts over economic structures**, since the divergence literature locates the dominant discrepancy in the emissions vector [moran-wood14; owen16]. Extend the constraint-embedded generative approach from single-region to multi-regional tables with heterogeneous cell reliabilities — the clearest single research target identified in this report [depretis26].

**Long term.** Converge on a shared schema that represents IO/EEIO objects natively alongside process-LCA objects; the absence of any IO representation in the dominant LCA exchange format is a fixable blocker on hybridisation [olca-schema; ghose22]. Establish cross-database validation that does not rely on shared upstream sources, and state publicly that inter-database spread is a lower bound on uncertainty rather than an estimate of it. Move release cadence toward near-real-time with explicitly flagged nowcast vintages, and publish the nowcast error record so that users can price the timeliness–accuracy trade themselves.


## 22.3 Academic input–output researchers

**Short term.** Stop reporting sectoral footprints to three significant figures; the measured sector-level coefficients of variation do not support the second [schulte24]. Apply the detectability test to your own proposals: if the claimed improvement is smaller than the noise floor of the quantity it feeds, say so. Report split variance and out-of-sample transfer for every accuracy claim, and treat a single random split as uninformative. Publish code and data version identifiers with every study — in the nearest neighbouring field only 2% do, and undocumented version drift alone moves results by 21% [kamali26].

**Medium term.** Execute the small number of high-value, immediately feasible studies this map identifies: automatic differentiation through an MRIO pipeline, delivering exact sensitivities of any footprint to all coefficients at the cost of one solve; conformal prediction on IO cells with conditional coverage; low-rank completion of the country–sector–year panel if the spectral test supports it; extension of input-criticality matrices by machine learning, validated against the Belgian benchmark [alleman23]; scenario discovery on an MRIO ensemble, which we continue to rate the most concrete near-term programme in the report. Use impact-weighted rather than cell-weighted losses when training anything that feeds a footprint. Design studies so that the imputation model and the validation model do not share a training corpus.

**Long term.** Build the Tier 3 case properly or abandon it: identify the specific empirical regimes where the linear model demonstrably fails — nonlinear cascade, within-sector heterogeneity, adaptive rationing — and test constrained learned alternatives *only there*, against classical baselines, with the accounting identities enforced structurally. Engage the graph-learning community directly; the Leontief–GNN correspondence is exact, the two literatures do not cite each other, and the cost of that separation is borne mostly by IO. Treat validation methodology as a first-class research output rather than a section of other papers.


## 22.4 Machine-learning and AI researchers

**Short term.** Recognise that input–output tables are an unusually good testbed for constrained learning: the constraints are exact, known, cheap to evaluate and economically meaningful, and the field has real problems and open data. Read the accounting before proposing an architecture; most of the failure modes catalogued here are invisible to standard ML evaluation because they are conservation failures rather than accuracy failures.

**Medium term.** Contribute the two capabilities the IO field cannot build for itself: differentiable, GPU-resident sparse solvers with exact projection layers for structured linear constraints at MRIO scale, and calibrated uncertainty methods that survive the small-N, non-i.i.d., distribution-shifting regime IO actually inhabits. Take the transfer problem seriously — cross-dataset degradation is the field's most robust negative finding [mungo23], and a method that does not transfer between countries is not deployable in a global database.

**Long term.** Treat the twenty-five-observation regime as a research problem rather than a disqualification. Almost all of modern ML assumes a data situation that macroeconomic accounts will never have; the methods that work under exact structural constraints and severe data poverty are a genuine research frontier, and IO is a well-posed instance of it.


## 22.5 Industrial-ecology and LCA community

**Short term.** Stop describing embedding-based proxy matching as solved; textual similarity is not environmental similarity, and retrieval-based matching systematically compresses cross-product variance — the opposite of what product-level differentiation is for. Report the deployed-but-unevaluated tools as such: at least one widely used LCA↔IO mapping module has no published accuracy evaluation [ciraig-mapping].

**Medium term.** Attack the double-counting correction in hybridisation with learning rather than heuristics, and evaluate it honestly — an ML-estimated correction biased low inflates footprints and biased high deflates them, and neither is visible in the output [pylcaio; agez20]. Bring physics-informed constrained learning to physical input–output tables and monetary–physical reconciliation, which is the textbook-correct application of the paradigm and has attracted essentially no attention. Build the paired process-versus-IO divergence dataset into a supervised diagnostic that *explains* divergence rather than one that corrects one database toward the other.

**Long term.** Resolve the monetary–physical boundary as a data problem rather than a convention problem: it underlies price-heterogeneity bias, the mishandling of recycling and negative-value flows, and the whole circular-economy modelling difficulty, and no amount of learning on monetary tables addresses it.


## 22.6 Regulators, standard-setters and policy bodies

**Short term.** Require disclosure of method for any AI-derived figure entering mandated reporting: the model class, the classification accuracy on a stated benchmark, and whether the figure is measured, estimated or imputed. The gap between 57.3% top-1 classification accuracy on the only public benchmark [atlas] and the confidence with which such numbers enter regulated disclosure is currently unbridged and undisclosed. State explicitly whether AI-derived estimates satisfy the primary-data provisions of the standards you set; at present practitioners are guessing.

**Medium term.** Fund and mandate the benchmarks the field cannot fund itself — particularly a real-ledger spend classification benchmark, which requires a regulator or a statistical office to solve the confidentiality problem that keeps it from existing. Write assurance standards that can accommodate non-deterministic components: an auditor cannot currently sign off on a figure that changes between runs, and no framework says what to do about it. Anticipate adversarial gaming — mandated reporting creates a direct financial incentive to be classified into a low-intensity sector, and no accuracy figure in this literature is measured under adversarial conditions. Align digital reporting taxonomies (XBRL-tagged sustainability data) so that disclosure output is machine-readable input to the accounts, closing the loop between corporate reporting and official statistics.

**Long term.** Decide, deliberately and publicly, where in the regulatory chain model-derived estimates are acceptable and where measurement is required — border adjustment, deforestation due diligence and product passports each currently rest on an unstated answer. Recognise the dual-use dimension of fine-grained supply-chain estimation: facility-level asset maps and chokepoint analyses are commercial and strategic intelligence as much as compliance tools, and the disclosure regime should be designed knowing that. Support the international comparability of ML-assisted national statistics; divergent national adoption will otherwise create incomparability that MRIO bakes into international footprints, in a form that is very hard to detect after the fact.


## 22.7 Companies, consultancies and assurance providers

**Short term.** Fix the price-basis error before improving the classifier: applying basic-price EEIO intensities to purchaser-price spend overstates footprints by the trade-and-transport margin plus taxes, plausibly 20–60% in retail-heavy categories, and it is invisible to every accuracy metric your vendor quotes. Ask vendors for accuracy on a stated public benchmark with a stated label space; "85% F1" on private data with an unspecified number of classes is not a comparable number. Stop presenting sector-average social and environmental risk screening as supplier-specific findings — the defamation and commercial-harm exposure is real, and "we screened 100% of our supply chain" on sector averages is compliance theatre.

**Medium term.** Route AI at the parts of the workflow where it is strong and constrain it elsewhere: translating a question into a specification, extracting data from documents with citation back to the source span, and drafting narrative whose every numeral is checked against the model run. Keep it away from factor recall and arithmetic. Build the human verification sampling regime alongside the automation, and publish the sampled error rate; without it, the automation's error rate is unknown by construction.

**Long term.** Move from spend-based to supplier-specific data where the abatement decisions actually sit, and treat EEIO as the screening layer it is good at being rather than the measurement layer it is not. Recognise that assurance providers using the same model families as preparers share their blind spots — correlated failure is the deep problem, and it is validation circularity wearing professional clothing.


## 22.8 Funders, journals and infrastructure providers

**Short term.** Require code and data-version deposition as a condition of publication in this area; the 2%-code-sharing base rate is the substrate on which everything else here is built [kamali26]. Require that evidence status be stated and that "proven" claims resolve to a primary source — the recurrence of mislabelled evidence across independent AI-assisted reviews (§21.3) makes this a practical editorial control, not a formality.

**Medium term.** Fund the unglamorous middle: benchmarks, concordances, provenance infrastructure, validation methodology and open licensing. None of it produces headline results and all of it is prerequisite. Fund at least one deliberately negative-result programme — a serious attempt to establish where ML does *not* beat classical methods on IO objects, which the field currently has no incentive to produce and badly needs.

**Long term.** Address the licensing asymmetry directly. The field's only open ML benchmark exists only because one database is openly licensed, which means cross-database generalisation — the property that decides whether any of this transfers — is structurally untestable. That is a funding and licensing decision, not a scientific one, and it currently caps what the field can know about its own methods.
